Chapter 1

Who He Is and What He Claims

In this chapter: what Adam Khoo says about his own record, his teaching philosophy, and his idea of financial freedom — reported strictly as his claims, with the self-reported figures flagged as such, so the reader knows from page one how this book handles a decade of unverifiable numbers.

1.1 A biography built from his own retelling

Nearly everything in this chapter comes from Khoo describing himself, on camera, across roughly a decade of videos. There is no independent audit, financial filing, or third-party record behind most of it — only his account of his own history, repeated with enough consistency that the repetition itself is worth noting, even though repetition is not verification.

He dates his own timeline the same way across multiple separate tellings: he says he started trading at eighteen and reached his first million by twenty-six, a span he himself points out is eight years, and elsewhere describes as nine to ten years — a minor inconsistency in his own retelling that this book preserves rather than smooths over. He says the money he made from trading is what got him ranked among Singapore's top twenty-five richest people under forty, with a net worth at the time he gives as over roughly nine million dollars, a figure the ledger flags weak since it rests on his self-report alone. In a later retelling he adds that this milestone came around age thirty-one, and that "the second and third millions came much faster" than the first.

Editor's note — why self-reported trading returns are unverifiable in principle

None of the figures in this section, or in the sections on his trading returns below, can be checked against an outside source. Personal trading accounts are not subject to audit, disclosure, or regulatory reporting the way a licensed fund is. A trader can show a screenshot of a brokerage balance, but a screenshot proves nothing about the history behind it — what was deposited and when, what was withdrawn, whether the account shown is the only account, or whether a losing period was simply not filmed. This is not a claim that Khoo is misrepresenting anything; it is a structural fact about self-reported trading results in general, true of every trader who narrates results on YouTube without third-party verification. The discipline this book follows — stating every figure as "he says" or "he claims," never as an established fact — exists because there is no way, from outside his brokerage, to do anything else responsibly.

He frames his own success as a product of learned skill rather than circumstance: he says he had no more talent, time, or education than his audience, and that what separated him was learning to direct effort and resources through leverage — other people's time, other people's experience, and other people's money. In a separate video he credits an "empowering mindset" and optimism, rather than intelligence, hard work, or talent, as the real differentiator between himself and peers who were by his account smarter or worked harder. He also discloses the more conventional side of his preparation: three years of business school with a major in finance, and, he says, over six hundred books on markets read since he was seventeen.

1.2 What he says about his own trading and investing returns

This is the section where the self-report discipline matters most, because the numbers are frequent, specific, and repeated across formats — webinars, trade reviews, and course promotions alike.

He gives a recurring monthly range for his trading returns: he says a conservative, achievable average is around 5 percent a month, while elsewhere he says 5 to 20 percent a month is "very possible and pretty common" among successful forex traders, and in another video puts his annual target at 50 to 60 percent. He is explicit, more than once, that these figures are not linear or guaranteed: showing what he describes as one of his own accounts, he says the average is about 5 percent a month but adds that it is "not always 5 percent because the market is not always the same," citing a month of negative 1 percent followed by a 20 percent month. He acknowledges his own month-to-month data contains losing months and strings of consecutive losses, and elsewhere states plainly that no matter how good a trader or investor is, "you cannot make money every day, week, month and year" — including himself.

On win rate, he gives a consistent range across several videos: 60 to 70 percent if a viewer follows his strategies, with a corresponding 30 to 40 percent chance of loss, and separately states that based on his own and his students' trading records, using his indicators and candlestick patterns, they are right 60 to 70 percent of the time. Elsewhere he narrows this to 50 to 60 percent, and in a different video states his win rate is "usually 50 to 60 percent" and that when it drops to about 40 percent, "you do not get the profits you want" — a real range, not a single number, and the book reports it as a range.

On his investing record specifically, he makes his most unqualified claim in a 2021 video: he claims that in the last twenty years he has never had a losing investment, and in the same video says his target is to beat the market every year with at least a 20 to 30 percent return, claiming he has done so every year. Elsewhere, however, he discloses a specific counter-example to any impression of a smooth record: he says his portfolio fell from about $990,000 to about $680,000 during the February–March 2020 crash, a drawdown of roughly 25 percent, and separately recounts the September 2018 Tencent episode where a gaming-approval ban cut the stock from about 400 to 260 — a roughly 47 percent drop — while he held through it. Where he has run a public track record with a stated methodology, he is careful to flag it as such: he states plainly that the 2007–2018 performance figures shown in one video are back-tested, not live results, and separately notes that "past performance is not a guarantee of future results".

He also volunteers, unprompted, a claim that runs directly counter to the professional trader he otherwise presents: he says that reading trends is how he made money "consistently every single year for the last 15 years," and adds that most people — including professional fund managers and bankers — have no idea how to read trends — a claim he offers without supporting figures.

Editor's note — reading a stated range honestly

When a figure is given as a range across several videos rather than a single constant, the honest way to report it is as a range with its variation intact, not as an average smoothed into one number. A 5 percent claim in one video and a 20 percent claim in another are not the same claim quietly split down the middle — they are two different statements made at two different times, and a reader deciding whether to trust a "typical month" figure needs to see that spread, not a number invented by averaging it away.

1.3 The teaching philosophy: psychology first

Across the corpus, Khoo states a specific breakdown of what makes a trader successful, and the ratio recurs with striking consistency: psychology accounts for 60 percent of trading success, position sizing and risk management for 30 percent, and the trading strategy itself for only 10 percent. In one framing he restates the same three-part split from the other direction — system plus position sizing making up 40 percent, with the remaining 60 percent being psychology — the same shape, described two ways. He says this is why people who read books and attend seminars to learn technical analysis still lose money: they never realize strategy is only ten percent of profitability. In his own paid programs, he says, he does not just teach strategies but focuses heavily on risk management, position sizing, and — most importantly — the 60 percent that is psychology.

He is explicit that skill in this domain is learned rather than innate, drawing a comparison to other professions: he says being a professional trader or investor is no different from being a professional doctor, banker, or teacher — it requires skills, knowledge, hard work, practice, and experience. Elsewhere he extends the same comparison, saying that like being a doctor or lawyer, "there's no free lunch" in trading — it requires real study, education, and practice, even after taking a course. He credits his own method to a combination of investing in his financial education, modeling investors including Peter Lynch, Charlie Munger, and Warren Buffett, and trial and error.

He is also consistent about what his teaching does not promise. He discloses that he is not a licensed financial advisor, describing himself instead as a personal investor and trader sharing insight for educational purposes, a disclaimer he repeats in a later video almost verbatim — content for education and entertainment, showing only how he manages his own money, with the instruction that viewers must make their own decisions. He states that taking his courses does not produce money "miraculously" — the student still has to put in the hours, the way a surgeon goes through medical school. When walking through his own claimed results, the video in question closes by directing viewers to his paid Wealth Academy program — a detail worth noting because it means the fullest account of the method behind any given result is kept behind a paywall the transcripts themselves do not cross.

1.4 Financial freedom, as he defines it

Khoo defines financial freedom consistently across many videos as the point where passive income exceeds expenses, and gives a parallel definition specific to his own three income streams — trading, investments, and rentals — where the same passive-income-exceeds-expenses condition applies. He defines passive income itself as income earned without actively selling your time, generated by cash-flow assets such as bonds, stocks, real estate, and fixed deposits.

He lays out a three-step process. Step one is to track monthly expenses and multiply them into an annual figure — the passive income target. Step two is to divide that annual figure by the expected yield of the asset class to be held, producing the capital required. Working an example of this arithmetic, he divides $60,000 of required annual income by a 60 percent annual return to arrive at $100,000 of required capital — or $200,000 if monthly spending is $10,000. In a more recent video he states a version of this same arithmetic as a general-purpose rule of thumb: multiply annual expenses by 25 to estimate the net worth required for financial freedom — for example, $100,000 a year in expenses implies $2.5 million needed. The two versions are not identical (one runs off a 60 percent annual trading return, the other off a 4 percent implied withdrawal rate typical of index-investing rules of thumb), and the book leaves that difference visible rather than reconciling it for him.

He grounds the underlying case for investing at all in a separate argument: not investing is riskier than investing, he says, because cash left in a bank loses to inflation and can be worth roughly a third of its value after twenty years. Working a passive, non-trading example of the same freedom arithmetic, he shows that saving 10 percent of a $60,000 salary — $6,000 a year — compounding in an index ETF at 12 percent for thirty years, becomes about $1.6 million.

He frames the case for building passive income as a rejection of salaried security: a salary is not security, he argues, because an employee does not own their own income source — an employer owns it and can replace them with someone younger, cheaper, and faster. He adds that lifelong employment is over even at large multinationals and government agencies, and that the longer someone stays at a company, the more expensive they become to retain, raising their own risk of being let go. He frames the response to this as a matter of responsibility rather than blame: there is no point blaming the government, the economy, or an employer, he says, because doing so hands them control over your life — taking responsibility is what lets a person act. He is careful, however, to bound the timeline: he says his course will not make anyone rich overnight, and that following the system while saving diligently should take three, five, or six years to reach financial freedom.

1.5 The wealth mindset material

A recurring set of claims frames wealth-building as a matter of mindset and risk tolerance rather than luck. He states as a general rule of life that the success a person enjoys is directly proportional to the level of risk or uncertainty they are willing to take on, and argues that most people never become rich because they associate too much fear with risk, believing outcomes are down to luck rather than something manageable. Successful people, he says, take large calculated risks not because they lack fear but because they believe outcomes can be controlled through risk management. In the same vein, he argues that after every market crash, some people end up poorer and some richer, and the difference is not luck but the skills a person has when the crash arrives.

He is candid that his own relationship to achievement changed over time. He says he used to believe he needed to achieve things — the first million, the house, the car, marriage — to be happy, and found that each achievement bought only a day of happiness before the striving resumed. His current philosophy, he says, is to "happily achieve" rather than "achieve to be happy" — finding satisfaction in the daily work and problems themselves rather than in a finish line. He extends the same argument to his own family in a 2022 video, saying that his children learning to invest and become financially free matters more to him than their grades or university outcomes.

He also argues that circumstances do not determine outcomes: most people, he says, cite external factors — no money, no connections, the wrong economy, the wrong boss — as what holds them back, when the real obstacle lies elsewhere, a point he supports by citing examples of privileged children who struggled and disadvantaged children who succeeded. He states that wealth cannot be built overnight — it takes time for money to compound and time to master a skill, and that the first lesson of wealth-building is not how much a person saves but the return they earn on their capital.

Editor's note — mindset claims and the evidence they rest on

The claims in this section — that risk tolerance predicts success, that mindset separates the wealthy from everyone else, that circumstances don't determine outcomes — are opinions Khoo states with conviction, illustrated with anecdotes rather than data. They are common themes in the broader personal- finance and motivational-speaking genre he has also worked in (he has an NLP and personal-development teaching background alongside his trading and investing content), and none of them are the kind of claim that can be tested against a market record the way a stated win rate can be. The book reports them as his stated philosophy because that is what they are — not because they have been independently shown to predict who gets rich.

What he doesn't say about this

Khoo does not disclose, in any card in this archive, the size or composition of his overall net worth as a single current figure, a full account statement, or brokerage records that would let a reader verify any of the return figures above. He does not address how the trading, investing, and real-estate income streams he describes are weighted relative to income from his courses and businesses themselves — a question that matters because the courses are the product being sold in videos that also cite his trading success as the reason to buy them. He also does not reconcile, in any single video, the different freedom-arithmetic formulas given in section 1.4 (a 60 percent annual trading return versus a 25x-expenses / roughly 4 percent rule), leaving it to the viewer to notice that they imply very different levels of achievable return.

Chapter summary

  • Khoo dates his own trajectory from age eighteen to his first million by twenty-six, describing this span as eight years in one telling and nine to ten in another.
  • He states a specific, repeated breakdown of trading success: 60 percent psychology, 30 percent position sizing and risk management, 10 percent strategy.
  • His stated trading returns vary by video from a "conservative" 5 percent a month to a claimed 5–20 percent a month, and his stated win rates vary from 50–60 percent to 60–70 percent depending on the telling — reported here as ranges, not as one number.
  • He claims a 20-year run without a losing investment and a yearly target of beating the market by 20–30 percent, while elsewhere disclosing a roughly 25 percent portfolio drawdown in the 2020 crash — the book presents both rather than only the unqualified claim.
  • He defines financial freedom as passive income exceeding expenses and gives a three-step process — track expenses, divide by target yield, acquire the needed capital — with two differently-scaled versions of the underlying arithmetic across videos.
  • He frames his teaching as requiring real study and practice, disclaims being a licensed financial advisor, and directs viewers who want the full method to his paid courses.
  • His wealth-mindset material argues that risk tolerance and mindset, not luck or circumstance, separate wealthy people from everyone else — presented as his stated philosophy, not as demonstrated fact.
  • Every return figure, win rate, and net-worth claim in this chapter is self-reported and unverifiable in principle; the book's discipline is to say "he claims" or "he says," never to restate these as established fact.
Chapter 2

The Two Books: Investing vs. Trading

In this chapter: the single distinction that governs every rule later in this book — why the same person, looking at the same falling stock, will refuse to exit one account and mandate an exit from the other, and why that isn't a contradiction so much as the architecture of his entire method.

2.1 One person, two accounts

Adam Khoo does not run one portfolio with one rule set. He runs two, side by side, and treats the difference between them as more important than any single setup or indicator taught elsewhere on the channel. In his own account of it, he holds good companies through a bear market in one portfolio while simultaneously profiting from the same decline in a second, separate portfolio — using put options, inverse ETFs, and short positions against index ETFs. The two sit next to each other, run by the same person, in the same week, pointed in opposite directions.

He names the split in the most direct way he has: investing is a marriage, trading is a one-night stand. When you invest, he says, you are committing to a company the way you commit to a spouse — you evaluate character (the business quality), education (the fundamentals), and heart (the long-term story), and once committed you stay loyal through the other party's mood swings. Short-term trading, by contrast, is a one-night stand: no loyalty, in and out fast, and because there is no relationship to fall back on, you "use protection" — his term for a stop loss. He repeats the analogy across at least a dozen separate videos spanning multiple years, which is itself evidence of how central it is to how he teaches the method — this is not a one-off metaphor he reached for once, it's the frame he returns to whenever the topic comes up at all.

The metaphor maps onto real, mechanical differences, not just a difference in mood. Every later chapter in this book — on stop-loss placement, on position sizing, on what counts as a good setup — is really a chapter about one of the two books, and assumes you already know which book is being discussed. This chapter exists to make that assumption explicit before the method gets any more detailed than it already has.

Editor's note — why "book" is the right word for this

In professional trading and portfolio management, a "book" refers to a collection of positions managed under one mandate — one risk budget, one set of rules, one performance measurement. A market-maker's "trading book" and an insurer's "investment book" are literally separate ledgers with separate capital and separate risk limits, even when run by the same firm. Khoo's split between his investing account and his trading account is the retail-scale version of the same idea: two books, two mandates, and — as this chapter documents — two completely different rule sets governing entries, exits, and how losses are handled.

2.2 The rule that defines the split: stop-loss, or no stop-loss

If one rule anchors the entire two-book architecture, it's this one, and he states it with almost no variation across a decade of videos: in the investing book there is no stop loss; in the trading book a stop loss is mandatory.

He states the investing side flatly — "for investments you do not have a stop loss" — because you believe in the company you bought. He gives the same rule again describing his own Alibaba position: "as an investment I don't have a stop loss," because he judges it a great company that will go higher eventually. He calls it obvious: an investment portfolio has no stop loss "because it is a good business," and you do not sell a good business just because its price has fallen. And he generalizes it as a two-sided rule in the same breath more than once: trading requires a stop loss, investing does not.

The trading side of the rule is stated with equal flatness. Every trade must be entered with a stop loss, with the trader prepared to actually take the loss if it comes. Once a stop loss is hit, he says the correct move is simply to exit and wait for the next signal — not to hold, and not to hope. Overvalued companies he would never invest in are exactly the companies he'll still trade short-term, and the difference is that the trade comes with a stop loss or is expressed through options rather than a naked long position. He puts it as sharply as anywhere in the corpus discussing ARK-style glamour stocks: he does buy them — but only in the trading portfolio, where a stop loss and options are present, never as an outright long-term holding.

Editor's note — why "no stop loss" is not the same as "no risk control"

Removing a price-based stop loss doesn't mean removing risk management — it means substituting a different control. In Khoo's investing book, the control moves upstream: to the fundamental screen a company has to pass before it's eligible to be bought at all (covered in Chapter 4), and to position sizing at entry (Chapter 6), rather than to a price level that triggers an exit. This is a real and legitimate approach used by long-horizon value investors generally — Warren Buffett has said publicly that he doesn't use stop losses on Berkshire's core equity holdings for a similar reason: a genuine business owner doesn't sell a stake in a good company because of a short-term price move. The risk control simply lives somewhere else in the process.

The stop-loss rule is the one Khoo states most often and most absolutely, but he does not present it as universal to all market participants — only as the rule that separates his two accounts from each other. Read against his own trading-book material (Chapter 10 covers this in full), the picture is symmetrical rather than contradictory: trading has a stop loss because a trading position hasn't earned the confidence that no stop loss requires.

2.3 Averaging down vs. cutting losses: the mirror-image response to a falling price

The stop-loss rule has a mirror-image twin, and it is just as load-bearing: what happens when the price of a position falls. In the investing book, a fall is an invitation to buy more. In the trading book, it is the signal to exit.

He states the investing response repeatedly. His long-run, fundamentals-based investment portfolio has no stop loss, and when the price falls further he averages down and buys more — a rule he ties explicitly to the fact that this portfolio is a separate account from his trading portfolio, run under separate rules. He gives the same sequence describing Facebook after its 2018 crash: as an investor, hold with no stop loss, and if it's a good company, hold through and buy more at the lower price to average down. Summarizing the whole split in one pass, he puts the investing side and the trading side directly against each other: in investing, the more the price drops, the more you buy and average down; in trading, you cut the loss fast the moment the setup stops working and move on to another stock.

The trading response is the opposite motion entirely. A short-term trader looking at the same falling chart would take a position with a stop loss placed below the prior swing low, or use options instead of a naked position — not add on weakness. Never move a stop loss to give a losing trade more room, he warns elsewhere — traders who keep giving a losing position "more room" eventually take a much bigger loss than the one they were trying to avoid. The same falling price, in the same market, produces the opposite instruction depending on which book the position lives in.

He is explicit that averaging down is not a free action available to anyone — it's conditional on having already screened the position as a good business before the price ever fell. He treats the averaging-down rule as safe only because the investing side of his method is restricted, in the first place, to companies that clear his quality and valuation screen (Chapter 4 and 5 cover that screen directly). Buy-and-hold investors following the no-stop-loss rule, he adds, should never use margin or borrowed money to do it — a leveraged account without a stop loss is how you turn a paper drawdown into a real bankruptcy. The absence of a stop loss is not an absence of caution; it's caution relocated to before the trade, rather than after it.

Editor's note — a real-world case where this exact split played out

The 2000–2002 dot-com crash is the clean historical illustration of why "average down" and "cut losses fast" have to be different rules for different kinds of positions. An investor holding Amazon through that crash — down more than 90% from its 1999 peak — who kept buying more because the underlying retail business kept growing revenue was rewarded over the following two decades. A trader holding a leveraged short-term position in Pets.com or Webvan with no exit discipline had no such story available, because there was no underlying business left to wait for. The rule only works when the thing being averaged into is real and durable — which is exactly the condition Khoo attaches to it.

2.4 The honest override: when he breaks his own no-stop-loss rule on camera

The corpus contains at least one case where Khoo states the no-stop-loss investing rule and then, discussing a specific holding, describes himself overriding it — and he gives his own reasoning for the override rather than treating it as an exception to be hidden. Discussing his dollar-cost-averaging ETF portfolio, he says that even here — a long-term, no-stop-loss account by his own general rule — if the entire underlying trend reverses down, he will simply exit, let the position "collapse 50 percent," and buy back in at what he judges to be the bottom. That statement sits, by his own framing elsewhere, in direct tension with the flat "no stop loss on investments" rule stated in Section 2.2.

This isn't presented in the source material as a contradiction he's caught in — it's presented as his own judgment call about a specific instrument (a trend-following ETF position rather than an individual quality-screened stock) under a specific condition (a full trend reversal, not an ordinary drawdown). The honest reading is not to resolve the tension by picking one rule as "the real one," but to preserve both statements as he made them: the no-stop-loss rule is his stated default for individual quality holdings, and he has, in his own words, carved out room to exit a broader trend-following position when the entire trend itself breaks. Chapter 7 returns to this exact tension when it covers exit behavior for investments in full, including his own disclosed cases of holding too long and of selling great companies far too early.

2.5 Two different win-rate expectations

The two-book split shows up again in the win rates Khoo attaches to each side — and here too the numbers are stated as ranges across different videos, not as one fixed figure, which is worth preserving rather than averaging away.

For investing, he states the win rate should sit around 90 percent if you pick the right companies and hold for the long term, and elsewhere gives 80 to 90 percent, tied specifically to a 5-to-10-year holding period rather than to picking a good business alone. In several videos he goes further and states the investing win rate at 100 percent, attributing it not to any skill of his own but to the fact that he restricts the investing book to very great, very safe, already-cheap companies and holds them long enough. He is explicit that the near-100 percent figure depends entirely on time horizon: buying a stock like McDonald's today gives an almost 100 percent chance of it being higher a few years out, but only a 40 to 60 percent chance of it being higher tomorrow or next week — which is the qualifier that keeps the very-high investing win rate from being read as "the market always goes up regardless of timeframe."

For trading, the stated win rate is dramatically lower — 50 to 60 percent, sometimes given as roughly 50 to 55 winners out of 100 trades. He is explicit that the lower win rate isn't a flaw in the trading book; it's paired with a higher payoff ratio (winning trades targeting roughly 2R against a 1R loss) and higher frequency, which is how the arithmetic still produces a positive return despite losing on nearly half of all trades — the worked math behind that arithmetic is covered fully in Chapter 14. The point for this chapter is narrower: he does not expect the same win rate from the two books, states that explicitly, and treats a near-100 percent expectation on the trading side as unrealistic in a way it is not on the investing side.

Editor's note — near-100 percent win rates and survivorship

A near-100 percent realized win rate on a stock portfolio, evaluated only after the fact, is a claim that deserves a specific caution independent of anything Khoo says about it: it can look accurate if a losing position that never recovered is quietly excluded from the count, or if the count only covers a period during which markets broadly rose. Reported win rates of this kind are flagged elsewhere in this book (Chapter 1) as self-reported and structurally hard to verify from outside; the qualifier he gives — that it depends on genuinely picking great companies and holding them long enough — is doing a lot of the work, and a reader applying this rule should treat the screening step (Chapter 4) as the part that actually has to hold up, not the win-rate figure itself.

2.6 The two books work together, not in isolation

The split is not simply two unrelated activities happening to be run by the same person — Khoo describes an explicit relationship between them. Cash flow generated on the trading side, he says, is what he then moves into the investing side, to be compounded in good businesses held for the long run. The trading book, in other words, is described in his own account as a funding mechanism for the investing book, not a separate hobby. He also describes running both simultaneously as a hedge against each other during a downturn: holding good companies through a bear market in the investing book, while using short positions, inverse ETFs, and put options in the trading book to profit from the same decline.

Options occupy an interesting position in this architecture: he treats them as belonging to the trading book, alongside a stop loss, even when the underlying instrument is one he might also hold as a long-term investment. Elsewhere he frames the investor/trader distinction as one that applies across both stocks and options — investors "marry" a position and hold long term, traders have "one-night stands," and options can serve either role depending on which side of the split the specific position is being run under. Chapter 11 covers the options side of the trading book directly; the point to carry forward from this chapter is that "which book is this position in" is a question that has to be answered before any stop-loss, sizing, or win-rate expectation from later chapters can be correctly applied to it.

2.7 What actually decides which book a stock goes into

Given that the same underlying company can sit in either book, the natural question is what decides the assignment. Khoo's answer, stated across several videos, is that the decision is made by the fundamentals-and-valuation screen, not by the chart, the ticker, or how exciting the stock currently is.

He is explicit that hyped, high-momentum "bubble stocks" should never be bought as investments — they belong only in the trading portfolio, run with protection and an immediate exit the moment the setup breaks. The reasoning he gives for always requiring a stop loss on trading positions is that a short-term trade may sit in a company that is neither fundamentally good nor undervalued — you're entering purely on the technicals, so you need the protection a fundamentally sound position wouldn't require. Read together with the win-rate material in Section 2.5, this is the same idea from the other direction: a position earns the no-stop-loss investing treatment only by first clearing a quality-and-valuation bar (the actual bar is the subject of Chapters 4 and 5); a position that hasn't cleared that bar, however attractive the chart looks, stays in the trading book by default and carries a stop loss because nothing else is protecting it.

This means the marriage/one-night-stand analogy from Section 2.1 isn't just color — it's a compressed statement of the actual gate. "Character, education and heart" is his shorthand for the fundamental criteria a company has to show before he'll commit to it without an exit plan; a stock that shows none of that but is moving fast can still be traded, just never invested in, and he states this as a "both/and" rather than an "either/or" — the same underlying business, evaluated on different terms, can legitimately sit in both books of the same person's portfolio at once. Elsewhere he states the point directly, without the analogy attached: he uses the marriage/one-night-stand framing specifically to argue that "the selection criteria differ" between the two books, not merely the exit rules — the split isn't only about what you do after you're in a position, it's about what qualifies a position to be entered as one type versus the other in the first place.

Editor's note — the marriage analogy as a compressed decision rule

It's worth flagging why a relationship analogy, repeated as often as this one is across Khoo's videos, is doing real teaching work rather than just being memorable. A marriage, in the analogy, presupposes vetting — you don't marry a stranger you met an hour ago, the same way you don't put a company in the no-stop-loss book without having checked its fundamentals first. A one-night stand presupposes the opposite: minimal vetting, short duration, and protection as the only safeguard. The analogy survives repetition across a decade of videos because it maps cleanly onto an actual, checkable procedure rather than being decorative — which is one reason a reader can treat it as more than a joke.

What he doesn't say about this

He does not, anywhere in the archive searched for this chapter, give a rule for what percentage of total capital should sit in the investing book versus the trading book — the split is described qualitatively (two separate accounts, two separate mandates) but not with a stated allocation ratio between them. He also does not specify a bright-line test for when a position that starts in the trading book should be reclassified into the investing book, or vice versa — the Facebook and Alibaba examples in this chapter show him choosing "invest" for a specific falling stock, but he doesn't state the general decision rule that produced that choice beyond "it's a good company." Finally, while he discusses the ETF-portfolio override in Section 2.4 as his own judgment call, he does not revisit it elsewhere in the corpus searched here to say whether he considers it a one-time exception or a standing amendment to the no-stop-loss rule for trend-following positions specifically — the two statements coexist in the record without him reconciling them explicitly.

Chapter summary

  • Khoo runs two separate portfolios under separate mandates at the same time: an investing book and a trading book, sometimes profiting from the same market move in opposite directions simultaneously.
  • He frames the split with a marriage/one-night-stand analogy repeated across more than a decade of videos: investing is a long-term commitment you hold through mood swings, trading is short, protected, and disposable.
  • The core mechanical rule: no stop loss in the investing book, mandatory stop loss in the trading book — stated flatly and repeatedly with no stated exception for the trading side.
  • The mirror-image rule for a falling price: average down and buy more in the investing book; cut the loss fast and move on in the trading book — conditional on the investing side being restricted to quality-screened businesses in the first place.
  • He documents his own override of the no-stop-loss rule for a trend-following ETF position, exiting and planning to buy back at the bottom — presented as his own reasoning about a specific instrument, not as a general exception.
  • The two books carry different win-rate expectations: roughly 80–100 percent for investing (conditional on long holding periods and company quality), versus 50–60 percent for trading (offset by a higher payoff ratio).
  • The books are not run in isolation — cash flow from trading funds the investing book, and the two are sometimes deliberately run as offsetting positions against the same market move.
  • Never use margin on the no-stop-loss investing side — a rule stated as absolute given that a leveraged position with no exit mechanism can produce real bankruptcy rather than a recoverable drawdown.
  • Which book a stock belongs in is decided by the fundamentals-and-valuation screen, not by the ticker or the chart — a hyped or bubble stock can be traded but should never be invested in, and the same company can legitimately sit in both books at once under different terms.

Try this

Before reading further in this book, take one position you currently hold or are considering, and write down, in one sentence, which book it belongs in and why — using his own test: would you be comfortable holding it through a 50 percent decline while buying more, the way he describes doing with a quality investment, or does it need a stop loss and a defined exit before you'd be willing to enter it at all, the way he describes every trading position ? If you can't answer that question for a position, his own material suggests you don't yet know which set of rules — the ones in Part II or the ones in Part III of this book — actually apply to it.

Chapter 3

What He Thinks Markets Are

In this chapter: the cyclical model of markets that sits underneath every strategy taught later in this book, why he considers prediction impossible in principle while making specific predictions almost every time he opens a camera, and how to read that tension honestly rather than pretend it isn't there.

3.1 A market that breathes, not one that trends forever

Before any screen, any setup, or any stop-loss rule, Khoo's method rests on a claim about what a market fundamentally is: something that moves in recurring phases rather than in a straight line, up or down, forever. He states the principle plainly — markets move through cycles, nothing goes up forever and nothing goes down forever, running from uptrend to consolidation to downtrend and back around again. He gives the same idea a four-part shape in several videos: an uptrend of rising demand, a distribution phase where big institutions quietly sell into retail buying, a downtrend once retail realizes no one else is buying, and an accumulation phase where institutions quietly buy back before the next uptrend begins. He calls the higher-order version of this the market "breathing" — breaths in and breaths out — while being explicit that no one can predict exactly when the next breath will come, only that it will.

This is not offered as poetry. He backs it with a specific, repeated statistic: in the long run the index always goes up, making higher highs and higher lows, however violent the short-term moves are, and by his count markets are actually in an uptrend roughly 90 percent of the time. He puts numbers on it more than once. Adding up historical bull and bear periods since 1935 gives 78 years of bull market against 8 years of bear market — a 91 percent bull-market share. Elsewhere he runs the same arithmetic and gets 64 bull years against 11.1 bear years, which he frames as markets being in "summer" 85 percent of the time and "winter" only 15 percent. He also states that the index posts an annual gain roughly 74 to 75 percent of the time, so buying in January and selling in December ends the year green about three years out of four. And he adds a counterweight to the bullish framing: a drop of more than 30 percent in a single year has happened in only about three of the last 90 years — the cyclical model is bullish on balance, but the numbers he cites describe a distribution, not a guarantee.

Bear markets, in his account, are the recurring, dependable part of the cycle rather than an aberration. He states the base rate directly and repeats it across years of videos: bear markets come roughly every eight to ten years and tend to last from six months to two years. He extends the base rate into a piece of arithmetic aimed at a younger investor: if a crash arrives every eight to ten years, a twenty- or thirty-year-old should expect to live through six or seven more of them across an investing lifetime. He pairs the frequency statistic with a severity range — historical bear markets have run from a 20-to-30 percent drawdown up to a 70 percent drawdown, with the bull markets that follow them typically returning 200 to 500 percent from the bottom. And he ties the cycle to valuation directly: index price-earnings ratios sit near 25 to 30 at market tops and have fallen as low as roughly 5 during the worst crises of the last 130 years, with the market's average top coming in around a PE of 25 to 26 (as high as 46 during the 2000 dot-com bubble).

Editor's note — reading a base rate correctly

A base rate like "bear markets come every eight to ten years" describes an average interval across a long historical sample; it does not describe a countdown timer. The gap between one bear market and the next has varied substantially even within the sample he draws from — a few years in some stretches, well over a decade in others. Meteorologists make the same distinction when they say a region has a "hundred-year flood": the number describes an average frequency over a long record, not a promise about which specific year the flood arrives. Khoo's statistic is real and useful for

sizing expectations over a multi-decade holding period; it is not,

by itself, a timing tool for any single year, and as the rest of this chapter shows, he does not actually use it that way either.

3.2 The mechanism behind the cycle: how a top forms and how a bottom forms

Khoo does not leave the four-phase cycle abstract — he attaches a mechanism, and the mechanism is about who is buying and who is selling, not about macro narrative. A top, in his description, forms during the "distribution" phase: big institutions begin selling into strength slowly enough that price keeps rising and retail investors keep piling in, unaware that the largest holders are quietly exiting. The uptrend typically ends, he adds, with a final parabolic surge — price moving up at close to 90 degrees rather than a healthy, sustainable 45-degree slope — which he reads as a sign of euphoria, the belief that price will keep rising forever.

The downtrend that follows begins, in his account, once retail investors notice that no new buyers are coming in and start selling in panic, and it bottoms out through the mirror-image process: "accumulation," where institutions buy back slowly enough that retail can't see it happening, followed by a final capitulation event where the last remaining panicked sellers capitulate and there is, definitionally, no one left to sell. He defines capitulation directly as the point where the crowd concludes the market will never recover, price collapses at close to 90 degrees on panic selling, and the word itself means "surrender". He states the logic in its most compressed form more than once: the market turns higher exactly when the last capitulating seller — "the last man" — has jumped, and the point of maximum panic is, for that structural reason, also the point of maximum opportunity.

He treats this as an emotional mechanism as much as a mechanical one. The stock market, he says, is not purely rational — it is emotional, and understanding a company's business logic is not sufficient without also reading the emotional state of the crowd trading it. A specific tell he gives for the transition out of a downtrend: negative news stops moving the price down, because the market has become so numbed to bad news that it no longer reacts to more of it — that resistance to bad news is, in his reading, itself a signal that institutional accumulation is already underway.

Editor's note — a real case where "bad news stops moving the price" showed up

One well-documented instance of this pattern is the US equity market in the first quarter of 2009. Bank failures, mass layoffs, and downward GDP revisions continued through February and March of that year, yet the S&P 500 stopped making new lows in early March and began a sustained rally even as headline news stayed grim for months afterward. The mechanism Khoo describes — institutional buying absorbing sell pressure while retail sentiment is still dominated by fear — is one widely cited explanation among market historians for why price and headline sentiment can decouple exactly at a turning point, though attributing any single episode to one specific cause after the fact is easier than identifying it in real time.

3.3 The "no one can predict" principle, stated as often as anything in the corpus

Across a decade of videos, one claim recurs with striking consistency: nobody can predict the market. He states it about specific, named authorities — no one can predict the stock market, he says, not Warren Buffett, George Soros, Ben Bernanke, or Janet Yellen, and anyone claiming they reliably can is either lying, guessing, or selling something. He tells his own students never to try, arguing that decisions made from a prediction almost always end either in a loss or in a missed opportunity. He generalizes the claim to news itself: there is no point reading financial news, in his view, because you cannot predict what the news will say next or how the market will react to it even once it does.

He roots the claim in a specific, retrospective exercise he returns to more than once — running through a montage of confident doom forecasts from named commentators across 2010–2018 that simply failed to happen, and drawing the lesson that reading and believing such calls costs you gains rather than protecting you from losses. He cites Alan Greenspan's 1996 "irrational exuberance" remark — a call that the market was overvalued and due for a fall — against what actually happened next: the S&P 500 continued from about 750 to roughly 1,500 in the years that followed. He extends the same critique to his own trading practice, saying that on any single trade the outcome is random and unknowable in advance — an edge only becomes visible across a large sample of fifty to a hundred trades, never on one.

The claim is stated broadly enough to cover every timeframe he discusses. He says no one can know how long an established trend will last, only where it currently sits. He says no one can know how long a bull market specifically will run — in one video giving a range of "one year, five years, or nine years" as equally plausible on the day the video was made. He extends it to bear markets and recessions, saying it is impossible to know with certainty when one will start, how long it will last, or how deep it will go. And he generalizes the whole principle into a single sentence more than once: investors and traders are not fortune tellers, they have no way to know when a trend will reverse, and all anyone actually has is a read of where price sits right now.

3.4 The tension, documented: the caveat and the forecast, back to back

The corpus does not merely contain the "no one can predict" claim and, in a separate set of videos, a set of specific market calls. It contains, dozens of times, both statements delivered within the same video — sometimes within the same breath — and the book states this plainly rather than smoothing it over, because it is one of the most consistent patterns anywhere in the archive.

The clearest single instance: he says no one can predict the future and that he has no idea when the next crash will happen, and then, within the same video, predicts one arriving within roughly a few months to a year. The pattern repeats with almost identical shape elsewhere. Asked directly whether anyone predicted a specific sell-off, he answers that no one can predict the future and there's no way to know in advance when a move like that happens — and then, in the same discussion, gives his own read of what happens next. Discussing a potential shakeout, he states explicitly that he cannot predict whether a breakout or a sell-off will occur, and then, later in the same video, repeatedly forecasts a likely sell-off scenario and how to position for it. Right after telling viewers he doesn't have a crystal ball and doesn't claim to know short-term direction, he states his own specific guess that a particular month will be more bullish than consensus expects. Immediately after repeating that no one can predict where the market goes in the short term, he states he expects the market to be higher by year's end, barring an unforeseen event. He does the same with market-moving catalysts: right after saying no one can predict the market's direction, he names the two specific near-term events he believes will actually drive it that week. And in one case he says the caveat, then guesses a wave-down window of "July, August, or September," citing that those months are seasonally weaker on average.

He is, at least once, unusually candid about the mechanism behind his own consistency here. Discussing his own annual outlook videos, he admits he tells viewers the market will go up almost every single year, because doing so alone gives him roughly a 73 percent chance of looking right — matching the base rate for an up year cited in Section 3.1 — while acknowledging in the same breath that no one can actually predict the future. Elsewhere he states outright that he has found, over years of making these calls, that market predictions "are not very useful," since no one truly knows how things will play out — a statement that itself follows a video full of specific calls.

The pattern also shows up over longer time horizons with named, dated targets. Discussing a 2026 bubble-risk video, he notes that Jeremy Grantham's April 2023 call for a 50 percent S&P 500 drop was instead followed by an 86 percent market rise, and tallies Grantham's crash calls across two decades — 2007, 2010, 2014, 2018, 2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, and 2026 — crediting him with being right only once, ahead of the 2007–2009 crisis. In the same video he runs the arithmetic on what happens if you'd simply stayed invested through the drop Grantham predicted rather than exit on his call: even a 50 percent drop from a stated starting level, held through with dividends reinvested over twenty years, still produces roughly a 316 percent total return, about 9.2 percent annualized. He runs a similar exercise contrasting his own bull-market call against a list of professional forecasters — Deutsche Bank, the IMF, and Marko Kolanovic among them — who he says called for a downturn that didn't arrive, and elsewhere lists a set of bearish public calls from Michael Burry, a fund manager forecasting a crash worse than 1929, and Morgan Stanley's Mike Wilson, contrasted against his own bullish position at the time.

Editor's note — why this tension is worth stating rather than resolving

It would be tidy to conclude that Khoo's "no one can predict" line is empty rhetoric and his real view is whatever forecast follows it, or conversely that the forecasts are throwaway color and the disclaimer is his real view. Neither reading survives contact with how often, and how consistently across nearly a decade, both statements appear together. A more honest reading, drawn from his own words rather than supplied by the book, is that he treats the two claims as operating at different levels: a philosophical claim about certainty (no one has a reliable crystal ball) alongside a practical claim about probability (given the base rates in Section 3.1, some outcomes are simply more likely than others, and he is willing to act on that likelihood while naming it a guess rather than a certainty). That reading is generous to him, and it is also not something he states explicitly anywhere in the material gathered for this chapter — so the book offers it as a possible resolution, not a confirmed one, and leaves the documented tension itself on the page either way.

3.5 Bottom-calling checklists — and the caveat that keeps them honest

Where Section 3.4 shows the tension in words, this section shows it in procedure: Khoo has built several named, mechanical checklists for calling a market bottom, and in nearly every case he pairs the checklist with an explicit statement of its own unreliability.

His most detailed bottom-fishing method combines two technical readings on weekly S&P 500 candles: Williams %R falling below minus 80, and a normalized Average True Range rising above 80, with both conditions required at once rather than either alone. He walks through its historical hit rate honestly rather than only showcasing its wins: in the 2000–2002 dot-com bear market, he says the two signals coincided at the mid-2002 low and called the bottom to the exact week; in the 2007–2009 financial crisis, the same two signals flagged the area before the February 2009 low but not the exact bottom, which arrived only after a second leg down; in March 2020 the same combination fired with the normalized ATR reading an unusually extreme 120. He states the honest limitation of the whole method directly: investors do not need to predict or catch the exact bottom, and he calls the indicator's entertainment value at least as real as its practical value.

A second, more informal signal he names repeatedly is what he calls the "sad face indicator": when mainstream financial news coverage of a falling market runs a photo of a visibly distressed person alongside language like "worst day," he reads that media saturation of despair as a contrarian marker of proximity to a bottom, citing a September 2021 CNBC headline as an example — and, in a line that undercuts any claim to precision, adds that if the sad-faced person in the photo is also shown praying, he treats that as an even stronger version of the same signal. He is direct about its failure mode too: his own March 2020 sad-face signal did not mark the exact low, and the market continued falling for roughly four more days after it fired. A parabolic, near-vertical plunge of eight to ten consecutive red candles is another pattern he names as a bottom signal, again only "at least temporarily", and he separately warns that traders should expect to be faked out by several false reversals before a real bottom forms — calling that normal rather than evidence the method is broken.

He extends the same honesty to sentiment-based signals drawn from positioning data rather than price action: record-low asset-manager positioning in equity futures as a contrarian bullish marker historically associated with past bear-market bottoms; a retail investor bull-bear ratio of 0.82 (more bears than bulls) as an extreme-sentiment marker; elevated put-option volume as a percentage of market cap as a fear gauge that reached similar extremes in March 2020 and 2011. Even after a confirming retracement level is cleared, he warns, markets have historically pulled back again before making a genuine new high — a caution against treating any single confirmation as final.

Editor's note — why a "requires both signals at once" rule is still not unfalsifiable, but a "can double-bottom" rule is close

There's a real methodological difference between the two kinds of caveat in this section. A rule like "Williams %R below minus 80 and normalized ATR above 80, both at once" is a testable, falsifiable condition — you can go back and check whether it fired, and whether price subsequently rose, across historical data, which is exactly what he does across three separate crises. A rule that a bottom pattern can "fake out" several times before the real one, or that a confirmed low can still be revisited afterward, is considerably harder to falsify in real time: almost any subsequent move can be retroactively explained as "one of the fake-outs" until, eventually, one of them turns out to be the actual low. That doesn't make the observation false — markets genuinely do retest lows — but it does mean a reader should notice that this class of caveat, honestly stated as it is, protects the method from ever being cleanly disproven within the window that would actually matter to a trader trying to act on it.

3.6 Watch what they do, not what they say

Khoo's own practice, at least as he describes it, leans toward distrusting public commentary — including expert commentary — in favor of what price and positioning actually show. That preference has a documented parallel outside his own channel, in cases where a well-known investor's public statements and disclosed trading positions diverged.

Michael Burry is the case that appears directly in this corpus: Khoo cites him as a hedge fund manager who made a reported five billion dollars during the 2008 subprime crisis by shorting mortgage-backed securities, and separately lists a public "sell" call from Burry among a montage of bearish forecasters contrasted against Khoo's own bullish position at the time.

Editor's note — public statements versus disclosed positions, three outside cases

Khoo's own material only goes as far as citing Burry's public calls; it's worth adding, as background the transcripts don't supply, that Burry himself has been a well-documented example of the gap between commentary and disclosed position — his firm's 13F regulatory filings have shown large reported bearish options positions (for example against major index ETFs in 2023) that were later reduced or closed within a quarter or two of being disclosed, well before the headlines built around the filing had fully faded. Jeremy Grantham, whose specific crash calls Khoo tallies directly in Section 3.4, is a second example: GMO's own disclosed asset-allocation shifts have at times moved toward more constructive equity positioning even in years when Grantham's public commentary remained heavily bearish. Stanley Druckenmiller is a third frequently cited case in financial media: his firm's 13F filings have shown him building and then unwinding sizable technology positions within single-quarter windows, on a timeline that a same-quarter public interview would not necessarily have signaled. None of this proves any of the three men were being misleading — public commentary and disclosed positions serve different purposes and are filed on different clocks, so some gap between them is structurally normal — but it is a real, checkable illustration of exactly the discipline Khoo's own practice implies: that a position statement filed with a regulator carries different evidentiary weight than a quote given on television, and a careful reader treats them differently rather than treating public commentary as if it were the position itself.

Read against Section 3.4, the throughline is consistent: Khoo distrusts forecasts — his own included, by his own admission — and the evidence he reaches for to make that case is almost always a comparison between what someone said publicly and what subsequently, verifiably, happened to price. The "watch what they do, not what they say" principle is never stated by him as a formal rule in these terms, but it is the pattern that his own examples, gathered across a decade of videos, keep demonstrating.

What he doesn't say

He does not, anywhere in the material gathered for this chapter, explain the seeming contradiction between stating "no one can predict the market" and then issuing a specific forecast in the same video — he never frames it, in his own words, as anything other than two separate, true statements sitting next to each other. He does not state a rule for how much weight a reader should put on one of his own forecasts relative to the disclaimer that precedes it, nor does he revisit his own past specific calls systematically to score their accuracy against his stated framework, apart from the isolated retrospective moments gathered here. He also never states, for any of his bottom-calling indicators, a quantified false-positive rate — how often the sad-face indicator or the parabolic-plunge pattern fires without a genuine bottom following — beyond the qualitative "expect to be faked out a few times" caveat. Finally, he does not address, in the corpus searched for this chapter, whether the "watch what they do, not what they say" instinct that runs through his treatment of other forecasters applies with equal force to his own publicly stated views versus his own disclosed trades — the parallel case for himself is left for the reader to draw, not something he draws explicitly for them.

Chapter summary

  • Khoo's core model is a market that moves through recurring, self-reinforcing phases — uptrend, distribution, downtrend, accumulation — driven by institutions quietly acting ahead of retail investors who only notice each phase once it is already underway.
  • He backs the cyclical model with base rates rather than only narrative: markets are in an uptrend roughly 85 to 91 percent of the time by his count, post an annual gain about 74 to 75 percent of years, and undergo a bear market roughly once every eight to ten years lasting six months to two years.
  • He states, across nearly a decade of videos and about nearly every named authority including himself, that no one can reliably predict the market's direction, timing, or duration.
  • That stated principle and a specific market forecast appear together, often in the same breath, dozens of times in the corpus — a documented, recurring pattern this book states plainly rather than resolves in his favor or against him.
  • He has, at least once, explicitly acknowledged the mechanism behind his own forecasting consistency — predicting an up year nearly every year gives him roughly a 73 percent chance of being right, matching the base rate he himself cites.
  • His bottom-calling checklists (the dual Williams %R / ATR signal, the "sad-face indicator," the parabolic-plunge pattern) are stated alongside explicit, honest caveats about their own unreliability and their history of false signals.
  • Long-horizon evidence he cites himself undercuts the value of acting on specific crash forecasts: staying invested through the very 50 percent drop Jeremy Grantham predicted in April 2023 would still have produced roughly a 316 percent total return over twenty years.
  • His own practice consistently favors checkable evidence — price action, disclosed positioning, historical base rates — over public commentary, including his own, a discipline documented outside his channel in cases like Michael Burry's, where disclosed trading positions have not always matched the public statements built around them.
Chapter 4

Value Momentum Investing: The Screen

In this chapter: the quality gate every candidate must clear in Adam Khoo's investing book before a valuation is even run — the growth-consistency test, the moat taxonomy, the profitability thresholds, and the debt checks — together with his own admissions of where the screen was applied late or wrongly.

4.1 Quality before price

Khoo states his own ordering rule directly: he does not buy a stock just because it is cheap, and quality is always the priority with valuation coming second. He frames the practical case for this ordering in terms of what a business actually is — a money-making machine whose future profits determine what it is eventually worth, regardless of short-term price volatility. The screen he applies before valuation is what he calls, across many videos, his "seven-step criteria" for a great business, though the exact count varies in how he presents it: in one video he calls it seven steps, in another eight, and in others he only walks through the "most important" of the seven on camera. The consistent shape, regardless of the count given in any one telling, is that the first several steps qualify the business on quality alone — a wide moat, consistent revenue and cash-flow growth, high return on capital, and low debt — before valuation enters the process at all.

He is candid about how selective this makes him. He says that in any market, less than one percent of stocks are investable by his standard — in the US, of roughly eight thousand stocks, he says fewer than fifty are safe enough to invest in. Companies like SpaceX or Micron, he says, sit in the ninety-nine percent he will not invest in, though he may still trade them short-term on price action alone. That distinction — tradeable versus investable — recurs throughout this book's account of his two portfolios, and it is the screen described in this chapter that draws the line between them.

Editor's note — why a quality gate belongs before valuation

A quality screen that runs before valuation is a standard idea in value investing, associated most closely with Warren Buffett's shift away from pure statistical cheapness (the "cigar butt" style of his mentor Benjamin Graham) toward paying a fair price for a wonderful business. The logic is that a cheap statistic attached to a deteriorating business can still lose money — the business erodes faster than the discount closes — while a wonderful business bought at a fair price keeps compounding for the holder even if the initial price was not a bargain. Khoo credits his own version of this framework to Buffett by name in several of the cards below, which is consistent with this being an inherited framework rather than one he originated.

4.2 The first gate: consistent growth

The opening test in nearly every telling of the seven steps is the same: has the company consistently grown sales revenue, net income, and cash flow from operations, and is that trend likely to continue. He is specific about the lookback window, which he states most often as five to ten years, though in at least one video he narrows it to just "five years" as the floor. He calls this step "a must," with no exceptions offered at that point in the video, and elsewhere says that this first criterion alone — a history of consistently increasing revenue, net income, and operating cash flow for at least five years — screens out ninety-nine percent of companies in any market on its own.

He checks each line separately rather than treating "growth" as one aggregate number: revenue, net income, and cash flow from operations each get checked over both a five-year and a ten-year window. Working through Salesforce as an example, he checks revenue, net profit, operating cash flow, and free cash flow all rising consistently over five to ten years before moving on to debt. He treats profit margins the same way, wanting gross and net margins rising or at least holding steady over the same period, and he compares the trailing twelve months against the five-year average to judge direction rather than looking at a single year in isolation.

This is not a rule he applies rigidly without exception. When examining Kraft Heinz, he judges its revenue as essentially flat over roughly five years — slightly up in 2016, slightly down in 2017 — and states plainly that this does not count as consistent growth by his standard, contrasting it with Facebook and Tencent. Applied to a holding he already owned, he notes that Disney fails this same first screening criterion, since both profit and free cash flow dropped during its streaming-service transition. He has also granted an explicit exception: for Celsius, a young, speculative growth company, he waived his usual five-year track-record requirement because free cash flow was already consistent even though net income was not yet — a conditional exception, stated as such, not a silent loosening of the rule.

A growth catalyst is checked alongside the historical record. He states a second criterion — a positive future growth rate anchored in an identifiable growth catalyst — as distinct from the backward-looking consistency check. For IPO candidates specifically, he wants the company operating in an industry with a secular growth catalyst — he names biotechnology, 5G, artificial intelligence, e-commerce, and social media as examples — and contrasts this with a "sunset industry" such as Kodak's film business.

4.3 The moat: what protects the growth

The second major gate is what he calls, borrowing the term from Warren Buffett, an economic moat — a sustainable competitive advantage that stops competitors from taking market share or customers. He states this as close to non-negotiable: he says he will never invest in a no-moat company, though he may still trade one short-term on price action, and elsewhere frames it as investing only in businesses with "no competition, or whose competitors are insignificant and unable to take market share". Across many videos he repeats the same practical consequence: a wide moat lets a company raise prices, protect its market share, and generate consistent, predictable growth in sales, income, and free cash flow — which is why the moat check sits upstream of, and reinforces, the growth-consistency check in section 4.2.

He names four or five recurring sources of moat, with some variation in count and ordering across videos, but the substance is stable:

  • Brand monopoly — top-of-mind recall, the first thing a consumer thinks of in a category, which he sometimes quantifies as holding more than fifty percent market share. His recurring examples are Google for search and YouTube for video, and Adobe, where he cites ninety percent of global creative professionals using at least one Adobe product.
  • Network effect — a platform where more users makes the platform more valuable to every other user, so growth is self-reinforcing. He applies this to Tencent's social and messaging dominance in China and to UnitedHealth's scale as an insurer.
  • High switching costs — it is expensive or inconvenient for an existing customer to move to a competitor. His examples include designers who have spent years learning Adobe's tools, users locked into Microsoft Office, and his own experience staying with an iPhone despite believing a Samsung phone might be better or cheaper, because of Apple's ecosystem lock-in.
  • High barriers to entry — a structural cost or technical hurdle that competitors cannot easily clear. His clearest example is chip foundries, where he estimates roughly five billion dollars is required to move up one manufacturing node, making the race effectively winner-take-all for whoever gets there first.
  • Economies of scale — cost advantages from sheer size that let a company price below smaller rivals. He applies this to Amazon's e-commerce and cloud share and to Tesla's roughly fifty-five percent fall in per-vehicle production cost over four years.

He is explicit that a company need not have every source at once: he says he only buys a business with at least one or two of the moat characteristics. He also treats moat strength as directly linked to the consistency test in section 4.2 — a company without a monopoly-like position tends to show choppy, up-and-down financials, which is why he contrasts Applied Materials's uneven results with ASML's steadily rising sales and profits.

In more recent videos he describes scoring moat quantitatively, on a one-to-ten scale, using an AI tool he calls "Stock Oracle" that breaks a company's moat down into the same component sources — brand loyalty and pricing power, barriers to entry, switching cost, network effect, and economies of scale. He says he generally prefers "wide moat" companies scoring high on this scale but will still buy a narrow-moat company around a 6 out of 10.

Editor's note — moat sources in standard competitive-strategy terms

The five categories Khoo names map closely onto sources of durable competitive advantage described in mainstream competitive-strategy writing — intangible brand assets, network effects, customer switching costs, structural barriers to entry, and cost advantages from scale — the same taxonomy popularized in equity-research contexts by Morningstar's moat ratings, a source he references directly when discussing Tesla (see below). None of this is Khoo's own invention; he attributes the moat concept itself to Buffett by name in multiple videos. It is included here because the reader needs the vocabulary to follow the rest of this chapter, not because Khoo claims originality for it.

Figure 4.1 — Moat types compared

Moat sourceWhat he tests forHis worked example
Brand monopolyTop-of-mind recall, often >50% category shareGoogle/YouTube
Network effectMore users make the product more valuable to every userTencent messaging in China; UnitedHealth scale
High switching costsExpensive or inconvenient for a customer to leaveAdobe design tools, Microsoft Office; Apple ecosystem
High barriers to entryStructural cost/technical hurdle blocking new entrantsChip foundries, ~$5B per node upgrade
Economies of scaleLarger size lets the company undercut smaller rivals on costAmazon e-commerce/cloud share; Tesla per-vehicle cost drop

Figure 4.1 — The five moat sources Khoo names across his videos, with the worked company example he gives for each. A company need not show all five; he says he requires at least one or two.

4.4 Profitability: ROE and ROIC thresholds

Once growth and moat pass, he applies a profitability filter built around two related ratios. He wants return on equity above 12 to 15 percent, treating anything above 15 percent as excellent, and states the same 12-percent floor and 15-percent "excellent" line repeatedly across videos spanning several years, applying it to companies as different as Winnebago (24 percent ROE), Nike (33 percent), and Lululemon (41 percent).

He treats return on invested capital as the more important of the two ratios, attributing that emphasis to Warren Buffett, and wants it above 12 to 15 percent and either consistent or rising. He explains the reason ROIC matters more than ROE: ROIC accounts for the debt used to finance a business, so a company can show a high ROE while its ROIC is much lower, meaning ROE alone can be inflated by leverage. His combined rule, stated in several videos, is that both ROE and ROIC above 12 to 15 percent together mark a "very good company," and a company where both sit well above 15 percent he calls a "compounder". He makes one consistent industry exception: for insurance companies, where ROIC is structurally lower, he says he can "close one eye" on that particular threshold, citing Chubb as an example.

Editor's note — ROE and ROIC, defined

Return on equity is net profit divided by shareholders' equity: how much profit a company generates per dollar that shareholders themselves have invested. Return on invested capital divides profit by total invested capital — equity plus debt — so it captures how efficiently the whole balance sheet, not just the equity slice, is being put to work. A company can borrow heavily to boost ROE (leverage magnifies the return on the equity portion) while its ROIC, measured against the full capital base, stays flat or falls — which is exactly the distortion Khoo is screening against by requiring both ratios to clear his threshold rather than relying on ROE alone.

4.5 Debt: three ratios, checked together

He states that debt cannot be judged in isolation — it has to be measured against earnings and cash flow to judge real financial strength, a point he raises specifically in defense of large tech companies (Meta, Google, Microsoft, Amazon) that carry substantial debt loads. He uses three ratios together, and is explicit that a company can pass one and fail the others:

  1. Current ratio — current assets divided by current liabilities. He wants this above one, treating it as a floor rather than an optimum, and applies it literally: he accepts a company with a ratio of 1.04 as passing. He uses General Electric's 0.67 current ratio in 2020 — more current liabilities than current assets — as a warning sign the company could not meet its short-term obligations, and cites Bed Bath & Beyond's 0.73 the same way. By contrast, he cites Meituan's 2.29 and ARM's 3.42 as comfortably strong.
  2. Debt-to-EBITDA — he treats a ratio above 3 as dangerous over-leverage and avoids such companies regardless of how cheap they look, using Oracle's software business as an example he passed on for exactly this reason despite the stock screening 44 percent undervalued.
  3. Debt-servicing ratio and interest coverage — he wants interest expense divided by operating cash flow below 30 percent, and separately wants interest coverage (operating income divided by interest expense) of at least three to five times or higher, citing Meta's coverage in the seventies as "very safe" in the example he used.

Worked across several companies in the ledger, these three ratios travel together rather than being checked in isolation: Mettler Toledo passes all three (debt/EBITDA 1.79, interest coverage 13), Paycom passes extremely comfortably (debt/EBITDA 0.04, interest coverage 300x), while Choice Hotels is passed over specifically because its debt/EBITDA of 3.98 fails even though its ROE and ROIC both look strong — illustrating that a profitability pass does not override a debt fail. He states the same principle for banks specifically: Silicon Valley Bank passed his ROE profitability filter at 12.1 percent, but he is explicit that passing profitability does not mean a bank is safe, since safety requires separate risk criteria entirely.

He also distinguishes a shrinking free cash flow that is a genuine warning sign from one that is not.
