If free cash flow turns negative because revenue and operating cash flow are themselves declining, he treats that as a real signal to sell or avoid. If free cash flow turns negative because of temporary growth capital expenditure while revenue and operating cash flow are still rising, he is not concerned — the same number, read two different ways depending on what is driving it.

4.6 Where the screen has been misapplied

Khoo's own record includes several disclosed cases where the quality screen either produced a distorted read or was corrected too late. Analyzing Autodesk, he identifies a specific valuation error that other people make: projecting the company's most recent year of profit — $1.3 billion — straight into the future, which produces an inflated intrinsic value without checking why the figure jumped so high. The jump, he explains, was driven by a one-time special income tax credit that does not represent ongoing earnings power; his own correction is to average several years of pre-tax earnings rather than project the single distorted year forward.

He also discloses moat judgments changing after the fact, on positions he already held. He lowered UnitedHealth's moat rating from wide to narrow after concluding new regulation could limit its pricing power, though he still considers the business effectively monopolistic in managed healthcare. He recounts selling all of his Adobe shares around $400 or more after he stopped being confident in its long-run moat, reinvesting the proceeds into Nvidia and Amazon, which he judged to have stronger moats at the time. He sold Baidu on the stated grounds that it had lost its dominance in search and its consumer stickiness, and sold Lululemon partly on concern that its moat was weakening from rising athleisure competition, more dated styles, and executive departures. On Tesla, he tracks the erosion directly: US EV market share falling from over 70 percent to 65 percent, cited as evidence the brand-monopoly source of its moat is "already eroding".

He frames the general decision rule this way: if a held company hits a short-term headwind — a cyclical downturn, higher R&D spending, a lawsuit — but its moat stays intact, that is not a mistake, and the response is to hold or add. If the moat itself permanently deteriorates after purchase — a structural shift in consumer behavior, regulation, or technology — that is a mistake, and the correct response is to sell and reinvest elsewhere. He restates the same distinction as a ten-item taxonomy in a later video: nine reasons a stock can drop are cyclical or temporary (missed estimates, lowered guidance, a cyclical demand slowdown, forced shareholder liquidation, and similar), and he is a buyer on all of them; the tenth reason — genuine structural deterioration of the business model or moat — is the one case where he is a seller.

Editor's note — self-correction as evidence the screen is real

A screen that a person only ever applies to justify decisions already made is not a real filter; a screen that occasionally forces a reversal on a position the person already holds and likely does not want to sell is evidence the filter is doing independent work. The Adobe sale and the UnitedHealth moat downgrade are both cases where Khoo's stated criteria moved against a position he held, rather than being used after the fact to justify one he wanted to keep — which is the kind of disclosure this book treats as more informative than a track record of calls that were simply correct.

4.7 Conditions and edge cases

The screen is not applied identically to every company. Cyclical companies are exempted from part of the process rather than screened the same way as steady compounders: he defines a cyclical company as one whose profits historically move up and down rather than rising consistently, and states that ordinary price-earnings analysis does not work for this category at all, illustrating the trap with Micron's low forward P/E of 5.6, which he calls an illusion because the current high earnings are not sustainable. His stated response when he cannot tell whether a cyclical company's earnings are sustainable is to stay away from investing in it rather than guess — while noting he may still trade the same name short-term on price action.

He also ties the length of his own valuation projection to the strength of the moat rather than using a fixed horizon for every company: he says he will only project ten years, rather than the twenty he uses for an established compounder, for a company like Snapchat that does not yet have a solid, wide moat. And profitability thresholds bend for financial companies: his 15 percent ROIC floor is relaxed for insurers such as Chubb, and his ROE floor for banks is stated separately and lower, at 10 percent rather than 12 to 15.

What he doesn't say about this

Khoo never states a single, fixed count for his own screening process across the archive — the "seven steps" are sometimes seven, sometimes described as eight, and in most videos he says he is only walking through the most important few rather than the full list, reserving the complete version for his paid course. He does not disclose a back-tested or third-party-verified record of how many candidates that pass every stage of this screen (growth, moat, ROE/ROIC, and debt, together) go on to outperform the market — the "less than one percent of stocks are investable" figure is stated as his personal standard, not as a measured result. He also does not explain, in any card in this archive, how he weighs a close call — for example a company passing four of the five criteria strongly but failing one by a small margin — beyond the individual examples given above (Choice Hotels failing on debt despite strong ROE/ROIC; Oracle failing on debt despite an attractive valuation). Whether there is a formal scoring system behind those individual calls, or whether each is a judgment made case by case, is not something he addresses directly.

Chapter summary

  • Quality is checked before valuation: he does not buy a stock for being cheap, and the "seven-step" screen qualifies a business on growth, moat, profitability, and debt before intrinsic value is calculated at all.
  • The first gate is consistent growth in revenue, net income, and operating cash flow, checked separately over a five-to-ten-year window, which by itself eliminates roughly ninety-nine percent of companies in any market.
  • He requires an economic moat and will not invest in a no-moat company, though he may still trade one short-term; the five recurring sources are brand monopoly, network effect, high switching costs, high barriers to entry, and economies of scale, with at least one or two required.
  • His profitability floor is return on equity and return on invested capital both above 12 to 15 percent, with ROIC weighted as more important because it is not inflated by leverage the way ROE can be.
  • Debt is checked with three ratios used together — current ratio above 1, debt-to-EBITDA below roughly 3, and interest coverage of at least 3 to 5 times — and a company can pass one of the three while failing the others.
  • The screen bends for specific categories: cyclical companies are excluded from ordinary P/E analysis, insurers and banks get a lower profitability bar, and projection horizons shorten for companies without an established moat.
  • He has disclosed the screen being misapplied and later corrected — averaging distorted tax-credit-inflated earnings at Autodesk, and downgrading moat ratings on positions he already held, including selling Adobe and Baidu outright after concluding their moats had weakened.
  • His decision rule for a price decline in a held stock is binary: nine kinds of drop (cyclical, headline, temporary) are buying opportunities so long as the moat is intact; the tenth — genuine structural deterioration of the business or its moat — is the one case where he sells.
Chapter 5

Valuation and Margin of Safety

In this chapter: the discounted-cash-flow conventions Khoo applies once a company clears the quality screen from Chapter 4, worked with his own disclosed numbers across Amazon, Alphabet, Adobe, NIO, and the Disney turnaround case, together with the margin-of-safety discipline that decides when — not whether — to buy.

5.1 What he is actually trying to measure

Khoo's starting claim is a simple one: the only true way to value a company is by how much money it can generate in the future, not by what the market is currently paying for it. He defines intrinsic value using discounted-cash-flow logic directly — a dollar received in the future is worth less than a dollar received today because of the opportunity cost of holding money, so every future dollar of cash a business will generate must be divided by (1 + a discount rate) for each year of delay before it counts toward the company's present worth. He treats net profit as a number that can be distorted by accounting choices — depreciation and capital expenditure timing among them — and treats free cash flow as the more honest measure of what a business actually generates. Once he has that per-year stream of value, discounted back to the present and divided by shares outstanding, he compares the resulting per-share figure to the market price: if the price sits meaningfully below it, the stock is undervalued by his model; if not, it isn't.

He is explicit that this is arithmetic he does himself and cross-checks against outside estimates, not a number he takes on faith from an analyst. Working through Adobe as a teaching example with his daughter, he pulls operating cash flow, capital expenditure, total debt, and cash directly from the company's own filings, then applies a growth rate sourced from external analyst projections rather than one he invents himself. He regularly checks his own resulting figure against Morningstar's and GuruFocus's published intrinsic-value estimates, and says he feels more comfortable when his own number comes in lower than theirs, since a more conservative valuation gives more margin of safety.

Editor's note — discounted cash flow, in one paragraph

A discounted-cash-flow (DCF) valuation projects how much cash a business will generate in each future year, then converts each of those future amounts into today's dollars by dividing by (1 + discount rate) raised to the power of how many years away it falls, and finally sums all those discounted amounts into one present value. Two inputs dominate the result far more than intuition suggests: the growth rate applied to cash flow, and the discount rate used to shrink future dollars. Small changes in either compound over many years and can move the final answer by a large multiple — which is exactly why section 5.6 works through why the far-future, least-certain years of a DCF often matter more to the final number than the near-certain first few years do.

5.2 The three-stage growth taper

Across the archive, Khoo applies growth to a company's cash flow (or net income — see section 5.4) in stages rather than a single flat rate for the whole projection. The pattern, repeated with variations across dozens of companies, is: a near-term rate for the first several years, usually sourced from a consensus of third-party analyst estimates; a reduced middle-stage rate; and a long-run terminal rate he pegs to GDP growth plus roughly one percent, which he treats as what any large, mature company converges toward once its growth advantage fades.

His near-term growth number is not his own guess. He says his practice is to average multiple third-party growth projections — he names Zacks, GuruFocus, Finviz, Capital IQ, and Simply Wall Street across different videos — rather than to substitute his own forecast. Working through Amazon in one full example, he fades the growth rate in three explicit stages: 34 percent for the first five years, halving to 15 percent for the next five, then down to four percent for the final ten years, which he describes as US GDP growth plus one percent and calls "standard assumptions". The same three-stage shape recurs on Nike (15 percent tapering to 4 percent), on Meta (6.8 percent for ten years, then 4 percent terminal), and on Nvidia (roughly 40.74 percent average, capped at 15 percent for years six through ten, then 4 percent terminal).

He is explicit that the growth rate is not his personal opinion of a company's prospects; it is meant to be an outside, checkable number. On Amazon's earlier 2020 valuation he stresses this directly: the growth rates he plugs in come from analysts who study the company, not from himself. When he does depart from a third-party number, he says so and flags it as a deliberate, conservative adjustment rather than a forecast — for example haircutting Alibaba's growth rate by 20 percent and calling the haircut itself "a guess," adding that the real impact is genuinely hard to project.

5.3 Where the discount rate comes from

For the discount rate, Khoo says he uses a version of the capital asset pricing model: the risk-free rate plus the stock's beta multiplied by the market risk premium, using ten-year averages for both the risk-free rate and the premium. He states this choice directly against two alternatives he attributes to other practitioners: Warren Buffett, he says, reportedly uses the 10-year Treasury yield alone as his discount rate, which he calls even more conservative than his own approach; and the CFA-taught convention is to use the weighted average cost of capital (WACC), blending the cost of debt and equity, which he does not use, opting instead for the cost of equity alone based on beta.

He translates beta into a discount rate through a banded lookup rather than a continuous formula in at least some of his walkthroughs: a beta near 1 (Tencent, at 1.04) gets the closest bucket's rate, which he states as 6 percent; a beta of 1.2 (Meta) gets roughly 6 percent; a low beta (UnitedHealth, at 5.37 percent) gets a correspondingly low discount rate; and any beta above 1.6 places a stock in his 8.4 percent band, which is why Tesla, with a beta of 1.88, is discounted at 8.4 percent in that video. He is explicit about what beta itself measures: how volatile a stock is relative to the market, so a beta of 1.88 means the stock is 88 percent more volatile than the S&P 500. For non-US companies he applies a higher discount rate specifically because of country risk — he uses 14 percent for Tencent in one video, well above the 6 percent his beta-bucket approach would otherwise imply, saying explicitly he raises it "because it is a China company".

5.4 Twenty years, no terminal value — and the alternative he uses instead

The single most repeated structural choice in Khoo's DCF method, stated across a decade of videos, is that he projects cash flow for exactly twenty years and then stops, rather than assuming the business continues indefinitely and capping the model with a terminal value the way most professional analysts do. He frames this explicitly as the more conservative choice: a business that survives past year twenty produces value he simply does not count, treating everything beyond the twentieth year as "a bonus". On Amazon specifically he notes the business will likely last thirty to fifty years, and he still caps the model at twenty, deliberately leaving that extra value on the table. He gives the same reasoning for using cost of equity rather than WACC as his discount rate: since he isn't discounting cash flows all the way to perpetuity, he says he doesn't need the more complex weighted-average formula that CFA convention pairs with a terminal-value model.

Not every company gets cash flow projected as the input. Khoo says he chooses among what amount to five valuation methods depending on the type of company: discounted free cash flow for businesses with consistent cash flow, discounted net income for insurers and commodity companies, and price-to-book or price-to-net-asset-value for banks, among others. The deciding test for choosing net income over free cash flow, he says, is consistency: if a company's capital expenditure is lumpy and its free cash flow grows unevenly, he projects net income instead, because projecting an inconsistent series doesn't make sense. Section 5.5 below works through exactly this choice on Amazon, where he uses both methods side by side.

Editor's note — why skipping the terminal value is conservative, and why it still matters less than it sounds

In a standard DCF, the terminal value — the assumed value of all cash flows beyond the projection window, discounted back to the present — often makes up the majority of a company's calculated worth, because a growing perpetuity of cash flows is worth much more than any finite run of years. Dropping the terminal value and stopping at year twenty removes that single largest component, which is a genuinely conservative choice, as Khoo states. But it does not remove the sensitivity to long-horizon assumptions that terminal values are usually criticized for: years eleven through twenty in his own model are still discounted at a low enough rate, and compound off high enough earlier-year growth, that they can still dominate the total. The model is more conservative than the industry standard in aggregate, but it is not immune to the general problem that a DCF's answer is mostly a statement about

assumptions for years the analyst is least able to know anything about.

5.5 Amazon, priced two ways

Khoo's fullest worked example of a single company valued by two different methods, side by side, is Amazon in his two-part "Evil Plan" series. He opens by explaining why he departs from his usual free-cash-flow approach for this company specifically: citing a distinction he attributes to Warren Buffett between maintenance capital expenditure (spending needed just to sustain the existing business, which should be deducted from cash flow when valuing it) and growth capital expenditure (spending to expand into new lines of business, which shouldn't be), he judges that Amazon's heavy, lumpy capex on new fulfillment and cloud infrastructure makes its reported free cash flow an unreliable input, and net profit history a more consistent one.

Method one — discounted net income. He first adjusts the trailing net income figure itself: rather than using the most recent trailing-twelve-month number, distorted in that period by a mark-to-market loss on Amazon's Rivian stake and a spike in R&D spending, he substitutes the prior full fiscal year's net income of $33 billion. Alongside that he inputs $73 billion of total debt (excluding capital leases) and $60 billion of cash and short-term investments. For growth, he averages third-party projections from Zacks, GuruFocus, and Finviz, which converge around 30 percent, applied for five years before tapering to 4 percent — the same three-stage taper pattern from section 5.2, this time applied to income rather than cash flow, and projected across the same twenty-year, no-terminal-value horizon from section 5.4. That model produces an intrinsic value of $191 per share against a $116 closing price, implying about 39 percent undervaluation.

Method two — sum-of-the-parts. He describes this as a second, more conservative check, valuing each of Amazon's business segments separately and adding them together rather than treating the company as one blended cash-flow stream. He values AWS at about $639 billion, from $71 billion of projected revenue, an operating margin above 30 percent (about $21.3 billion of operating profit), and a 30x price-to-earnings multiple. He values the advertising business at about $480 billion, from $40 billion of projected revenue at a 40 percent operating margin (about $16 billion of operating profit) and the same 30x multiple. He values the retail/e-commerce business at about $372 billion by applying Walmart's price-to-gross-merchandise-value multiple of 0.62x to Amazon's roughly $600 billion of GMV. And he values the Prime subscription business at about $136 billion, from $34 billion of revenue at a conservative assumed 20 percent operating margin (about $6.8 billion of operating profit) and a 20x multiple. Summed, the four segments total roughly $1.6 trillion, which works out to about $159 per share — noticeably below the $191 the discounted-net-income method produced.

He does not treat the gap between $191 and $159 as a problem to resolve into one number; both stand as separate, cross-checking estimates, and the lower one is, by his own stated preference in section 5.1, the more comfortable of the two to rely on for margin of safety.

Editor's note — why two methods can legitimately disagree by 20 percent

Discounted net income and sum-of-the-parts are answering slightly different questions. The income-based method treats Amazon as one earnings stream and applies a single growth and discount assumption to the whole company; the segment method prices each business line on the multiple a comparable pure-play company in that specific market would command — cloud infrastructure priced like a software business, retail priced like Walmart's GMV multiple,

and so on. A gap between the two is not evidence one of them is wrong; it is

a measure of how much the company's actual value depends on which comparison set (blended-earnings history versus segment-level peers) an analyst chooses to trust. Reporting both, as Khoo does here, is more honest than collapsing them into a single number.

5.6 Alphabet and Adobe: the same conventions, smaller gaps

Applied to Alphabet, the method looks the same but the inputs are cleaner, since Alphabet's free cash flow is consistent enough that Khoo values it directly rather than switching to net income. In one pass he takes a five-year analyst-consensus growth projection of 17.61 percent, halves it to about 8.8 percent for the back half of the projection, and applies it to the latest free cash flow, producing an intrinsic value near $161 against a $116 share price — about 28 percent undervalued. In a later video, with updated inputs of $67 billion trailing free cash flow, $12 billion debt, and $139 billion cash, the same twenty-year discounted-free-cash-flow model produces roughly $3,757 per share pre-split against a roughly $2,800 market price. Later still, projecting 20.5 percent free-cash-flow growth for five years and 15 percent for the next five, he arrives at $237 per share against a $162 price, about 32 percent undervalued. The recurring shape across all three passes is the same: a consensus-sourced near-term growth rate, a step down for the back half of the projection, cost-of-equity discounting from beta, and a comparison to the live market price rather than a fixed target.

Adobe gets the same three-stage-taper treatment with a smaller undervaluation gap, and it is where Khoo states his margin-of-safety convention most explicitly. His base-case model — operating cash flow, capital expenditure, total debt, and cash as inputs, a five-year growth rate sourced externally, then 15 percent for years six through ten and 4 percent for the remaining ten — produces $613 per share against a $428 price, a 30 percent discount. He then states a rule he applies generally, not just to Adobe: valuation should always carry a base case and a pessimistic case, and he demonstrates it by halving Adobe's growth assumption from roughly 16 percent to roughly 8 percent, which still produces $498 per share — a figure he calls the margin of safety. In a later video, after Adobe's acquisition of Figma, he separates the acquisition's cost from the core business's value: his pre-acquisition DCF still values Adobe at $488 per share against a roughly $287 price, and in an "ultra-conservative" scenario where he assumes Figma is worth literally zero and subtracts the full $20 billion purchase price (about $42 per share) from that $488 base, the result is still $446 — comfortably above the market price. He justifies the Figma purchase price itself with a separate market-sizing exercise: at a conservative 25 percent share of a $16.5 billion projected UX/UI design market, Figma would generate about $4.12 billion in sales within three years, meaning Adobe effectively paid about 5 times that figure; at a 50 percent share he judges more realistic, the multiple falls to about 2.5 times.

5.7 NIO: the same model, a different verdict

Not every company he runs through this process comes out undervalued, and NIO is his clearest disclosed case of the model returning a clear "no." Taking a 62 percent five-year growth rate from Simply Wall Street — which he calls "really optimistic" but uses anyway, in keeping with his stated practice of sourcing growth from third parties rather than his own view — his resulting intrinsic value comes to $12.49 per share against a $60.31 market price, a 382 percent premium. In a separate video he prices NIO at $45 against an intrinsic value of $29, calling it a momentum stock roughly 30–40 percent overvalued that he "would not dare invest in," while explicitly granting that market sentiment can still push the price higher regardless of what his model says. NIO is useful in this book precisely because it shows the DCF discipline is not merely decorative: when the same three-stage-growth, cost-of-equity-discounting, twenty-year model that generates a "buy" signal for Amazon or Alphabet is applied to NIO, it generates a clear pass, and Khoo reports that outcome rather than reaching for a different method that would flatter the stock.

5.8 The Disney turnaround variant

Disney is Khoo's fullest worked example of what this book calls the turnaround variant of his valuation method — a modified version of the same DCF machinery, applied to a company he judges to be a fundamentally sound business passing through temporary, self-inflicted trouble rather than permanent decline. He defines a turnaround stock generally as a good, fundamentally strong company whose share price has been beaten down by bad news, where the central question is whether that bad news is temporary and will eventually be resolved. For Disney specifically, he notes its free cash flow peaked at about $9.8 billion in 2018 before declining through its streaming-service transition, and treats that earlier peak as the level he wants the business to climb back toward as evidence the turnaround is real.

The variant departs from his standard method in one specific input. Rather than using trailing-twelve-month free cash flow — his default input, described in section 5.1 — he says that to value a turnaround business he annualizes only the two most recent quarters of free cash flow, doubling their sum, because the older quarters in a trailing-twelve-month figure still reflect the pre-turnaround, depressed run rate and would understate where the business now stands. Everything else follows the same shape as the rest of this chapter: he applies an 18.33 percent growth rate for the next ten years, averaged from Capital IQ, Finviz, Zacks, and GuruFocus and explicitly elevated above what he'd use for a stable compounder because this is a turnaround case, tapering to a 4 percent terminal rate, discounted at 6.6 percent based on the stock's 1.29 beta. That combination produces an intrinsic value of about $152 per share against an $83 share price at the time — roughly 45 percent undervalued. As a check on how much the elevated turnaround growth rate itself was doing, he reruns the same model with his usual, less generous 15-percent-capped growth assumption instead, and still gets an intrinsic value of about $135 per share — still a solid margin of safety at the $83 price either way.

The cards in this archive state the annualized-FCF input and the elevated growth rate explicitly for the turnaround variant; they do not state a distinct position-sizing rule tied specifically to turnaround investments as opposed to his standard compounders. That gap is recorded honestly in the closing section of this chapter rather than filled in.

Editor's note — why a turnaround case gets a different cash-flow input

A trailing-twelve-month figure blends four quarters together, which is a reasonable way to smooth out normal seasonal noise in a stable business. In a genuine turnaround, though, the whole point is that the business changed materially partway through that twelve-month window — a new strategy took hold, a cost structure was cut, a bad product line was discontinued. Blending the pre-change and post-change quarters together produces a number that is not a fair description of either period; it understates the company's current run rate by diluting recent improvement with older, worse results. Annualizing only the most recent one or two quarters is a standard analyst technique for exactly this situation — sometimes called a "run-rate" annualization — and Khoo's use of it here is consistent with that convention, though he doesn't use that specific term himself.

What he doesn't say about this

Khoo never states, in any card in this archive, a single canonical formula for his risk-free rate or market-risk-premium inputs beyond "ten-year averages" — the exact numbers he plugs into the capital asset pricing model are not given directly, only the resulting discount rate for specific companies. He does not explain, across the cards gathered here, exactly how he decides which of his five valuation methods (discounted free cash flow, discounted net income, price-to-book, sum-of-the-parts, or a market-sizing approach like the one used for Figma) applies to a borderline company beyond the general consistency test described in section 5.4 — the choice for clear cases like a bank or an insurer is stated plainly, but no card describes his reasoning for a company that sits between categories. He states a distinct position-sizing convention for turnaround investments is plausible given how differently he frames the risk of a turnaround thesis compared to an established compounder, but no card in this archive states such a rule explicitly for Disney or any other turnaround holding, so this book does not attribute one to him. Nor does any card disclose a verified, audited track record of how his DCF-derived "undervalued" calls have performed on average over time — the individual outcomes reported in this book (Amazon, Alphabet, GameStop's later price, and others) are the specific, disclosed cases that exist in the archive, not a systematic accounting.

Chapter summary

  • Intrinsic value, in Khoo's method, is the present value of a company's future cash flow (or net income, for less cash-flow-consistent companies), discounted back at a rate built from the risk-free rate plus beta times the market risk premium.
  • Growth is applied in three stages — a near-term rate averaged from multiple third-party analyst sources, a reduced middle-stage rate, and a terminal rate pegged to GDP growth plus about one percent — rather than a single flat assumption for the whole projection.
  • He projects cash flow for exactly twenty years and stops, deliberately omitting the terminal-value-to-perpetuity that most professional DCF models use, and treats anything beyond year twenty as an unclaimed bonus.
  • On Amazon he runs two independent methods side by side — discounted net income ($191/share) and sum-of-the-parts by business segment ($159/share) — and treats the lower, more conservative figure as the more trustworthy one rather than averaging them.
  • He always pairs a base-case valuation with a pessimistic case built by cutting the growth assumption, and calls the resulting lower figure the margin of safety, as demonstrated on Adobe ($613 base versus $498 pessimistic).
  • The same model produces both buy signals (Amazon, Alphabet, Adobe) and clear no-signals (NIO, priced 30-380 percent above his intrinsic value depending on the video), showing the discipline is applied consistently rather than used only to justify stocks he already likes.
  • For turnaround situations like Disney, he modifies one input — annualizing only the two most recent quarters of free cash flow instead of using a trailing-twelve-month figure — because older quarters still reflect the pre-turnaround run rate.
  • His buying rule, stated repeatedly, is to purchase only below his calculated intrinsic value, with the size of the discount he demands rising for positions he already holds heavily or for businesses he judges harder to predict.

Try this

Khoo's own instruction, demonstrated on Adobe, is to build two versions of any valuation before acting on it: a base case using the growth rate you actually believe (or the analyst consensus you're using), and a pessimistic case built by materially cutting that growth assumption — he halves it in his own example — and to treat the resulting lower number, not the base case, as your real margin-of-safety threshold before buying.

Chapter 6

Portfolio Construction

In this chapter: how a position that has passed the screen and the valuation test in Chapters 4 and 5 actually gets bought — in what size, in how many pieces, alongside what else, and what gets deliberately left out.

6.1 Why he never buys all at once

Once a company clears his quality screen and looks cheap enough to buy, Khoo treats the purchase itself as a separate decision with its own discipline. He states the rule plainly and repeats it, in close to the same words, across years of videos: as an investor you never go all in at one time. Given a large lump sum to invest, he says the same rule applies regardless of how much conviction he has in the pick — you always buy in stages over a period of time rather than all at once.

The reasoning he gives is consistent: nobody, himself included, can pick the exact bottom of a decline, so wise investors buy their best companies in stages and average into the position rather than betting the whole allocation on one entry price. Averaging in this way means that even if a sell-off continues after the first buy, the position's average cost can still land above the eventual low — or, if the price never falls further, the investor has already captured most of the position at a reasonable level. He frames the alternative — one full-size purchase at a single price — as a bet on a level he has no way of confirming in advance.

Editor's note — averaging-in versus a limit order at one price

The mechanical difference between "buy in stages" and "buy once" is that the first method trades certainty of entry price for reduced regret risk. A single lump-sum buy either gets the best price available in the whole episode or one that looks bad in hindsight; a staged buy guarantees an average somewhere in the middle of whatever range the price travels through while the tranches are being filled. It is the same logic behind a dollar-cost-averaging schedule (discussed in 6.6), applied here to a single stock over a shorter, price-triggered timeline rather than a fixed calendar one.

6.2 The tranche procedure, in his own numbers

Where he gives specifics, the procedure has a recognizable shape across many separate examples: split the intended position into three or four pieces, and buy each piece at a pre-identified price level rather than on a calendar schedule.

The trigger he most often names is a percentage decline from a recent high. Working through a hypothetical during a market sell-off, he says of a hundred dollars he intends to invest, he might put in twenty-five now — roughly a quarter — and buy more if the price falls to a bigger discount. In a later video he generalizes this into an explicit rule: add another tranche of investment every time price drops a further 10 percent from the high, rather than ever going all in. Elsewhere he states the same quarter-sized rule of thumb directly: a further quarter position on each additional 10 percent decline, continuing until the full position is built.

Alongside the percentage-decline trigger, he also builds tranches around support levels he marks on the chart in advance. Working a real example on Facebook during its 2018 crash, he describes putting in about a third of an intended $10,000 allocation immediately, with the next tranche triggered if price breaks to the next support level, and the final third at the level after that. He gives the same pattern for other names: for Bank of America he names three specific price levels — $27.70, then $26 to $27, then $23 — as the points at which each of three or four tranches would be bought; for Meta he names four levels ($541, $496, $453, $414) and states the rule behind them directly — he always sets three to four buy levels because he never buys a full position at once, always averaging in. For an intended 100-share position he describes buying half at the first support level and the other half at the next, and in a separate video describes a quarter-position-at-a-time version of the same idea. He also gives a much finer-grained version of the rule in one video: buying toward a target allocation roughly 1 percent of the portfolio at a time — a smaller increment than the quarter- or third-based examples elsewhere, and the book reports both scales rather than picking one.

He extends the same logic to names he trades in size over years rather than weeks: he says he builds positions in companies like Amazon, Alphabet, and Microsoft gradually, adding tranches when the stock is undervalued and technically retraces to a support level, rather than buying all the shares at once. He does treat the rule as conditional on the trend still being intact, not something to apply blindly into a collapsing chart: with Alibaba during its 2022 delisting scare, he says he would normally add at a support level, but with every support broken he declined to add during the downtrend, because a broken trend "keeps going lower" — a real exception to the averaging-down instinct, stated in his own words.

Editor's note — a real case where staged buying beat a single entry

He backs the general claim with one historical test rather than only anecdote: applying the 10-percent-drop averaging rule through the 1973–74 bear market, he shows the resulting average cost would have broken even about seven months after the market's eventual bottom, versus roughly seven years for an investor who had put the whole sum in at a single point before the decline. He also points to Berkshire Hathaway's purchase of Coca-Cola shares, which took Buffett's firm seven years to complete, as an illustration that building a serious position is "a marathon rather than a single lump-sum buy". Both examples are about a specific instrument over a specific historical window, not a claim about every stock in every period — a distinction worth keeping, since a strategy that helped in a multi-year secular bear market does not automatically transfer to a sharp V-shaped one.

6.3 Position caps and the minimum-holdings rule

Separately from how a position is built, Khoo states rules about how large any one position, and any one country's exposure, is allowed to become.

On single-stock size, his most frequently repeated figure is a 5 percent ceiling: he says his position-sizing rule is to never allocate more than 5 percent of his portfolio to any single company, no matter how great the business. He demonstrates the rule as a live decision rather than only a stated number — declining to add to Alibaba despite liking its chart because the position was already at what he called his maximum allocation, given at 5 percent in one telling and about 6 percent in another, closer to the same period. Elsewhere he gives a looser version of the same cap tied to portfolio size rather than a single fixed number: capping position size so that no stock exceeds roughly 10 percent of a portfolio built around ten holdings. The book reports these as the range he has actually stated — 5 to 10 percent depending on the video — rather than resolving it to one figure he never picked himself.

On the number of positions held at all, he is more consistent: hold a minimum of eight to ten stocks, he says in several videos, so that no single company can sink the whole portfolio. In one of these he ties the minimum directly to the per-position cap: eight to ten stocks so that each one accounts for at most about 10 percent of the portfolio. He also gives an upper bound on the same range — not more than twenty or twenty-five names, because beyond that it becomes too hard to keep track of each one, though a separate video puts his own comfortable ceiling at around forty. Describing his own approach by name, he calls it "concentrated diversification" — a minimum of ten stocks, allowing up to twenty, thirty, or forty, with ten to thirty as "good enough" given the time it takes to actually monitor each holding. In the same video he contrasts this with two reference points he attributes to other investors: Warren Buffett running thirty to forty stocks ("concentrated diversification" by his description), and Charlie Munger running as few as five ("super concentrated").

On country and sector concentration, he states a 20 percent ceiling on any single emerging market — China, India, or otherwise — regardless of how attractive it looks. On sector correlation within a portfolio of concurrent positions, he gives a distinct rule aimed at trading rather than long-term holdings: no more than two positions in the same sector or industry at once, so that positions are not effectively duplicating the same bet.

Editor's note — a stated cap is a ceiling, not a target

Notice the caps above describe upper limits, not recommended sizes. A 5 percent maximum on a single stock does not mean every position should be built to 5 percent; it means a position is not added to further once it reaches that level, as he demonstrates with the Alibaba example above. The distinction matters for a reader trying to reverse-engineer a target allocation from a stated maximum — the two are not the same number, and he does not give one that pins down the other precisely.

6.4 The recurring sector-avoidance pattern

Across many separate videos, spanning several years, Khoo names the same small set of sectors he says he does not hold as long-term investments — a pattern consistent enough, and repeated across enough unrelated occasions, that it functions as a standing rule even though he rarely frames it that way in any single video.

Energy is the sector named most often. He says he does not really buy energy stocks as long-term investments because he finds the sector unpredictable and highly competitive, and in another video states he has zero exposure to energy for the same reason, along with real estate, because both have historically underperformed the S&P 500 given low profit margins, high cyclicality, and unpredictable cash flows. He is careful, in multiple tellings, to draw a line between investing and trading here: he does not buy energy names for the long-term book, but says he may trade them occasionally with options as a separate, shorter-dated bet — a hedge worth preserving, since it means the avoidance rule is scoped to the no-stop-loss investing book, not to his trading activity as a whole.

Alongside energy, he names a consistent set of other categories he avoids as investments: industrials, utilities, materials and commodity stocks, and separately, auto companies — both electric and legacy manufacturers — along with airlines and shipping. His stated reasoning for the group is the same each time: these are cyclical businesses whose revenue and profits swing heavily with the broader economy, which he contrasts with the more predictable, moat-protected businesses his Chapter 4 screen is built to find. He does acknowledge, in the same breath, that other investors profit from cyclical sectors — this is presented as his own avoidance rule, not a claim that the sectors are universally a bad trade.

Small-cap stocks get the same treatment for a related but distinct reason — risk rather than cyclicality. He says he does not buy small-cap companies because he considers them too risky, preferring large, dominant businesses he describes as "impenetrable," bought at a discount. He does make one disclosed exception to the individual-name avoidance: rather than picking a small-cap stock, he buys the small-cap growth ETF VBK to take a shorter-to-medium-term position on the category as a whole catching up, without picking a single company within it. In a later portfolio disclosure, that same VBK position appears as part of what he describes as roughly 46 percent of his book held outside mega-cap AI and technology names — evidence that the sector-avoidance pattern applies to individual stock selection within these categories, not to all exposure to them.

Editor's note — a cyclical sector, illustrated

Airlines are a clean real-world case of the pattern Khoo describes. An airline's costs are dominated by fuel and labor, both of which move against the business in a strong economy (fuel demand rises, labor gets scarcer), while its revenue is dominated by discretionary travel spending, which collapses first in a downturn. The result is a business whose margins swing from thin profit to outright loss across a single economic cycle largely outside management's control — the mechanical reason a "moat" screen like the one in Chapter 4 tends to exclude the sector before cyclicality is even named as a separate criterion.

6.5 Dollar-cost averaging as the passive alternative

Distinct from tranche-buying a single stock at support levels, Khoo describes dollar-cost averaging (DCA) as a separate, calendar-based method aimed mainly at index and ETF positions rather than individual companies. He defines it consistently: invest a fixed dollar amount at regular intervals — his worked examples include $2,000 every six months, $5,000 every year, or $10,000 every year — regardless of the price on the day. He stresses that for the method to work, the dollar amount has to stay fixed and consistent once chosen; changing it partway defeats the purpose. The mechanical benefit he cites is that a fixed sum buys fewer shares when the price is high and more shares when it is low, so the resulting average cost lands somewhere in the middle of the range — never the top, but also never the bottom.

He is explicit about where he thinks the method belongs and where it does not: DCA should be used only on an index or ETF he is confident will keep rising over the long run, never on an individual company, because an individual company can go out of business entirely while an index cannot. He describes his own long-term portfolio as run this way — selecting companies he holds like Warren Buffett and adding to them every few months — but adds a candid qualifier about his own preference: dollar-cost averaging is not his favorite strategy, he says; it is simply the simpler one, and what he personally does day to day is closer to trend-following.

He also links the method to timing. Asked when the best time is to start buying an index ETF, he says a person can buy at any time because the index tends to rise eventually, though the best entries come when the market is temporarily down. Separately he states his general operating rule for the long-term book as staying invested at all times, since no one can know in advance which year will be strongly up or down, treating temporary drops as buying opportunities rather than realized losses provided the underlying holding is not sold.

6.6 Rebalancing, and his own reservation about it

For a fixed multi-asset allocation, Khoo describes an annual rebalancing routine. Working a specific example, he lays out a passive portfolio split 30 percent US stocks (SPY), 40 percent long-term bonds (TLT), 15 percent intermediate bonds (IEI), 8 percent gold (GLD), and 8 percent commodities (DBA), to be rebalanced once a year back to those original target percentages. He describes the mechanics plainly: rebalancing means selling whatever has grown to be overweight and buying whatever has fallen to be underweight, restoring each holding to its target share. He works the arithmetic through a concrete case: after a year of growth to $137,000, US stocks have drifted from a 24 percent target to 28 percent of the account, so roughly $4,300 of SPY gets sold and the proceeds moved into TLT to bring stocks back down to target.

He is candid about the trade-off passive rebalancing produces. It assures a portfolio will probably never lose very much in any single scenario, he says, but by the same mechanism it also assures the portfolio will never make a lot, because a strong-performing asset is continually trimmed to fund a weaker one. He offers, in the same video, an active alternative he says he prefers for himself: trend-follow each asset in the multi-asset mix separately — long the ones in an uptrend, short the ones in a downtrend — with no rebalancing back to fixed weights at all.

That preference for trend over mechanical rebalancing resurfaces later, more pointedly, in a video where he criticizes the standard fund-manager version of the practice directly: selling winners to buy underperformers, in his view, causes underperformance rather than improving it — a sharper statement than his earlier, more neutral description of the trade-off, and the book notes the shift in tone rather than treating the two statements as saying exactly the same thing.

What he doesn't say about this

Khoo does not reconcile the different single-stock caps he has stated across videos — 5 percent in several tellings, roughly 6 percent in a live example, and up to 10 percent in a portfolio built around ten holdings — into one number he says he actually follows today. He also does not state a single rule for how the tranche triggers relate to each other: some examples use a fixed percentage-decline trigger (10 percent from the high), others use chart-based support levels identified in advance, and he never states in any card in this archive which of the two he would use when they disagree — for instance, when a marked support level sits at a 15 percent decline rather than a clean 10 percent one. He does not give a single stated cap for total exposure to the sectors he avoids as a group (energy, industrials, utilities, materials, autos) the way he does for a single emerging market at 20 percent — only that he holds close to none of them individually. Nor does he address how the 8-to-10-stock minimum and the "up to forty" ceiling interact with the 5-to-10-percent single-position cap: a forty-stock portfolio with an average position near 2 or 3 percent sits well under any of his stated maximums, but he never states whether that many names is still consistent with the "concentrated diversification" he says he personally prefers.

Chapter summary

  • He states, across many separate videos and years, that he never buys a full position all at once — he buys in stages, and repeats this as a general rule regardless of conviction in the pick.
  • His tranche triggers take two forms he does not fully reconcile: a fixed percentage-decline rule (commonly a further 10 percent drop per tranche), and pre-marked chart support levels, illustrated with real price levels for Bank of America and Meta.
  • He backs the staged-buying approach with one historical test — the 10-percent-drop rule through the 1973–74 crash broke even in about seven months versus roughly seven years for a single lump-sum entry — and one real-world case, Berkshire's seven-year build of its Coca-Cola position.
  • His stated single-position cap ranges from 5 to 10 percent of the portfolio depending on the video, demonstrated live when he declined to add further to an already-maxed Alibaba position.
  • He consistently states a minimum of eight to ten holdings to avoid any one company sinking the portfolio, with stated upper bounds ranging from twenty-five to forty depending on the telling.
  • Across many videos over several years he states he avoids energy, industrials, utilities, materials, autos, and small-caps as long-term investment categories — while explicitly carving out occasional options-based trades in some of these same sectors as a separate activity from his investing book.
  • He defines dollar-cost averaging as fixed, regular purchases into an index or ETF he trusts to rise long-term, and states this method should never be applied to a single company that could go out of business — while noting it is not his personally preferred strategy, only the simpler one.
  • For a fixed multi-asset mix he describes annual rebalancing back to target percentages, worked through real arithmetic, but states a preference for trend-following each asset separately instead, and later criticizes standard fund-manager rebalancing as, in his view, a cause of underperformance.

Try this

He gives a concrete, repeatable version of the tranche exercise: take a company you'd want to own, and rather than picking one entry price, set three or four buy levels in advance — for example, spaced at each further 10 percent decline from the current price, or at the next few chart support levels below it — before deciding how much to commit to each one.

Chapter 7

Trade Management for Investors: When (Not) to Sell

In this chapter: why the investing book has no stop loss and never gets one, what actually does trigger an exit when price alone doesn't, and the honest record of Khoo getting this wrong in both directions — holding a fraud-plagued healthcare giant longer than he now seems fully at peace with, and selling Facebook and Nvidia far too early, at a quantified cost he states himself.

7.1 The rule restated: price falling is not a reason to sell

Chapter 2 established the spine of the two-book architecture: no stop loss on the investing book, mandatory stop loss on the trading book. This chapter returns to that rule from the exit side specifically — not whether a stop loss exists, but what does the work a stop loss would otherwise do, once price alone is taken off the table as a sell signal.

He states the principle at its most direct: you sell an investment not based on what happens to the price, but only when the fundamentals deteriorate and the reasons you bought are no longer valid. He restates the same idea as a trigger rather than a philosophy — the sell trigger for an investment is the moment the company fails his criteria, when the fundamentals change, and that is the point you get out. Asked directly whether a falling price should worry a long-term holder, he answers by relocating the question entirely: the decision should turn on whether the underlying business is still growing, still making money, and still has a long runway — not on short-term price action.

He names the psychological trap on the other side of this rule too. Most amateur investors never sell a losing position because they fear locking in the loss, telling themselves it's "only a paper loss" until the day they actually sell — which he calls deluding themselves. The no-stop-loss rule is not a license for that kind of denial. It's a redirection: instead of monitoring price, monitor the business. Chapter 2's mirror-image rule — average down as price falls, provided the position already cleared the quality-and-valuation screen — is the buying half of this same logic; this chapter is about the selling half.

Editor's note — why "no stop loss" still needs an exit rule

A stop loss is a mechanical, price-triggered exit that requires no ongoing judgment once it's set. Removing it doesn't remove the need for an exit rule — it moves the trigger from a number on a chart to a judgment about the business, which is a harder thing to monitor and a much easier thing to rationalize away. This is the central tension this chapter documents: Khoo's stated rule is judgment-based and, by his own later admissions, judgment is exactly where he has gotten it wrong — both by waiting too long to act and by acting too soon.

7.2 What actually triggers a sale

Across several videos he gives a more granular account of what counts as a legitimate reason to sell, and it's narrower than "the stock went down." Step three of his own investing framework names three conditions: the company's fundamentals deteriorate, it is no longer a good business, or the price becomes overvalued and turns into a downtrend. He adds the qualifier that a good company genuinely can become a lousy one, and an uptrend genuinely can turn into a bear-market downtrend — the rule isn't static optimism, it allows for real deterioration.

He gives a fuller list elsewhere, describing three reasons to sell in order. Needing the cash — to spend on something like a house or a car, or to redeploy into a more undervalued opportunity — is one legitimate reason, independent of the business's health. Extreme overvaluation is the third: he defines "extreme" as the stock trading more than 100 percent above his calculated intrinsic value, at which point he slowly takes profits rather than exiting all at once. Elsewhere he gives a tighter number for the same idea — he likes to sell at least 20 to 30 percent above intrinsic value so he feels he's getting a good deal — and a looser one, describing the threshold as "egregious" overvaluation, roughly double intrinsic value, citing his own partial exit from Nvidia at that point while still holding about 40 percent of the original position and letting it run. The numbers move between videos — 20-30 percent, 100 percent, "roughly double" — which the book reports as the range he's actually stated rather than collapsing into one figure.

He is specific about what does not meet this bar. Most temporary bad news — product recalls, lawsuits, PR scandals — does not change his holding, because none of it touches the durable economics of the business. The one exception he draws a hard line around is accounting or fraud scandals, which he treats as an immediate sell-or-avoid signal, citing Super Micro Computer as a live example and Enron and WorldCom as historical cases where accounting fraud took a company to zero. A management execution mistake — he classifies Estée Lauder's 60 percent decline this way, tracing it to COVID disruption in Asia travel retail plus an inventory over-order — is treated as a hold rather than a sell, because he judges it a fixable operating error rather than structural moat deterioration. The distinction he's drawing, across all of these, is between noise that doesn't touch the moat and damage that does.

Editor's note — the fraud carve-out and why it's structurally different

The reason a fraud scandal gets treated differently from ordinary bad news is that fraud attacks the reliability of the financial statements themselves — the inputs every valuation in this book depends on. A disappointing quarter or a lawsuit changes the numbers you're valuing; a fraud allegation calls into question whether the numbers were ever real. Enron's 2001 collapse is the textbook case: a company reporting healthy earnings turned out to be using off-balance-sheet entities to hide billions in debt, and once the fraud surfaced the stock went from about $90 to worthless in under a year, because there was no longer a trustworthy set of financials to value at all. That's the category Khoo is drawing a hard line around, distinct from the fixable, survivable bad news he otherwise holds through.

Two of his stated exit rules are worth holding side by side because they sit at different distances from certainty. When he does decide to exit, he avoids selling into the low — nothing falls in a straight line, so he waits for a relief rally toward a resistance level before getting out, whether he's exiting a rejected business or reducing a bear-market position. And when trimming a winner near a suspected top, his exit is explicitly discretionary and gradual: he never sells an entire position at once, because no one can reliably call the top — the same scaling-out logic Chapter 10 documents on the trading side, applied here to an investment that has run further than expected.

7.3 The honest override: his self-named biggest mistake

Adam Khoo has, on camera, named his own single biggest recurring investing mistake, and it is the opposite of holding too long — it's exiting good companies too early and trying to time a re-entry. Asked directly about it, he says his biggest mistake over the years has been jumping in and out of great companies, and that with a genuine high-growth monopoly business the right approach is to never sell at all, holding for decades — he names Facebook, Amazon, Alphabet, and Microsoft as the class of business this applies to. He repeats the same self-assessment in a separate video: he admits he used to sell great stocks — Apple, Facebook, Amazon, Nvidia — and try to time the re-entry, and now believes he would be far richer had he simply held them for the last ten to fifteen years without touching them.

He backs the admission with an argument for why selling winners is structurally costly beyond the specific stocks named. Citing an "80/20" pattern — roughly 20 percent of a portfolio's holdings generating about 80 percent of its total gains — he uses it to argue directly against selling winners early, since a portfolio's long-run return depends disproportionately on not being out of the handful of positions that end up doing the compounding.

The two disclosed cases below are where he puts numbers on that mistake.

7.4 The Facebook/Meta case: sold too early, quantified

Khoo gives one of the more specific self-disclosed cost figures in the whole corpus describing his own history with Meta. He recounts buying Facebook very early after its post-IPO price drop, then selling too early — he calls himself "dumb enough to sell it very early" — and having to buy back in at much higher prices afterward. He gives the actual numbers: he first bought at $19, sold around $40-50 thinking he'd banked a 100 percent profit, then rebought over the following years at $100, $150, $200, and $300. He states that he now holds over $650,000 in Meta as a result of the rebuys. His own stated conclusion from the episode is direct: holding for the long run as an investor produces bigger returns than getting in and out for short-term profit as a speculator, and he draws that lesson explicitly from his own Facebook/Meta history rather than as an abstract rule.

This case pairs directly with his general long-term-holding exit rule, stated in the same video: for an investor who believes in the company, the rule is to hold and track intrinsic value, only considering an exit once price is more than 100 percent above intrinsic value — not to exit on price action alone. He contrasts that explicitly with a separate rule he gives for a purely speculative post-IPO trade, where the exit is mechanical — sell half once price closes below the 20-day EMA, sell the rest once the 20 crosses below the 40 — a rule he reserves for speculation, not for a position he actually believes in as a business. Read against the Meta history, the 100-percent-above-intrinsic-value threshold looks like the rule he wishes he'd been running in 2012, rather than the "up 100 percent, take the win" instinct he says he actually acted on.

7.5 The Nvidia case: two disclosed round trips, one self-description as "a bloody idiot"

The Nvidia history is documented across at least two separate videos, both self-disclosed, and both describe the same shape of mistake: selling a position he judged overvalued, only to buy it back later at a materially higher price.

The first account: he first bought Nvidia in November 2022 at $15 (split- adjusted), sold it at $48, then bought back in around $530-550 — a round trip he narrates by calling himself "a bloody idiot" for selling and having to re-enter much higher, noting the sequence left his resulting average cost basis at about $60 instead of the $15 he originally paid. A second, separately dated account gives different specific numbers for what appears to be the same general pattern: he describes first buying near $150 in late 2022, selling all shares at $380 against a $200 intrinsic-value estimate at the time, then buying all shares back at an average of about $490 once he had revalued intrinsic value near $400. The exact prices differ between the two tellings — a reminder, in line with the book's discipline elsewhere, that self-reported trade histories told across different videos are not always internally reconciled to the dollar — but the shape of the mistake he's describing is consistent across both: selling a great business into strength, and paying a much higher price to get back in once conviction returned.

He gives his own justification for why buying back at a higher price than he sold isn't itself a contradiction: intrinsic value is not a fixed number, it rises as a growing business keeps growing and falls as a deteriorating one deteriorates, so a past sale price shouldn't anchor a present buy decision. That justification defends the re-entry as rational. It does not, in his own framing, defend the original exit — which is the half of the round trip he separately calls being "a bloody idiot" about.

Editor's note — what "selling on valuation" costs when growth compounds fast

The mechanism behind both round trips is the same one behind the Facebook case: for a business whose earnings are compounding quickly, "overvalued relative to today's intrinsic value" and "still cheap relative to where intrinsic value will be in two years" can both be true at once. Selling on the first read and buying back on the second means paying the round-trip transaction cost of being right twice in a row about direction, while giving up the compounding that would have happened between the two trades for free. It's the same trade-off value investors describe when discussing why Charlie Munger argued for extreme portfolio concentration and long holding periods in fast-compounding businesses — the tax and friction cost of trading in and out of a winner is real, even when each individual valuation call turns out to be directionally correct.

7.6 The UnitedHealth case: a long, escalating hold, and an eventual exit he frames as discomfort rather than valuation

UnitedHealth is the clearest documented case in the archive of a multi-year, high-conviction hold running through a deep and prolonged drawdown — and it is worth tracing in sequence, because the sequence itself is the finding.

Khoo held and repeatedly added to UnitedHealth for years, describing it in 2019 as one of several holdings priced below his calculated intrinsic value that he held through that year's bearish consensus, and adding to it again in 2021 as short-term issues left an otherwise great company undervalued. Through 2023 and 2024 he continued restating rising intrinsic-value estimates against the market price — $470, then $548 against a $531 price he called slightly undervalued and disclosed as a large position he was actively adding to. He named it repeatedly among his preferred holdings for its scale-driven, network-effect moat.

Then the stock fell hard — down roughly 60 percent from its highs by mid-2025 — on a mix of rising medical-utilization costs and, more seriously, fraud allegations. Through the bulk of that drawdown his response followed the investing-book playbook exactly as stated in earlier chapters: he continued to frame the decline as within historical range, pointing to prior UnitedHealth drawdowns of 56, 60, and 76 percent that each fully recovered, and used that precedent to argue the then-current drop was a normal correction rather than a crisis. He kept revaluing it, arriving at intrinsic value estimates in the $412-746 range across several methodologies, and his team deliberately chose a conservative $570 figure against a market price of $380. He disclosed actively buying more of it through this stretch, calling it a high-quality company undervalued because of short-term bad news, while explicitly saying he could not predict exactly when it would turn. He also disclosed, in the same period, that his own analyst team downgraded UnitedHealth's moat rating — overriding an AI tool's 9-out-of-10 "wide moat" score to classify it as narrow-moat instead, because government regulation restricts the premiums it can charge.

The sequence ends with an exit, and he narrates the reasoning for it plainly rather than folding it into the valuation story. He discloses selling all of his UnitedHealth shares for only a slight profit after the fraud allegations surfaced, stating that he still believes the company is "too big to fail" and will likely recover — but that he personally isn't comfortable holding a business with what he characterizes as a culture of alleged systemic fraud. He immediately reinvested the proceeds into Novo Nordisk, HCA Healthcare, Pool Corporation, and ASML.

Read against Section 7.2's stated sell rules, the UnitedHealth exit is a real-world instance of the fraud carve-out set out in Section 7.2 — not a valuation call — and he says so directly: he isn't confident the alleged fraud disqualifies the company from ever recovering, only that he isn't personally willing to hold through it. What the book flags honestly is the gap between the sell and the fraud allegations surfacing: the record shows him continuing to add to the position, restate rising intrinsic-value estimates, and cite historical drawdown precedent for a considerable stretch after the fraud allegations and the moat downgrade were both already public and already reflected in his own videos, before eventually exiting for only a slight profit on a stock that had, by his own account, fallen roughly 60 percent from its highs. He never states in any video gathered here that he held UnitedHealth too long, and the book does not put that judgment in his mouth — but the documented sequence is one where the moat downgrade and the fraud allegations both preceded the eventual exit by enough real time, and enough continued buying, that a reader comparing this case to the Facebook and Nvidia cases in Sections 7.4-7.5 can reasonably see it as the mirror-image mistake: where those two show selling too fast, UnitedHealth shows a hold that, by the numbers he himself reports, gave back a large share of its gains before he acted.

7.7 Why drawdown alone is not treated as an exit signal

The UnitedHealth case only makes sense against the backdrop of how large and how ordinary Khoo treats a drawdown to be for even a well-chosen holding — material he laid out at length in a video made specifically to explain his own portfolio's underperformance.

He discloses that his portfolio was down 11.05 percent over the first three months of the year in question, a slightly bigger drawdown than the S&P 500's 9 percent over the same stretch, attributing the gap to his heavier weighting in communication-services and consumer-discretionary names hit by AI-disruption fears, versus an index propped up by energy, materials, utilities, and consumer-defensive sectors he generally avoids. Zoomed out, he reports his portfolio gained 267 percent over the prior seven years against 175 percent for the S&P 500 — but with drawdowns of 36 percent in the 2020 COVID crash, 26 percent in the 2022 rate-hike bear market, and 19 percent in the 2025 tariff sell-off along the way.

He backs the point that large drawdowns are a normal feature of even the best long-run records, not a sign of error, with a set of cited outside statistics rather than only his own numbers. He cites Warren Buffett's Berkshire Hathaway compounding roughly 4,000 percent since the 1990s against the S&P 500's 1,200 percent, while enduring drawdowns of 51 percent in the late 1990s and 54 percent during the financial crisis. He cites Peter Lynch's 13-year run managing the Magellan Fund — a 604 percent gain against the S&P's 223 percent, a 29 percent compound annual growth rate — while enduring yearly drawdowns of 56, 27, 42, and 32 percent, each larger than the S&P's decline in the same period. He pairs that record with a statistic about the fund's own investors rather than the fund itself: more than half of retail investors in the Magellan Fund lost money over that same 13-year run, despite it being one of the best-performing funds of all time, because most bought in after a rally and sold in panic after the subsequent drawdown, repeating the cycle.

He cites two further outside sources to generalize the point beyond individual examples. Citing a Morgan Stanley research note by Michael Mauboussin and Callahan, he states the median maximum drawdown across 6,500 US stocks from 1985 to 2024 was 85 percent — and that even the six biggest wealth-creating companies over that period (Apple, Microsoft, Nvidia, Alphabet, Amazon, and Exxon Mobil) had an average maximum drawdown of 80 percent. He also cites Wes Gray's article "Even God Would Get Fired," which found that even a portfolio built with perfect hindsight foresight of the five best-performing stocks over the following five years would still pass through a 76 percent drawdown and five separate 30 percent drawdowns on its way to beating the market. His stated conclusion from all of this material is explicit: a drawdown is not evidence you're wrong or doing anything badly — it is a structural feature of owning equities at all, one that happens even to investors who own exactly the right companies.

He gives one further, single-stock illustration of the same point with Amazon, citing its 95 percent drop from 1999 to 2001, followed by separate drawdowns of 56 percent in 2022 and 33 percent twice in 2024 and 2025, all while the stock still compounded to a roughly 258,000 percent total gain against the S&P 500's much smaller return over the same span.

Editor's note — reading the drawdown-tolerance material against the UnitedHealth case, not as a contradiction of it

These two pieces of material — "drawdowns this large are normal and no reason to sell" and "he eventually sold UnitedHealth after a roughly 60-percent drawdown" — are not presented anywhere in the source material as in tension, and the book does not force them into one. His stated distinction, consistent across both, is that price alone (a drawdown, however large) is not a sell signal, but a change in the underlying business — and he explicitly places an alleged fraud culture in that second category, separate from ordinary price decline. Whether the moat downgrade and fraud allegations should, by his own stated rules, have triggered an earlier exit than the one he actually made is a judgment this book leaves to the reader; the material simply documents the dates in sequence so that judgment can be made.

What he doesn't say about this

He never states, anywhere in the material gathered for this chapter, a maximum number of years or a maximum percentage decline he'll tolerate before a "hold through the drawdown" position gets automatically reassessed — the UnitedHealth sequence shows him continuing to add and revalue for an extended stretch after both the moat downgrade and the fraud allegations were already public, without ever stating a rule for how long that reassessment window should be. He does not reconcile the different overvaluation thresholds he's given for extreme overvaluation — 20-30 percent above intrinsic value in one video, 100 percent in another, "roughly double" in a third — into one figure, and no video in the archive puts the three side by side. He never revisits the Facebook or Nvidia sales to ask whether a monitoring rule could have caught the mistake earlier, framing both instead as lessons learned in hindsight rather than process failures he's since patched with a new procedure. And while he discloses his own year-to-date and multi-year portfolio drawdown figures candidly, he does not disclose a comparable running tally of exactly how much the Facebook and Nvidia early sales cost him in dollar terms beyond the specific buy/sell prices stated — the reader can approximate the opportunity cost from the numbers given, but he does not state a total himself the way he does, for example, when estimating Carl Icahn's opportunity cost from selling Apple early.

Chapter summary

  • The investing book has no price-based stop loss; the exit signal is a change in the business — fundamentals deteriorating, the company no longer being a good business, or price becoming extremely overvalued — never price decline by itself.
  • His stated overvaluation thresholds for a sell vary by video — 20-30 percent above intrinsic value, 100 percent above it, or "roughly double" it — and even then he scales out gradually rather than exiting all at once.
  • Most bad news (recalls, lawsuits, PR scandals, a fixable management mistake) does not trigger a sale; an accounting or fraud scandal is the one category he treats as an immediate exit signal, citing Enron and WorldCom as the historical warning.
  • He names his own biggest recurring mistake as jumping in and out of great companies and trying to time re-entries, and states he'd be far richer having simply held.
  • He discloses selling Facebook/Meta far too early after an early gain, then rebuying at $100, $150, $200, and $300 versus his original $19, ending with over $650,000 in the position.
  • He discloses two Nvidia round trips — selling near $48 and rebuying near $530-550 in one account, selling at $380 and rebuying near $490 in another — calling himself "a bloody idiot" for the exit in the first.
  • He held and kept adding to UnitedHealth through a roughly 60 percent drawdown, a moat downgrade, and fraud allegations, before eventually selling the entire position for only a slight profit, citing discomfort with an alleged fraud culture rather than a valuation call.
  • Large drawdowns are a normal feature of even the best long-run records, not a signal of error — a claim he backs with Buffett's, Lynch's, and Amazon's own drawdown histories and with outside research on typical maximum drawdowns across thousands of stocks.

Try this

Using his own stated test, write down the specific fundamental condition that would have to change for you to sell each of your current long-term holdings — not a price level, a business condition, following his own framing that the sell trigger is the company failing its original criteria, not the stock falling. Then, separately, note what price would put each holding in his "extreme overvaluation" zone using the more conservative end of his own stated range — 20 to 30 percent above your own intrinsic-value estimate — so you have both triggers written down before either one is tested by a real drawdown or a real rally.

Chapter 8

Reading the Chart

In this chapter: the small, repeatedly reused toolkit Khoo brings to any chart — trend read from a handful of moving-average pairs, support and resistance treated as zones rather than lines, and the short list of candlestick patterns he uses to time entries inside all of it.

8.1 Trend first, everything else second

Khoo's technical method does not start with a pattern or an indicator; it starts with a question — is this an uptrend, a downtrend, or neither — and almost everything else in this chapter is a way of answering that question more precisely, or of deciding what to do once it's answered. He is explicit that price structure itself is the first read: a confirmed uptrend shows higher highs and higher lows, and he layers moving averages on top of that structure rather than in place of it.

The moving averages he reaches for are not exotic.
