Asked directly why he doesn't use a MACD zero-line cross to read trend, he says the 12/26 EMAs behind it are too fast and throw off too many false signals, and that he prefers a shorter pair — the 20 and 40 — for the short-term trend, and a slower pair — the 50 and 150 — for the medium-term trend. That two-speed structure — a fast pair for the near-term wobble, a slow pair for the trend that actually matters for holding a position — recurs across nearly a decade of videos, from a 2017 webinar through a 2024 breakdown that adds the 200 as a third, slower layer for confirmation, to a 2026 video restating the same 50/150 pairing (or its weekly equivalent, 10/30 weeks) as a technique he says he has taught for over twenty years and shared publicly for over ten.

Editor's note — why two moving-average speeds instead of one

A single moving average has to trade off responsiveness against noise: fast enough to matter and it whipsaws in every chop; slow enough to filter chop and it lags every real turn by weeks. Pairing two averages of different speeds sidesteps the trade-off rather than solving it — the fast one gives an early read, the slow one gives a filter, and treating a crossover between them as a signal (rather than reading either average in isolation) means the signal only fires when the near-term and medium-term pictures actually diverge. It's a standard trend-following construction, not something unique to this method — variants of it appear throughout technical-analysis literature under names like "dual moving-average crossover" — but it's worth naming because it explains why Khoo talks in pairs rather than single numbers throughout this chapter.

8.2 The moving-average toolkit — and its stated variants

He does not use one crossover pair; he uses several, layered by timeframe, and he is consistent that the exact numbers shift depending on the video and the market. For stocks, the recurring short-term pair is the 20 and 40 exponential moving averages: the 20 above the 40 signals a short-term uptrend, the 20 below the 40 a short-term downtrend. The medium-term pair is the 50 and 150 — sometimes simple, sometimes exponential, and he says outright it doesn't make much difference which in the medium term. He also names a 20/50 pairing in the same short-term-trend role elsewhere in the corpus, alongside stochastics and loss-streak estimates that likewise vary by video rather than settling on one number — a documented internal inconsistency the book reports as a range rather than resolving into a single "correct" pair.

He credits the 50/150 technique explicitly to Victor Sperandeo's book Methods of a Wall Street Master, saying plainly that he did not invent it. He backs it with a specific, if modest, statistical claim: a backtest spanning twenty to fifty years shows the crossover identifying a new bull market with over 90 percent accuracy — not 100. He also names its limits without being asked: the method works well in smooth, gradual transitions and "fails badly" in a rapid, parabolic plunge, because moving averages are lagging indicators that would have a trader selling near the bottom and buying back near the top. He gives his own case as the example — he did not sell during the 2020 COVID crash despite getting a 50/150 downtrend signal, because he recognized the 90-degree drop as exactly that kind of parabolic move where the signal should be overridden. He also notes the method works best on large, liquid names and "doesn't work well with all stocks".

Layered together, he describes a staged entry: enter early on a reversal candlestick at support, add on a cross above the 50-day moving average, add more on a 20/40 crossover confirming the short-term trend, and add the most on the 50/150 crossover with price above the 200 confirming the medium-term trend — accepting a progressively higher entry price at each stage in exchange for more confirmation. The same three-timeframe stack — 20/40 EMA, 50/150, and the 200 all aligning — reappears as his bar for calling a new rally confirmed, alongside a separate requirement that the index close above its 50-day average for at least five straight trading days first.

Editor's note — a forex-specific variant: 18/50/200

In his forex material specifically, the moving-average set is different from the stock convention above: the 18, 50, and 200 (the 18 and 50 exponential, the 200 simple), used together rather than as a single pair. He says his advanced forex strategies use mostly this 18/50/200 set rather than the 6-period average that features in his basic course's impulse-pullback and impulse-pin-bar setups. A clear uptrend by this reading is the 18 above the 50, the 50 above the 200, all three sloping up; a clear downtrend is the reverse stack — 200 on top, then 50, then 18, all sloping down — which he demonstrates on USD/CHF 15-minute candles. This is presented here as the forex variant, not a replacement for the 20/40 and 50/150 pairs used on stocks — he does not reconcile the two conventions across asset classes in the archive, and the book reports both rather than picking one as "the" system.

8.3 A crossover is not a trend change on its own

The single most repeated qualifier in this whole toolkit is that the crossover, by itself, proves nothing. Across roughly a decade of videos — 2016 through 2026 — Khoo states some version of the same two-part test: the 50 has to cross the 150 (or the 20/40, at the shorter timeframe), and both moving averages have to change slope in the matching direction, before he'll call it a real trend change. He states the negative case just as often as the positive one: a 50/150 cross with the 150 still sloping up is not a sell signal, and he's shown demonstrating exactly this on-camera — passing on a 50/150 downtrend cross on a Salesforce chart because the 150 (drawn in green) was still sloping upward, and passing on a short-selling setup for the same reason on an earlier chart, noting he "would not act on" a cross the 150's slope hadn't confirmed.

He restates the identical two-condition test for the 200-day average used on its own: price crossing below the 200 is not a confirmed downtrend until the 200 itself starts sloping down — until then, he calls it "the last line of defence" for the uptrend, since price often dips below and quickly reclaims it. The pattern holds for the reverse signal too — the 200 crossing back up, or the 50 recrossing above the 150, only counts once the slope changes to match. In the most recent card in the archive on this point, from a 2026 video, he states the rule in its most compact form yet: the crossover and the slope of both lines have to change direction to match before the signal is valid; a crossover without a slope change is not a valid signal. That is the same test he was demonstrating in 2016 and 2017, restated a decade later in almost the same words — one of the most durable, unrevised pieces of doctrine in the entire archive.

He treats the accuracy of the fully-confirmed signal (crossover plus slope) as strong but not perfect: he cites a backtest showing better than 90 percent reliability for the 50/150 bull-market signal, repeating the "not 100 percent" qualifier each time he gives the number. For higher confidence still — he puts a number on it, 99 percent — he adds two further conditions: the 200-day average also sloping upward, and price breaking a trend-line resistance.

8.4 Support and resistance: a zone, not a line

Khoo's second load-bearing convention is definitional: he explicitly defines support and resistance as zones where price tends to meet and reverse, not as precise lines. He gives the demand-and-supply logic behind each — resistance is a zone with an excess of sellers who psychologically feel the price is too high; support is a zone with an excess of buyers waiting below — and states the practical consequence directly: treat the level as a zone with a buffer, because price can overshoot slightly past it or fall short before reversing.

Editor's note — why "zone, not line" matters for anyone trading the level

A single price drawn as a line invites a trader to treat a one-tick miss or overshoot as a failure of the level, when the underlying claim — a cluster of orders sitting in a price range, not at one exact print — never made that promise. Reading a level as a band a few percent (or a few points, on a lower-priced instrument) wide is standard practice across technical trading generally, not something specific to this method; it's included here because it's the frame that makes several of Khoo's other rules — the three-touch test, the broken-resistance-becomes-support principle below — behave sensibly rather than looking arbitrary on an exact tick.

He is precise about how many times a level needs to be tested before he'll trust it. A level connecting only two points he calls a "probationary" resistance — his own term — treated as unconfirmed until it is touched a third time. He applies the identical test to a double top, which he also calls "probationary rather than significant" on only two touches, and states the forex version of the same rule almost verbatim: a level tested once or twice is "potential or probationary," with the third touch acting as confirmation. He applies the same three-touch requirement to support as well as resistance, and shows it inside a live trade example — an O'Reilly Automotive entry where trend-line support "confirmed on three touches" is one of five converging factors behind the trade.

The other recurring convention is that a broken level flips role. He states it as a general principle — support becomes resistance and resistance becomes support once a level is broken and price returns to it — and gives the mechanism: a broken resistance becomes support because the break itself means demand exceeded the overhanging supply, so buyers who missed the level the first time now wait there. He uses this repeatedly in live analysis, from a 2018 stock review where a resistance tested four times finally breaks and becomes the buy level on a bounce candle, through a 2024 S&P call naming 4169 as strong support precisely because it was former resistance, reinforced by the 50/150 averages and a multi-year trendline.

8.5 Chart patterns he uses — and one he doesn't

Beyond raw support/resistance zones, Khoo names a small set of recurring chart patterns, mostly built on top of the same zone-and-touch logic. The double top is a high, a pullback, and a second high at or slightly above the first that fails to hold — he defines a specific mechanical test for it, closing back below the prior high within three days, and is explicit that the pattern by itself is not strongly bearish; what compounds it is a confluence with an overbought reading, such as stochastics above 80 or price at the upper Bollinger Band. The double bottom is its mirror, and he adds a variant he attributes to observed market-maker behavior rather than to random price action: a "forced" or "false" double bottom, where the second low deliberately breaches the first to trigger retail stop-losses before reversing sharply higher — a pattern he also calls a "bear trap" in later videos and credits, in some tellings, to material from other named traders (Alon Chu, Elson Chu) rather than presenting as his own invention.

One pattern he explicitly rejects: asked about the head and shoulders, he says outright that he does not believe in it — the single clearest statement in the archive of a well-known technical pattern he declines to use, worth recording alongside the ones he does.

A separate consolidation-based pattern recurs specifically in his momentum trading material: an explosive flag breakout, where a stock that has already moved over 100 percent in roughly two months forms a downward-sloping flag, and he buys the breakout above that flag rather than chasing the initial move. He treats a bull flag (or bear flag in a downtrend) the same way in forex — as his primary strategy there, describing it as "surfing" price off the moving averages using the same template on every currency pair he trades.

Editor's note — how a flag differs from a double top or bottom

The patterns in section 8.5 fall into two families that are easy to conflate. A double top or double bottom is a reversal pattern: it marks a level where an existing trend is expected to end and turn the other way. A flag is a continuation pattern: the "pole" is the sharp initial move, and the "flag" is a brief, shallow, counter-sloping pause before price resumes in the same direction as the pole. Reading a flag as though it were a reversal — shorting into the pause because price looks like it's rolling over — is a common beginner error in technical trading generally; Khoo's own framing of the flag as an entry to buy the breakout, not a level to fade, keeps the two families distinct in his own material.

8.6 Candlestick patterns: the five he teaches, and how he stacks them

Khoo's candlestick material is more compact and more consistent than his chart-pattern material — a named set of five patterns, taught together in a single video and reused unchanged across years of live analysis. He frames candlesticks explicitly as an overlay on trend and support/resistance, not a standalone signal: they tell him the day-to-day sentiment inside a trend and let him fine-tune entries, and he states plainly that a candlestick pattern by itself is unreliable unless it appears in the right context and the right location.

The five: a bullish or bearish pin bar — a small or absent body with a long shadow making up at least two-thirds of the candle's range, most reliable at a support (bullish) or resistance (bearish) level, ideally with the shadow cutting through that level. A tweezer bottom or top — a large-bodied candle followed by an identical, opposite-colored candle, which combined he calls a "synthetic" pin bar. The one white soldier and its mirror, the one black crow — a candle that opens beyond and closes beyond the previous day's opposite-colored candle, ideally with a higher high and higher low (or the reverse). The morning star and evening star — a three-candle reversal where the middle candle gaps and shows indecision, ideally a small body with roughly equal shadows, and the third candle closes more than halfway into the first candle's range. And the bullish or bearish engulfing pattern, which he defines more loosely than the textbook version — requiring only that the second candle's body swallow the first candle's body, not the full range including shadows, a simplification he names explicitly as a loosened standard.

His summary rule for all five: go long only when a bullish pattern appears at the support of an uptrend, and go short only when a bearish pattern appears at the resistance of a downtrend. In live use across years of market reviews, the engulfing pattern in particular becomes his default trigger candle for both entries and exits — waiting for a bullish engulfing before adding to a long at a support level, or treating a bearish engulfing at resistance as a reason not to buy or to start taking profits. He is candid, more than once, that the absence of the pattern is itself informative: he holds a long specifically because no bearish reversal candle — no pin bar, no engulfing, no one black crow — ever appeared.

8.7 Stacking the toolkit: confluence

None of the tools in this chapter are meant to be used alone, and Khoo names the practice of combining them: "confluence," which he defines as the point where several independent technical signals — moving averages, trend lines, horizontal support and resistance, Fibonacci retracement levels, divergence, and candlestick patterns — converge on the same price and the same conclusion. He gives an explicit reason confluence works rather than treating it as a superstition: when traders using different tools all read the same level the same way, they all act on it at once, which he calls a self-fulfilling mechanism — the shared read becomes the reason the level holds.

He grades setups by how many of these signals line up: three converging signals is "a pretty good setup," four at once is "excellent". He treats a moving average itself as one confluence signal when price retraces to it and bounces, and is explicit elsewhere that he never uses a single tool in isolation — Fibonacci in particular is "never used by itself," always checked against a moving-average or horizontal support level before he trusts it. In a worked trade example on PII, he lists out a full confluence stack: a clear uptrend, price bouncing off the 20 moving average, a corrective wave landing exactly on the 61.8 percent Fibonacci retracement, and oversold stochastics — which he summarizes as "all the stars are aligned". He restates the underlying discipline in general terms in a later video: never form a trade thesis from one indicator or pattern alone; build a case from multiple signals, because when several tools say the same thing independently, the probability behind the read is higher.

The same layering shows up in the two chart patterns he trades most, both built from moving averages plus structure rather than from a single line. His primary forex setup, which he calls "surfing," is the bull flag (or bear flag in a downtrend) — a pattern he says he applies with the same template to every currency pair, describing it as literally surfing price off the moving averages themselves rather than off a separate drawn trendline. On stocks, a counter-trend wedge inside an established uptrend — price coiling into a series of lower highs "like a coiling spring" — becomes a breakout entry the moment price clears the wedge's own counter-trend line, which he treats as a second uptrend-continuation entry alongside the plain moving-average bounce.

Editor's note — why "more signals agreeing" is not the same as "higher odds of being right"

Confluence, as Khoo describes it, is a real phenomenon in liquid, widely watched markets: if enough participants draw a 50-day moving average or a 61.8 percent Fibonacci level in the same place, the crowd's own orders can make that level behave the way the tools predicted, independent of any deeper "true" reason price should turn there. What confluence does not do is turn several individually weak signals into one strong one in the statistical sense — moving averages, Fibonacci levels, and trend lines drawn off the same recent price history are not independent measurements, so stacking three of them can still be closer to counting one signal three times than to genuinely triangulating from separate evidence. Khoo does not address this distinction in the archive; the "three signals is good, four is excellent" grading he gives is his own heuristic, not a claim about statistical independence.

What he doesn't say

Khoo never states, in any card in this archive, a single canonical moving-average pair for stock trend-reading — the 20/40, 20/50, and 50/150 figures all recur, sometimes in the same video, without him flagging that the short-term number itself is not fixed. He does not reconcile the stock convention with the separate 18/50/200 forex convention in any video that addresses both asset classes together; a reader has to notice, as this book does, that the two systems are documented separately and never merged. He gives a backtested accuracy figure for the 50/150 crossover ("over 90 percent") repeatedly, but never discloses the exact sample — which index, what date range beyond "20 to 50 years," how ties or marginal slope changes were scored — so the number should be read as his own summary of his own review, not as an independently reproducible statistic. He explains why he rejects the head and shoulders pattern only by asserting it, without walking through a counter-example the way he does for patterns he keeps. And while he names the three-touch rule for both support and resistance, he never states a numeric tolerance for what counts as a "touch" versus a clean miss — the zone-not-line principle implies some buffer, but he never gives it a number.

Chapter summary

  • Trend is read first from price structure (higher highs/higher lows) and then confirmed with layered moving-average pairs — a fast pair for the short-term trend, a slower pair for the medium-term trend.
  • For stocks, the recurring pairs are 20/40 (sometimes 20/50) exponential moving averages for the short term and 50/150 for the medium term, reported here as documented variants rather than resolved into one number.
  • A crossover alone is never a trend-change signal in his method — both moving averages must also change slope to match the new direction, a test he restates with almost no variation from 2016 through 2026.
  • The 50/150 crossover technique is explicitly credited to Victor Sperandeo, backed with a stated (not-100-percent) backtested accuracy above 90 percent, and flagged by Khoo himself as unreliable in fast, parabolic moves.
  • Forex uses a distinct 18/50/200 moving-average convention (18 and 50 exponential, 200 simple), kept separate from the stock convention rather than merged with it.
  • Support and resistance are defined as zones with a buffer, not exact lines, and a level needs a third touch before he calls it confirmed — his own term for an unconfirmed, twice-touched level is "probationary".
  • A broken resistance becomes support (and the reverse) because the break itself proves demand exceeded supply at that price — a principle he applies repeatedly in live trade reviews to project the next level.
  • His five named candlestick patterns — pin bar, tweezer, one white soldier/one black crow, morning/evening star, and engulfing — are taught as an overlay on trend and support/resistance, never as standalone signals, with the engulfing becoming his most-used live trigger candle.
  • He never uses one signal alone — he defines "confluence" as multiple tools (moving averages, trend lines, support/resistance, Fibonacci, candlesticks) agreeing on the same price, grading three signals as a good setup and four as excellent.

Try this

Build the same two-condition screen he says he coded himself: scan for stocks where the 20-day exponential moving average is greater than the 40-day exponential moving average (short-term uptrend) and the 50-day simple moving average is greater than the 150-day simple moving average (medium-term uptrend). Pull up ten to twenty results and, for each, check the slope of both averages by eye before assuming the crossover is valid — the qualifier in section 8.3 means the screen only tells you where a crossover exists, not whether the trend change is confirmed.

Chapter 9

Setups and Entries

In this chapter: what actually has to line up, in Khoo's own trading framework, before he clicks buy or sell — the confluence of levels, moving averages, and candlestick confirmation he requires, the named setups he reuses across years of trade walkthroughs, and his own repeated admission that none of it works every time.

9.1 One signal is never enough

Khoo states his entry philosophy as a single organizing rule before he ever names an individual setup: he does not trade off one indicator or one level, he trades off confluence — the meeting, merging, or convergence of different technical signals that all anticipate the same price move at the same time. He lists six signals whose convergence he counts toward this: moving averages, trend lines, horizontal support and resistance, candlestick patterns, Fibonacci retracements, and momentum oscillators. He grades setups by how many of these line up — three converging signals is, in his words, "a pretty good setup," and four at once is excellent — and says that in his own trading he usually only takes a trade when at least three or four signals agree simultaneously. Elsewhere he defines confluence more narrowly and specifically for candlestick-driven entries: a candlestick pattern, a trend line, a Fibonacci level, and a horizontal support level all giving the same signal at once.

He gives a mechanical reason confluence works rather than treating it as superstition: when traders using different tools — moving averages, Fibonacci, trend lines — all arrive at the same expected direction, they tend to act on it together, and that concentration of buying or selling is what makes the price actually move the way the signals predicted. He calls it a self-fulfilling prophecy, and separately calls confluence "what professionals do to gain an edge in the markets".

Editor's note — why a self-fulfilling prophecy is not circular reasoning

It sounds suspicious to say a signal works because enough people believe it works, but the mechanism is real and well documented outside Khoo's own material: a round-number level or a widely-watched moving average (the 200-day is the most common example across markets, not specific to his teaching) attracts disproportionate order flow precisely because many independent participants are watching the same chart feature. That doesn't make the level magic, and it doesn't mean it will hold this time — it means the crowd's attention is itself one input into where price goes next, alongside the fundamentals Khoo covers elsewhere in this book.

Khoo walks through several real trades to show what stacked confluence looks like in practice rather than as an abstract checklist. On O'Reilly Automotive he took a long with four converging signals at once: a bullish "one white soldier" candlestick pattern, a bounce off the 50-day moving average, oversold stochastics, and a supporting trend line, and on a second O'Reilly entry he counted five factors — trend-line support confirmed on three touches, a horizontal support level, and further confirmation beyond that. On another recent trade he counted five converging levels: a bullish pin bar, a bounce off the 100-day moving average, a clear trend line, and two further signals at the same point. On Home Depot he took a long where the retracement bounced off the 200-day moving average, sat exactly at the 61.8% Fibonacci retracement, and carried further confirmation. These are not four different strategies — they are the same confluence requirement applied to four different charts.

9.2 The bounce: his most-repeated setup

Across the corpus, the single most frequently demonstrated entry is what Khoo calls the bounce strategy. He describes it plainly: stocks on an uptrend move in waves, and price tends to bounce off a moving average — the 50, the 100, or the 150, depending on the chart — rather than moving in a straight line. His stated procedure is to buy when price retraces to the moving average and bounces off it, treating that dip as the buying opportunity within an established uptrend rather than a reason for concern. He is explicit that he does not buy at the moving average itself — he waits for a confirmation candle that shows the bounce has actually started before entering.

He works the arithmetic of a long bounce trade concretely: buy at $89, stop loss at $84.50 for a risk of $4.50 a share, with a target set at double that risk, $98. The mirror short version, taken when price retraces up to a moving average in a downtrend, is worked the same way: sell at $34.50 with a stop loss at $35.50, a risk of $1 a share, in the direction that profits from further decline. Different videos code the bounce with slightly different mechanical detail — in one framing the bounce is defined as the candle's low cutting below the moving average while the body (open and close) stays above it, and in another he layers a two-candle "trade-through" pattern plus a confirmation candle on top of the moving-average touch before entering.

He is also candid that the bounce works cleanly with the trend and poorly against it: very short-term swing traders sometimes buy a bounce as a counter-trend trade, he notes, but must be very careful to exit quickly before the larger downtrend resumes and overwhelms the small bounce.

He does not treat this as a curiosity confined to his own trading. Elsewhere he generalizes the same wave logic to long-term investors adding to positions — buying every time price corrects and bounces off a moving average, sometimes at the 50, sometimes deeper at the 150 — and to ETF investors, saying a good time to add is when the index dips to a moving average, bounces, and the trend resumes.

He also gives disclosed performance figures for the bounce strategy specifically, which is unusual candor for a setup he demonstrates so frequently: compiled across roughly 500 real student trades, the bounce strategy returned 315R total, an average of 0.63R per trade, at an overall win rate of 56 percent. In one monthly aggregate he cites, 11 bounce trades produced nine wins and two losses, an 81 percent win rate for that particular month — a figure well above the 56 percent long-run average, which is itself a small demonstration of how much a single month's results can diverge from the strategy's real, larger-sample rate.

9.3 Confirmed trends, breakouts, and the mirror-image short

Beyond the bounce, Khoo names two further recurring entries for a confirmed uptrend. The first is the small consolidation breakout: price makes the same high repeatedly — tested at least three times to count as a real resistance level — and he buys once price breaks decisively above it. The second is the entry after a confirmed trend reversal itself: he requires two conditions before calling a downtrend turned bull — the moving averages must stop sloping down and flatten, and the faster average (the 50, in his usual setup) must cross above the slower one — at which point he says to start buying aggressively and to keep buying every subsequent dip.

He is explicit about the timing cost of waiting for that confirmation: the 50/150 crossover, he concedes, gets you into a new trend a bit late, which is exactly why he layers earlier entries like the bounce and the mean-reversion setups described below on top of it rather than waiting on the crossover alone. He also warns about a specific false-confirmation trap: amateurs buy a 50/150 crossover while the 150 is still sloping down, mistaking a temporary cross for a real trend change, and the price often keeps falling afterward — which is the same slope-change qualifier this book's prior chapter attaches to moving-average crossovers generally, applied here directly to an entry decision.

For a downtrend, every uptrend entry has a stated mirror. The mirror of the bounce is a rally into a moving average or trend line that fails and rolls back down, which he treats as the short-side equivalent entry. The mirror of the consolidation breakout is a mini consolidation breakdown, where support tested at least three times finally gives way. He states the timing rule for shorting a rally in similar terms to the long-side breakout: the best time to short on a downtrend is when price rallies into resistance and fails to break it — he sells only after that failure is visible, not in anticipation of it.

He also names two chart patterns as reversal setups rather than trend-following ones. The double top — price makes a high, retreats, then makes a second high at or slightly above the first — is a setup he shorts once the second high fails to exceed the pattern meaningfully. Its mirror, the double bottom (and sometimes triple bottom), is the accumulation-phase reversal pattern that experienced swing traders buy once the pattern completes.

Not every technically valid pattern gets taken. He describes skipping a technically correct "one black crow" reversal signal because the distance from his entry to a sensible stop loss made the resulting risk-per-share too large relative to the likely reward — the pattern was real, but the trade's arithmetic wasn't good enough. This is the same discipline restated from a different angle: confluence tells him a setup is present, but a poor risk-to-reward ratio can still veto it.

9.4 Candlestick confirmation: the vocabulary he actually uses

Khoo's candlestick vocabulary is narrower than the full traditional catalogue, and he defines each pattern with a specific geometric rule rather than gesturing at a shape. The bullish pin bar is a candle with a small body — or no body — and a long lower shadow making up roughly two-thirds of the candle's total range; the bearish pin bar is its mirror, a long upper shadow of the same proportion. He says colour is not diagnostic — a pin bar that happens to close red still counts as bullish if the lower-shadow geometry is there. He explains the pattern psychologically: sellers pushed price down for most of the session, but buyers pushed it back up before the close, and the long lower shadow is the visible record of that reversal in sentiment.

He defines the "one white soldier" as a bullish candle that opens above the previous bearish candle's close and closes above its open, ideally with a higher high and higher low than that prior candle; the "one black crow" is the bearish mirror. He defines the morning star as a three-candle sequence — a large bearish candle, a small indecision candle that gaps down, then a bullish candle that gaps up and closes into the first candle's body — with the evening star as its mirror at the top of a trend. For engulfing patterns, he explicitly loosens the textbook definition: he requires only that the second candle's body engulfs the first candle's body, not its entire high-low range including wicks.

Across all of these, he attaches the same conditional qualifier: a pattern's reliability depends on where it sits, not just on its shape. A bullish pin bar has its highest probability at a support level within an uptrend, with the lower shadow ideally cutting below that support line. A bullish engulfing pattern on an uptrend with no clear support level underneath it gets downgraded — he prefers one sitting on a recognizable level, such as the 50-day moving average. A morning star has the highest probability at a support level, ideally within an uptrend rather than a fresh downtrend. This is the candlestick-confirmation half of the confluence requirement from section 9.1 made explicit: the shape alone is not the signal, the shape at a level is.

He names the accepted bullish reversal set together in one place: a bullish pin bar, a bullish engulfing pattern, or a one white soldier — and elsewhere adds a further filter on top of the moving-average bounce itself, narrowing his watchlist to setups where the bounce coincides with one of these patterns specifically.

9.5 Momentum indicators: the confirmation layer, not the trigger

Khoo treats momentum oscillators as a confirming layer on top of price structure, not as a standalone entry signal. His stated favourites for reading trend-following momentum are the force index for strength, the MACD for momentum, and the parabolic SAR, alongside moving averages for direction; his favoured oscillators for reading overbought and oversold conditions are full stochastics, Bollinger Bands, MACD used as an oscillator, and the Williams %R.

On stochastics specifically, he reads a %K/%D reading above 80 as overbought and below 20 as oversold, though several trade walkthroughs use a looser below-30 threshold as "temporarily oversold and due to bounce" instead — a real inconsistency across videos that this book preserves as a range rather than smoothing into one number, the same way it treats his other stated thresholds elsewhere. On MACD, he uses the standard 12/26/9 setting, defines the signal line as a 9-day EMA of the MACD line, and reads a MACD-line-above-signal-line cross as bullish momentum, wanting that condition true before going long. He states a specific reservation about one common MACD reading: he says he personally does not use the MACD zero-line cross to judge trend, because the underlying 12- and 26-period EMAs are too fast and generate frequent false signals. He uses the Williams %R mainly for a specific deep-oversold "capitulation" setup, requiring both a 10-period and a 260-period reading to drop below minus 80 before treating the market as sufficiently stretched to snap back.

He is explicit that these tools are read in combination, not singly. For a long entry on an uptrend, he wants two of three momentum readings — parabolic SAR, force index, and MACD — bullish, or accepts just one of the three if a bullish candlestick pattern is also present. Hunting for divergence, he runs MACD, RSI, and full stochastics together on the same chart, and on one Intel setup accepted the trade because two of the three showed lower highs against price while the third was merely flat — "two out of three enough," in his framing. He also draws a specific distinction readers are likely to conflate: RSI measures a stock's strength against its own past price performance, while a separate "relative strength" reading he also uses measures a stock's performance against the broader market (typically the S&P 500) — he warns these are "completely different" despite the similar name.

Editor's note — divergence as a warning, not a trigger

Khoo is careful, in more than one video, to frame divergence — price making a new high or low while an oscillator does not confirm it — as an early warning that a reversal may be coming, not as a signal to act on immediately. Seeing the divergence is not itself a sell or buy signal; he still waits for a confirmation candle before entering. This matters because divergence is one of the more visually persuasive indicator patterns available on a chart, and it is correspondingly easy to overreact to — the discipline he describes here is what keeps an early warning from becoming a premature entry.

He also warns against a specific failure mode connected to indicators more generally: he says that traders on a losing streak feel an overwhelming temptation to pile on more indicators — Bollinger Bands, ADX, divergence, Ichimoku Cloud — hoping one more tool will filter out the losses and reveal a "holy grail". His own stance runs the other way: a small, reused toolkit read for confluence, not an expanding stack of oscillators searched for the one that finally works.

9.6 Waiting for confirmation, and refusing to chase

Khoo states, more than once, a rule that cuts across every setup described above: he never predicts the market and never enters purely because a level or pattern looks like it is about to complete — he always waits for confirmation, even when he has already called something a great buying opportunity in advance. The mechanical form of that confirmation, for a bullish setup, is a confirmation candle that closes above the high of the prior swing low candle, with the buy order placed a small distance above that confirmation candle's high.

The other half of the same discipline is a rule against entering too late. He states it flatly for forex: never chase the price — if it has already moved without you, whether up or down, you do not chase it. He extends the identical rule to equities, calling it "never chase the girl" — his own phrasing — meaning he never adds shares during a wave up and only adds when price pulls back to a level of support. When an order he has placed is not triggered and price moves away without him, he says he does not chase it, comparing the situation, in his own words, to not chasing after a bride who has already left.

He is candid that waiting for confirmation has a real cost: because his second buy point (a deeper, more conservative entry) does not always occur, he says he personally prefers entering at the first, earlier buy point, while acknowledging either choice is legitimate depending on the trader. He is equally candid that being faked out is a normal, expected part of waiting for confirmation rather than a sign the method has failed: he says traders should expect to be faked out by a few false reversals before the real bottom arrives, and that this is normal, not a signal the approach is broken.

9.7 What he says these setups actually deliver — and where they fail

This is the section where Khoo's own numbers do more to qualify his setups than any outside critique could. He states directly that a realistic short-term trading win rate sits around 45 to 60 percent, elsewhere narrowing it to "about 50 to 60 percent — roughly half hit the stop loss" and noting the approach only works because winners are sized larger than losers. In a later video he restates the same ceiling in blunter terms: no matter which technical method is used, the realistic best win rate in trading is about 60 percent, meaning roughly 40 out of 100 trades lose. In one specific monthly track record he cites, October produced 20 trades — 12 wins, 5 losses, 1 breakeven, roughly a 63 percent win rate — but he immediately cautions against expecting that to hold, saying not to anticipate a 90 or even 100 percent win rate and that the realistic long-term average for the system is closer to 50–60 percent. He also names the two most common reasons he sees for a low win rate in the first place: trading against the trend, and taking low-quality, low-probability trades that lacked real confluence.

He extends the same candour to the levels and candlestick patterns themselves, not only to overall win rates. A support bounce on AT&T, he notes in one walkthrough, was a "fair technical setup" that still failed — not because the chart reading was wrong, but because the stock's relative strength was too weak to carry a bounce. He explicitly caveats one of his own reversal calls, saying a signal like a bounce off a moving average on weekly candles is "a good reversal signal" while adding, in the same breath, that "no signal is 100 percent". Asked directly how a trader knows a corrective wave has actually ended, he answers that there are no guarantees in trading, only ways to estimate high-probability outcomes — the main one being the confluence and confirmation process described in this chapter, not any single indicator. Even a specific, named candlestick pattern gets the same treatment on camera: reading a bearish engulfing candle, he says it usually signals further decline but adds there are "no guarantees" and that price can still reverse back up.

Editor's note — reading a stated win rate as a real constraint, not marketing

A trader who says his method wins 50–63 percent of the time, and means it, is making a claim that roughly four times in ten (or more) the setup will lose money even when executed correctly. That is a structural feature of the approach, not evidence the trader is doing something wrong on the losing trades — trend-following and mean-reversion entries built around probabilistic signals are supposed to lose a meaningful share of the time, and are only profitable in aggregate because of position sizing and reward-to-risk ratios covered elsewhere in this book. A reader evaluating whether to use any of the setups in this chapter should treat the 45–60 percent range as the honest baseline, not as a floor beneath which something has gone wrong.

What he doesn't say about this

Khoo does not reconcile, in any single video in this archive, the differing stochastics oversold thresholds he uses across setups — sometimes below 20, sometimes below 30 — leaving a reader to notice the inconsistency rather than having it flagged for them. He does not explain, in mechanistic terms, why confluence should be expected to raise a trade's odds beyond citing the self-fulfilling-prophecy argument in section 9.1; he offers no worked statistics comparing a three-signal setup's win rate against a one-signal setup's, only the general claim that more agreement means a "pretty good" or "excellent" trade. He also does not address what happens when his stated signals conflict outright — for instance, a bullish candlestick pattern appearing while momentum indicators read bearish — beyond the general two-of-three rule given for a handful of specific setups; there is no general tie-breaking rule stated for confluence as a whole. Finally, while he discloses aggregate win-rate and R-multiple figures for a few named systems (the bounce strategy, the intraday gap system), he does not disclose a comparable track record for the majority of setups described in this chapter, including the pin-bar and engulfing-pattern entries that recur most often in his walkthroughs.

Chapter summary

  • Khoo's entry philosophy is confluence, not any single indicator: he lists six signal types (moving averages, trend lines, support/resistance, candlesticks, Fibonacci, oscillators) and says three converging signals is a good setup, four an excellent one, and that he personally only takes trades with three or four in agreement.
  • His most-repeated setup is the bounce — buying (or shorting) a retracement to a moving average once a confirmation candle shows the bounce has started, never at the level itself, with disclosed results of 315R over roughly 500 student trades at a 56 percent win rate.
  • Confirmed-trend entries require the moving averages to flatten and cross, not merely cross while still sloping the old direction — a distinction he says amateurs miss, buying crossovers that keep failing.
  • Every candlestick pattern he uses (pin bar, engulfing, one white soldier/one black crow, morning/evening star) is defined by an exact geometric rule and is explicitly rated higher at a support or resistance level than in open space.
  • Momentum indicators (stochastics, MACD, Williams %R, RSI) function as a confirming layer requiring two-of-three agreement in several of his named systems, not as standalone triggers, and he warns against piling on more indicators as a substitute for a real setup.
  • His stated rule against chasing applies across both trading and investing: never enter into a wave that has already moved without you — "never chase the girl" — always wait for the pullback.
  • He states his realistic win rate ceiling directly and repeatedly, in the 45–63 percent range depending on the video and the system, and explicitly warns against expecting the high end of that range to hold.
  • He is candid on camera that individual setups fail even when correctly read — a fair technical bounce can still not work, and "no signal is 100 percent" — treating this as a normal cost of the method rather than a flaw to be engineered away.

Try this

Khoo's own instruction for building the pattern-recognition side of this chapter is to pull up a chart and count, rather than guess: he suggests reviewing a stock's chart history and marking every place a chosen moving average was tested, to see for yourself how often a bounce held versus failed, before trusting the setup with real money. Pick a stock you know, mark every touch of its 50-day moving average over the past two years, and note the confluence present (or absent) at each touch — a trend line, a horizontal level, a candlestick pattern — against whether the bounce actually held. The exercise is his own logic applied literally: confluence is a claim about probability, and the only way to trust a probability is to have counted it yourself.

Chapter 10

Stops, Targets, and Trade Management

In this chapter: how the trading book's exit is decided before the entry order is even placed — where the stop goes and why, how a profit target is sized as a multiple of risk, the mirrored logic that has him bidding a breakout up while bidding a falling stock down, and the documented nights, on his own camera, when he didn't follow his own exit rule.

10.1 The exit decided before entry

Chapter 2 established the two-book split: no stop loss on the investing book, a mandatory stop loss on the trading book. This chapter is about what "mandatory" actually means in practice — because a rule to always use a stop is only useful if it comes with rules for where the stop goes, how far the target sits from it, and what happens when price gets close to either one.

He states the mandatory-stop rule at its most basic: always use a stop loss, and never enter a trade without one already defined. He is equally insistent that the stop, once set, is not a suggestion to be revisited when price gets uncomfortable — he sets the losing side of his own risk simulator to a fixed multiple of exactly one, because he says he always hits the stop and never removes it. He goes further in a separate video, stating as a firm rule that a stop loss should never be shifted lower or removed once it is in place, warning that doing so might work once or twice but will eventually produce the one large loss that wipes out an entire account. He frames the temptation itself as a psychological pattern: after a run of stops that get hit and are then followed by the price recovering, traders come to resent their own stop and start moving it away from the market to avoid being "shaken out" — precisely the habit he says destroys accounts.

The logic he gives for entering with the exit already fixed is arithmetic, not sentiment: a trade's risk (the distance from entry to stop) and its reward (the distance from entry to target) both have to be known before the position is taken, because the reward is defined as a multiple of the risk — a convention covered in the next section. None of that arithmetic is possible if the stop is decided after the fact.

10.2 Placing the stop beyond the obvious level

Given that a stop must exist, the next question is where. He is explicit that the naive answer — put the stop just past the support or resistance line itself — is a mistake, because that price is exactly where everyone else's stop sits too. Price can dip a few cents below a support level, take out that cluster of stops, and bounce right back up without ever threatening the trade thesis; a stop placed with no buffer gets caught in that dip. He generalizes the point: a stop must sit far enough beyond the level to give price room to pierce support and bounce back, not sit exactly on the line where the crowd is already stopped out.

His stated buffer size varies by instrument and method. On the 15-minute forex charts he normally trades, he gives a working buffer of roughly 8 to 12 pips, and separately says he derives that buffer from twice the Average True Range — though in practice, he adds, he usually just defaults to about 8 pips from experience rather than recalculating it each time. He describes reading the ATR value itself loosely, taking roughly the midpoint between its recent high and low and doubling that figure for the buffer. On stock swing trades he applies the same ATR logic to a specific level, placing the stop one ATR below the support level so the trade has room before being stopped out. In simpler candlestick setups, he uses a flat cents-based buffer instead of ATR — a few cents above a swing high for a short, or a few cents below a swing low for a long — sizes that only make sense on stocks trading in the tens or hundreds of dollars, not as a universal distance.

The same logic runs in the other direction for targets. He does not set a resistance-based target exactly at the resistance level either, placing it about one ATR short of the level instead, because price often falls just short of a resistance level and reverses before actually touching it. Stop and target buffers are, in his framing, the same insurance against the same problem: prices rarely respect a level to the cent, and a plan built on exact levels gets defeated by the noise around them.

Editor's note — why obvious stop levels get taken out

The pattern Khoo describes has a name in market-microstructure terms: liquidity is often clustered exactly at round numbers and at visually obvious chart levels, because that is where the largest number of retail traders independently choose to place orders. Large participants who need to fill sizable orders without moving the market look for exactly this kind of clustered liquidity to trade against — a widely discussed phenomenon in currency markets around round-number levels like 1.1000 on EUR/USD, where a cluster of stop and limit orders sits just past the figure. None of this requires a conspiracy; it only requires that enough independent traders place orders at the same visually obvious price for that price to become a magnet for the last leg of a move before it reverses. Khoo's buffer rule is a practical response to that mechanical fact, not a claim about who is doing the sweeping.

10.3 Targets as a multiple of risk — and an inconsistency he owns

Once the stop defines the risk distance (his "1R"), the target is set as a multiple of that distance rather than as an independent price forecast. The convention he states most often for stocks is a 2R target: risk one dollar to make two, so a losing trade costs 1R and a winning trade returns 2R. He states the same 2:1 framing generally for stock trading, sometimes allowing a somewhat lower minimum such as 1.5R to 2R depending on the setup.

For forex, he states a different number, and — unusually for the channel — he names the discrepancy himself rather than leaving a reader to notice it. He says 1.5R is his usual profit target for forex, and separately says he found 1.5R to be the optimum figure specifically for the impulse-pullback bull flag strategy he trades on 15-minute forex candles. Asked to account for the gap with his stock-trading target, he gives the reason directly: forex is markedly more volatile than stocks, and a 2R target on forex "does not get hit as often," which lowers the strategy's win rate. He restates the same comparison elsewhere — a 1.5R target in forex against the 2R he teaches in his stock course, because forex prices move more erratically — and adds a transaction-cost argument specific to smaller stop distances: when his stop loss is only ten pips or so, he still holds to 1.5R rather than something larger, because at ten to fifteen pips the round-turn cost eats a large share of a smaller expected return.

He does not treat 1.5R as a fixed law even within forex. In one later video he revises an earlier stated rule, saying that where he used to always target 1.5R, he has since found 1.6R more profitable under certain conditions — a small but real change to a number he had previously stated as settled. And he explicitly denies that 1.5R is universal at all: in other strategies he may use 2R, and in some stock trades even 4R, because the right multiple depends on the specific strategy being run, not on a single constant.

He also warns against simply pushing the multiple higher across the board. Set the target too high — his example is 3R or 4R paired with a trailing stop — and a trade that would have closed at a clean 1.5R win instead frequently gives back the gain and closes as a scratch or a full 1R loss, because greed for a bigger number converts winners into losers on the way back down. He narrates the psychological version of the same trap: a trader takes the disciplined 2R exit by the rule, watches price keep running without them, moves the target to 4R on the next trade out of regret, and gets burned when that trade reverses before reaching it. The lesson he draws is that regret over a target hit "too early" is the emotion that pulls a working system apart, not a signal that the rule was wrong.

He is just as clear that habitually shrinking the win side breaks the system from the other direction: cutting winners short at 0.5R to 0.7R while losses still run to a full 1R removes the positive expectancy a 2R-target system depends on, because the occasional big win can no longer offset the more frequent losses.

Editor's note — reading the 1.5R-versus-2R split as evidence, not a flaw

A single trader stating two different numbers for what is, on its face, the same idea — risk-to-reward ratio — could read as inconsistency worth discounting. Read differently, it is one of the more useful things in this chapter: he is naming a real difference between asset classes (forex's higher volatility relative to typical stop distance) and adjusting a rule of thumb to compensate, rather than applying one number everywhere out of habit. The book's discipline is to report both figures with their stated conditions rather than average them into a false single constant — the same approach Chapter 14 takes with his other stated ranges (moving-average pairings, stochastic settings, loss-streak estimates) that also shift across videos.

10.4 The mirrored entry rule

The outline for this book names a pattern that runs across both books in opposite directions: when he is trading, his entries chase price higher as confirmation builds; when he is investing, his entries step down to meet a falling price. Same trader, same instinct to control the fill, aimed at opposite signals.

On the trading side, entries are built to trigger on strength, not to anticipate it. A buy-stop order sits a few cents above the high of a signal candle, so the trade only fires if price actually continues in the intended direction the next session — if price falls instead, the order is never filled and no position is taken. The same logic runs on the execution side of a fill: placing a live order, he starts at the bid and raises it five cents at a time until filled, buying his way up toward the ask rather than waiting passively for a better price, and does the mirror version selling — starting at the highest offer and lowering it step by step until a seller takes it. On options fills specifically, he starts a limit order near the mid of the bid-ask spread and works the price up incrementally until it fills, rather than crossing the whole spread at once. In every one of these cases the price he is willing to accept moves toward the market, not away from it — he is bidding a trade up to get in on confirmed strength.

On the investing side the same instinct runs backward. He states flatly that he never buys an entire position at once, buying instead in three or four tranches spread across three or four support levels as price falls. He gives a specific version of the schedule: a quarter of the planned allocation at the first buy level, then another quarter at each successive support level as the price drops further. He states the underlying reasoning for why he buys this way rather than trying to call a single bottom: no one can reliably identify the exact low, so spreading the entry across levels below the current price reduces that single-point risk. In one video he goes further than merely accepting lower fills — he says he actively hopes the price falls further after his first tranche so that he can add at deeper support, while noting that in practice price more often rebounds after the first or second tranche than keeps falling to the third. He gives a threshold version of the same rule tied to valuation rather than chart support: he starts buying when the share price is 15 percent below his estimate of intrinsic value, adds more at 20 percent below, and doubles up at 30 to 35 percent below, describing that deepest level as where the market is "clearly wrong" about the business.

The mirror is exact in structure and opposite in direction: the trading entry bids the price up to confirm the market agrees with him before he commits; the investing entry bids the price down, treating a falling price as a better deal on a company he has already decided he believes in, and using tranches rather than a single stop-defined trigger to manage that entry.

10.5 Tightening the stop without loosening it

Trade management between entry and exit is not limited to leaving the original stop untouched. He describes moving a stop toward breakeven once a trade has already worked in his favor — specifically, once price has moved 1R in profit, he moves the stop up to the entry price, so a subsequent reversal produces a scratch trade at worst rather than a full loss. This is consistent with the never-loosen rule from 10.1: the stop is allowed to move only in the direction that reduces risk, never in the direction that gives the trade more room to be wrong.

He also documents adjusting a stop mid-trade for reasons beyond a simple breakeven rule, and is willing to show the cost of doing so. Reviewing a stock trade on HELE, he moved his stop up the moment he saw a single bearish candle appear against his position, and was stopped out for a 1.62R gain instead of riding to his planned 2R target — he notes in the same review that the stock went on to reach the original target anyway after he had already exited. He reports this as a real trade with a real outcome, not as a success story: the discretionary tightening protected an existing gain, but it also cost the difference between 1.62R and the 2R the plan called for.

10.6 Scaling out rather than exiting all at once

For positions that have moved well into profit, he does not describe a single all-or-nothing exit as his only tool. He lays out three alternative ways to close a winning trade depending on what price is doing: exit when a fast moving average crosses back below a slower one, trail a stop beneath a longer moving average and let the trade run, or scale out in pieces once price has gone parabolic well above its moving averages. In an older walkthrough of a longer-hold "piranha" trade he stacks three separate profit targets at the 50-, 150-, and 200-day moving averages in sequence, treating a break above the 50 as the point where he shifts from taking partial profit to holding for the longer term.

He applies the same scaling logic to trimming a position that has run further than expected without a clean reversal signal yet. Near a suspected short-term top he scales out gradually — selling a half or a third of the position rather than the whole thing at once — and only exits what remains once a clear bearish candlestick close confirms the top. He gives the same instruction for a longer, still-fundamentally-sound holding in an overbought market: rather than sell the position outright, he slowly sells a bit at a time while still holding the rest, because he judges the market could keep running higher even though it looks stretched. He states the general principle behind all of these: he never sells an entire position in one shot on a top call, because no one can reliably identify the exact top any more than the exact bottom.

10.7 Where he documents breaking his own rule

The most useful material in this chapter is not the stated rules — it is the handful of videos where he shows himself, live, coming right up against one of his own exit rules and then not following it in the moment, on camera, with the reasoning narrated as it happens.

His stated closing rule for a credit spread is to lock in the gain once he has realized close to 90 percent of the available premium, treating the last sliver as not worth the residual risk of holding into expiry. In the same video he narrates checking a specific Amazon spread against that threshold, finding it had not yet reached 90 percent, and choosing to keep waiting through the session for more decay rather than close it there. He frames the remaining amount at that point as only a few hundred dollars still to be squeezed out — and then, as Amazon's price began to fall later that same session and his open profit started shrinking on expiry day itself, he chose to wait a while longer for still more, rather than close the position immediately while the gain was still large. Only once the remaining premium had shrunk to a few dollars did he close immediately rather than hold for the last cents. The sequence is a documented, narrated case of a stated 90-percent rule getting stretched in real time by the same trader who states it, under the ordinary pull of "just a bit more" — the exact temptation his own psychology material (Chapter 16) describes traders falling for.

The options-management material contains a second inconsistency worth naming plainly: elsewhere he states his premium-cutting threshold not as 90 percent realized but as 50 percent of the premium at risk — he says he only cuts a losing option position once he is down about half the cost paid, and separately says he usually cuts an option loss at 50 percent of premium. A 90-percent-of-gain closing rule and a 50-percent-of-loss cutting rule address different situations — one a winning credit spread, the other a losing debit position — but the book flags that he has never, across the cards gathered here, stated a single unified number for "when a premium position gets closed" the way he has for a stock stop-loss buffer. Readers following his options material should expect the exact threshold to depend on which side of the trade they are on and which video they are drawing the rule from.

A separate, starker case appears in a video he titles as a confession. Trading GameStop puts during the 2021 short-squeeze period, he states he will not buy back a losing put for a small loss and intends to simply let it run to expiry — reasoning that out of the money he keeps the full premium, and in the money he takes the shares. That stated intention runs directly against the general trading-book instruction from 10.1 to never enter, or stay in, a position without a live exit plan capping the downside; here the plan is explicitly to remove the exit and ride the position to whatever expiry delivers. He discloses the position size and premium involved without softening the account — the book records it as his own stated departure from his own general rule, not as a hidden lapse the archive happens to reveal.

What he doesn't say about this

He never states a single reconciled rule for when an options position gets closed — the 90-percent-of-realized-gain figure and the 50-percent-of-loss figure sit in different videos addressing different situations, and no video in the archive puts them side by side or explains why the thresholds differ by that much. He does not give a stop-buffer figure for instruments outside forex pips, stock cents, and one-ATR multiples — cryptocurrency and index-future stop placement are not addressed in the cards this book draws from. He never discusses what happens to a resting stop order during a price gap, when the fill can occur well past the stop level itself — a real risk for any stop-loss order, and one the archive does not show him addressing directly. And while he narrates the Amazon and GameStop cases candidly, he does not step back in either video to generalize from them into a revised rule; the lapses are shown, not analyzed, and the reader is left to draw the "regret loop" lesson from Chapter 16's psychology material rather than from anything he says in the trade-management videos themselves.

Chapter summary

  • The trading book's stop loss is set before entry and is never loosened once placed — only tightened toward breakeven as a trade moves in his favor.
  • Stops and targets both sit a buffer beyond the obvious chart level (roughly 8-12 pips or one ATR in forex, a few cents or one ATR in stocks), because the exact level is where the crowd's own orders cluster and get swept first.
  • His default target is 2R on stocks but 1.5R on forex, a difference he attributes to forex's higher volatility relative to typical stop size and which he has himself revised (to 1.6R) and denied is universal.
  • Pushing a target multiple higher than the tested rule — 3R or 4R chased out of regret — more often turns a clean win into a scratch or a loss than it captures the extra move.
  • His trading entries chase confirmed strength (buy-stop orders above a breakout, bidding a fill up toward the ask), while his investing entries do the opposite, buying in tranches at progressively lower support or valuation levels as price falls.
  • Winning trades are not always closed in one shot: he trails stops, stacks sequential moving-average targets, and scales out a half or a third at a time near a suspected top rather than exiting the whole position at once.
  • He has documented, on camera, waiting past his own stated 90-percent-of- premium closing rule on a falling Amazon spread, and separately stating an intent to let a losing GameStop put ride to expiry with no exit plan at all — both presented in his own words, not smoothed into tidy examples.
  • His stated thresholds for closing an options position are not unified: 90 percent of realized gain in one video, 50 percent of premium at risk in others, with no video reconciling the two.

Try this

He suggests marking out, on a chart you already hold or watch, exactly where you would place a stop using his buffer method — one ATR (or roughly 8-12 pips on forex, a few cents on a stock) beyond the nearest obvious support or resistance rather than directly on it — and then separately writing down the 2R (or 1.5R, if forex) target that his risk- multiple convention implies from that same stop distance, before checking whether the trade would still be worth taking at that risk-to-reward.

Chapter 11

Options: Selling Time for a Living

In this chapter: how he uses options as a premium seller rather than a directional bettor — the mechanics of covered calls, cash-secured puts, and credit spreads; the Greeks and implied volatility that price all of them; and the specific documented pattern that runs through his highest-conviction strategy — a headline win rate built on a structural loss he only avoids by actually doing the discretionary work of cutting early.

11.1 A premium seller, not a lottery-ticket buyer

Chapter 10 covered stop-loss and target mechanics for stock trades. Options work differently: risk is defined by the structure itself rather than by a stop order, and that changes what "managing a trade" means. He sets up the distinction early — buying a call option outright, he says, is like buying a lottery ticket: you win occasionally, but more often than not lose the whole premium back. He calls a plain bought call expensive on its own terms too, since the underlying has to move far enough, fast enough, just to reach breakeven.

Against that, he lists what he actually uses options for as an investor: hedging a portfolio like buying insurance, generating additional income by selling premium, and — the framing that recurs across the corpus — a third use that this chapter treats as his real center of gravity. He says plainly that non-directional, premium-selling strategies are his personal favorite and most of his own option use, specifically because he often has no firm view on which way a stock will go. He tells students to sell options constantly, but only in defined, hedged structures, never naked — the recurring instruction that separates his approach from simply writing options for whatever premium is on offer.

Editor's note — why selling options works differently than buying them

An option's price has two pieces: intrinsic value (what it would be worth if exercised right now) and extrinsic value (everything else, decaying to zero by expiry). A buyer needs the underlying to move enough, fast enough, to beat that decay. A seller collects the extrinsic value up front and profits if the option simply expires worthless or is closed early for less than it was sold for — time itself works for the position rather than against it, as long as the underlying stays on the seller's side of the strike. That asymmetry — many small, likely wins funded by decay, against a smaller number of larger losses when the underlying moves hard against the strike — is the mathematical shape behind every "high win rate" options strategy in this chapter, his own included.

11.2 The Greeks and implied volatility, in his own vocabulary

He teaches five Greeks by name — delta, gamma, vega, theta, and rho — setting rho aside because it only matters for very long-dated options. Delta he defines as the change in an option's price per one-dollar move in the underlying, so a delta of 0.8 gains roughly 80 cents per dollar the stock moves; call deltas run positive, put deltas negative, because puts move opposite to the stock. He gives the map across strikes: at-the-money sits at 0.5, out-of-the-money strikes sit below it, in-the-money strikes run from 0.5 up toward 1, and in practice you never actually see an exact 1 or minus 1. Crucially for the rest of this chapter, he treats delta as doubling as a probability: a 0.5 delta means roughly a 50 percent chance of finishing in the money, a 0.6 delta roughly 60 percent.

Gamma he calls "the delta of delta" — the rate at which delta itself changes per dollar move, or acceleration where delta is speed. It peaks at-the-money and shrinks as an option moves further in or out of the money, and it grows as expiration approaches, which he frames as bad for a time-decay-focused seller but good for a buyer if the underlying is moving their way. Theta is the daily decay rate — a theta of minus 0.05 loses five cents a day, weekends included, with the daily bleed accelerating as expiry nears. Vega measures how much premium moves per one percent change in implied volatility, and is larger the further out the expiration sits. In a spread, he notes, a bought leg's theta and a sold leg's theta run in opposite directions and largely cancel, which is part of why he prefers spreads to single-leg positions.

Implied volatility (IV) he defines as the market's own view of a stock's future volatility, since option prices are always forward-pricing an expectation. He works a concrete case: 20 percent implied volatility on a $50 stock means the market expects roughly a 20 percent move, up or down, within a year, with about 68 percent probability — one standard deviation. He tells students the number itself comes out of the Black-Scholes pricing model and to leave the underlying math to the mathematicians. Raw IV isn't enough on its own — he ranks it against the stock's own trailing year to get an IV percentile or IV rank, and treats an IV percentile above 50 as meaning options are expensive, which is when he prefers to sell rather than buy. In one walkthrough he runs a broker scanner filtered to a 52-week IV rank above 30 to surface stocks worth selling premium on, and separately treats his platform's "52-week IV rank" and another platform's "current IV percentile" as the same measurement under different names. He states a general preference for selling puts specifically when volatility is high, because that is when premiums are richest, and recommends selling cash-secured puts or credit spreads into a VIX spike for the same reason.

11.3 Covered calls: income against shares he already owns

A covered call means selling a call option against 100 shares he already holds. He frames the appeal for an investor plainly: sell calls every month on stocks already owned, collecting roughly 2 percent a month, which he annualizes to about 24 percent a year on top of the position. He normally selects the strike by delta, choosing 40 to 50 delta, and typically sells with around 30 days to expiration — in one demo picking a 33-day expiry specifically. He works a worked example on Intel: selling calls roughly every 40 days and annualizing the premium collected at 3.9 percent per period across about nine such periods a year comes to roughly 34.9 percent a year from premium alone if the sale is repeated. On McDonald's he describes letting his shares get called away at the 285 strike plus a $6.52 premium, for a net sale price of $291.52.

He frames the trade-off honestly rather than as pure upside. If a covered call finishes in the money, the shares are sold at the strike — he calls this a "win-win" when he was already happy to sell at that price, since he either keeps the premium outright or sells at strike-plus-premium, an even larger gain given his typically low cost basis. If the stock keeps rising past the strike, though, he still owns the risk of missing further gains, and he can always use the cash from the assigned sale to buy the shares back and start the strategy again.

That trade-off is not hypothetical for him. He discloses selling covered calls on some of his Meta shares, which then were called away — reducing his position size relative to what it could have been, and he calls this "stupid" in hindsight. The clearer, more costly version of the same mistake is Nvidia. He sold a covered call on Nvidia, bought around $150, at a $380 strike, and was assigned — his shares called away — right before an earnings beat sent the stock much higher. He names it directly as a costly mistake this year, selling covered calls on high-growth stocks like Nvidia and Meta and being forced to sell shares too early, missing the subsequent upside. The stated rule he draws from that experience is specific: never sell covered calls on high-growth stocks at all, restrict the strategy to more defensive, lower-growth names — he names Pepsi, Procter & Gamble, and Nike — because a growth stock can explode suddenly and a capped upside on it costs real money.

Editor's note — why a covered call caps upside but not downside

Selling a call against owned shares collects premium but obligates the seller to hand the shares over at the strike if the stock finishes above it — capping the position's upside at strike-plus-premium no matter how far the stock runs past that point. The downside is not capped at all: the shares can still fall the full distance, offset only by the (typically modest) premium collected. A covered call is therefore not a hedge against a large loss — it trades away tail upside for a small amount of steady income, which is exactly the trade that went against him on Nvidia when the tail event was a large earnings-driven rally rather than a crash.

11.4 Cash-secured puts: getting paid to wait for a price

A cash-secured put sells a put while holding the cash to buy 100 shares at the strike if assigned. He calls it a strategy that pays every month while waiting for a share price to fall to the level he actually wants to buy at, and describes the whole approach as "getting paid to wait" for the shares to come to him. He walks through the outcome tree: if the stock stays above the strike, the put expires worthless and he keeps the premium with no shares bought; if it falls below the strike and stays there, he is obligated to buy at the strike, with the premium received lowering his effective purchase price. He calls selling cash-secured puts on good companies a "win-win" for a value investor — he wins in every scenario, either premium or a discounted entry — while distinguishing that framing from trading, where the underlying stock actually has to collapse for him to lose.

Worked examples with real numbers recur across the corpus. On Coca-Cola he sold three contracts of the April 44 puts at an average $0.57, collecting about $170. On Lockheed Martin he sold a 360-strike put expiring July 10 for $5.49, collecting $549 on one contract, obligating him to buy 100 shares if price sat below 360 at expiry. On Google he sold a cash-secured put at the 88 strike, 45 days out, so that a close below 88 obligates a buy at 88 net of premium. On Apple he sold three contracts at the 165 strike for $296 total premium, explicitly to get paid while waiting for a lower entry to add shares. On an LVMH position he shows the put sold at $8 later trading at $2, an unrealized gain of $612 against a maximum possible profit of $1,000 if it expires worthless. He states a typical expiration window of 30 to 45 days as the "sweet spot" for this strategy.

He attributes the whole approach, notably, not to himself but to Warren Buffett — calling cash-secured puts Buffett's favorite strategy and the one he uses most, and defending the structure against the "naked put" label by pointing out the seller is prepared and funded to actually own the shares at the strike.

11.5 Credit spreads and the bull put spread extreme
