Once cash-secured puts require more capital than a trader has, he introduces the credit spread — the bull put spread — as the alternative: sell a put and buy a further out-of-the-money put to cap the risk, so the position needs only the margin on the strike-width rather than the full cash to buy shares. He defines the bullish and bearish versions plainly: a bull call spread buys a call and sells one higher; a bull put spread sells a put and buys one lower; the bear-side mirrors run the same logic in reverse.

His stated strike-selection convention for a standard credit spread runs around 20 to 30 delta on the short leg across several videos — 20 delta, 20-25 delta, 22-30 delta, 20-28 delta — treated as roughly the same working range rather than a single fixed number. He reads a short strike's delta directly as an approximate win probability: a 19-delta short strike implies about an 81 percent chance the spread finishes as a win; a 22-delta short put implies roughly a 78 percent win rate; a 24-delta short put on Tesla implies about 76 percent. He backs the delta-as-win-rate reading with outside evidence, citing tastytrade research over 12 years of data finding that selling puts at 30 delta carries about a 20 percent chance of losing money. His usual expiration window for these spreads is 30 to 45 days, and he sets a minimum credit requirement relative to strike width — at least $1 of credit for every $5 of distance between the strikes.

The strategy he names as his favorite of all — the "bull put spread extreme" — pushes the same structure to its edge. He defines it as selling far out-of-the-money put credit spreads on uptrending stocks at strong support levels, at a delta of 10. He states a 10 delta reads as roughly a 10 percent chance the stock finishes below that strike, which is exactly why he chooses it — it implies a 90 percent win rate. He calls it his favorite strategy overall, saying it generates most of his profits every single month, and states the 90 percent figure repeatedly and consistently across videos, including a claim that the win rate is high enough to enter even without waiting for a bullish candlestick confirmation first.

He is direct that the trade-off is real, not a free lunch: he describes it himself as a strategy that risks more to make less per winning trade in exchange for that high win rate, and states the general principle underneath all his premium-selling structures — a high win rate means risking more to get less, you cannot have both a high win rate and a favorable risk-to-reward at the same time.

The triad: headline win rate, structural max loss, discretionary cut

This is the pairing the chapter is built around, and the archive documents all three legs of it explicitly. First, the headline: a 90 percent win rate on the bull put spread extreme, stated repeatedly. Second, the structural loss that a 10-delta credit spread carries if simply held to expiry: because the position is a spread, not a naked put, the worst case is capped at the full width between strikes minus the credit received — but that cap is still the entire remaining premium at risk, not a small number, and he is explicit that letting a losing spread run to that full structural maximum is not what he actually does. Third, the discretionary exit that keeps the realized loss well below the structural one: he cuts the spread the moment price breaks the short strike or the stock's trend changes, so he says he never actually absorbs the full spread-width loss, and states his managed maximum loss runs around $1.40, with most losses actually closer to fifty cents to a dollar.

He gives at least two worked pairs of numbers that make the gap concrete. In one example he states a credit spread's structural maximum loss at $400 if left to expire worthless below the short strike — and, applying his own discretionary early-exit rule, the realized loss on that same trade came to only about $114, roughly 1.4 times the $82 target profit, far below the $400 structural figure. In a second, similar example on a Tesla-style spread, he states the structural max loss as $390 and says it should never actually happen, because his cut-loss rule is to close the trade immediately once price touches the sold strike rather than let it run to expiry. Both examples describe the same mechanism: the headline win rate is a property of the structure at entry, priced by delta; the structural max loss is a property of the structure if abandoned to fate; and the realized loss is a property of what he actually does in the middle, which is cut early.

That middle step is discretionary, not structural — nothing about the option contract itself enforces it. The book's Chapter 10 material on stop discipline is directly relevant here: he documents, on his own camera, waiting past a stated 90-percent-of-premium closing rule on a falling Amazon spread before finally closing it (see Chapter 10.7). The same temptation to let a good trade run "a bit longer" applies with more force on the loss side of a credit spread, where hesitating to cut is what turns a managed $1.40 loss into something closer to the structural maximum. He states the rule for cutting losses on this strategy in more than one place — never let the spread reach its structural loss, cut the moment the short strike is breached — but the archive gives no example of him narrating a bull-put-spread-extreme trade where he failed to cut on time, the way it does for the Amazon and GameStop cases in Chapter 10. The discipline is stated with unusual consistency here; whether it holds every single month across the ten-plus years the strategy has been running is not something the cards in this archive can independently verify — the 90 percent figure is his own claim about his own results, and self-reported returns are flagged throughout this book as a claim, not an established fact (see Chapter 1).

11.6 Debit spreads, iron condors, and the strategy map

Debit spreads sit on the buying side of the same mechanics: a bull call spread buys a call and sells a higher-strike call against it, using the premium received to lower the net cost and reduce theta exposure relative to a plain bought call. He worked one such spread at a $363 maximum loss against a $637 maximum profit, and a bear put spread the same way in reverse, buying a put and selling a lower-strike put, with the maximum loss defined as the net premium paid if the market never reaches the strikes. He states he trades debit spreads comparatively rarely, favoring credit spreads and cash-secured puts as his main strategies instead. For his own portfolio hedging on index futures, though, his stated preference flips to debit spreads specifically — bear put spreads and bull call spreads — rather than credit structures.

For sideways or genuinely uncertain markets he adds non-directional structures: the iron condor, which combines a bear call spread above the current price with a bull put spread below it, profiting as long as price stays inside both boundaries. He worked one iron condor on Alphabet with boundaries at 97 and 114, collecting a $1.90 credit per contract against an estimated $85 max loss, and a second on Viva with boundaries near 163-197 for a $220 credit against roughly $100 max loss. He lists iron condors alongside covered calls, diagonal spreads, and calendar spreads as his sideways-market toolkit.

He gives an explicit map from market view to structure: very bullish calls for a back ratio spread, moderately bullish a bull call spread, neutral-to-bullish a bull put spread extreme or cash-secured puts; on the bear side, neutral-to-bearish takes a bear call spread, moderately bearish a bear put spread, and a strong-collapse view a bearish synthetic or put back ratio.

His market viewStructure he maps to itSource
Very bullishBack ratio spread
Moderately bullishBull call spread (debit)
Neutral-to-bullishBull put spread extreme / cash-secured put
Neutral / sidewaysIron condor, diagonal or calendar spread, covered call
Neutral-to-bearishBear call spread
Moderately bearishBear put spread (debit)
Strong collapse viewBearish synthetic / put back ratio

11.7 Attribution: what he credits to Bang Pham Van

Several of the more elaborate structures discussed on the channel are not presented as his own invention. He repeatedly and explicitly credits a specific set of strategies to his business partner, whom he calls "the options genius" or "options grandmaster" — Bang Pham Van. He credits the Snipex and Snipex 4B strategies to Bang Pham Van directly, describing them as carrying a 90 percent win rate and allowing S&P index trading with as little as $100. He credits the "Snipe X" calendar put spread variant to Bang Pham Van rather than to himself, and the "bear bounce spread" — a synthetic short stock plus a protective call — the same way. He credits the inverse back ratio strategy entirely to Bang Pham Van, describing it as normally reserved for a paid subscriber community and taught to him rather than developed by him. He credits a specific technique for safely shorting Alibaba while still holding the long-term shares to Bang Pham Van by name, and states more broadly that a number of the proprietary structures taught in his paid options course are credited jointly to Bang Pham Van and himself, claiming these cannot be found in any textbook. He separately states Bang Pham Van generates 50 to 100 percent returns on his own portfolio consistently — a claim about a third party's results, reported here strictly as his claim.

The book's discipline throughout is to keep this material clearly attributed rather than folding it into "his method": Snipex, the calendar variants, the inverse back ratio, and the Alibaba hedge technique are Bang Pham Van's strategies as Khoo himself presents them, appearing on the channel because Khoo teaches them, not because he originated them. (Appendix D covers the broader question of co-trainer material excluded from this book on the same grounds.)

What he doesn't say about this

He never reconciles the two loss-cutting thresholds that recur across the options material — a 90-percent-of-realized-gain closing rule for winning spreads (Chapter 10.7) against a stop-loss-at-the-short-strike rule for losing ones stated here — into one unified "when do I close an options position" framework; the two rules address different situations, but no video puts them side by side. He states the bull put spread extreme's 90 percent win rate as a fact about his own trading in numerous videos but never discloses the underlying trade count, date range, or an independently auditable log behind that figure — as with his other self-reported track-record claims (Chapter 1), the number should be read as his stated claim about himself, not as verified performance. He does not address what happens to a credit spread's short strike during an overnight gap, when the discretionary "cut the moment price touches the strike" rule cannot be executed at the intended price. And while he names Bang Pham Van as the source of several named strategies, the archive gives no video where Bang Pham Van's own underlying win-rate or track-record claims for those strategies are independently examined rather than simply repeated.

Chapter summary

  • He uses options primarily as a premium seller, favoring non-directional and income-generating structures over directional bets, because he says he often has no strong view on which way a stock will move.
  • The five Greeks he teaches — delta, gamma, vega, theta, rho — price every structure in this chapter; delta doubles as an approximate win probability, which is the mechanism behind every stated "win rate" figure in the chapter.
  • Covered calls collect roughly 2%/month against shares he owns but cap upside at the strike — a trade-off that cost him real gains when Nvidia and Meta shares were called away right before further rallies, a mistake he names directly and now restricts the strategy to defensive names for.
  • Cash-secured puts are framed as "getting paid to wait" for a target buy price, attributed by him to Warren Buffett rather than to his own invention, with worked examples on Coca-Cola, Lockheed Martin, Google, and Apple.
  • His highest-conviction strategy, the bull put spread extreme, sells 10-delta credit spreads on uptrending stocks at support for a claimed 90 percent win rate — but that win rate is a property of the structure at entry, not a guarantee against the structural max loss the spread carries if held to expiry.
  • The gap between headline win rate and structural max loss is closed only by a discretionary rule he must actually execute — cutting the trade the moment the short strike is breached — and worked examples show realized losses (about $114 and effectively near-zero) running far below stated structural maximums ($400 and $390) when that rule is followed.
  • Several of the more elaborate structures on the channel — Snipex, the calendar spread variants, the inverse back ratio, and a specific Alibaba hedge — are explicitly credited by him to his business partner Bang Pham Van, not presented as his own invention.
  • He states the underlying trade-off of premium selling directly: a higher win rate means risking more to make less per winning trade, and he treats this as an unavoidable arithmetic fact rather than something a better strategy can escape.

Try this

He suggests picking a stock you already track, checking its current implied volatility percentile against its own one-year range, and then — if the percentile reads high — working out what a 20-25 delta credit spread would look like on it: the short strike's implied win probability, the credit collected, the strike width, and the structural maximum loss that width implies, before separately writing down at what price you would actually cut the position early under his rule of exiting the moment the short strike is touched.

Chapter 12

Shorting, Bear Markets, and Crashes

In this chapter: what Khoo does when a trend turns down — the rules that govern when he will and won't short, the four-step plan he runs on his investment portfolio during a bear market, his seven-sign bubble checklist, and the honest limits of all three, stated in his own words.

12.1 Why he bothers learning to short at all

Chapter 3 covered how Khoo thinks about the market's cycle in the abstract. This chapter covers what he actually does once the cycle turns down — first as a short-term trader, then as an investor managing a portfolio through a bear market, and finally as someone trying to recognize a bubble before it bursts.

His starting premise is that direction shouldn't matter to a trader's ability to make money. He says there is no good or bad market, only a bullish one or a bearish one, because a falling market is exactly as usable as a rising one if you know how to be short. He states his motive for learning the skill directly: he wants to make money every single month and every single year, which is only possible if he can also profit when prices fall, not just when they rise. He frames it as a straightforward expansion of opportunity — shorting doubles the number of setups available to him, because he can now profit from the down legs of a chart as well as the up legs, and he adds a claim about the population of stocks worth trading: in his view, for every one company whose fundamentals are genuinely improving, roughly ten are deteriorating, so restricting yourself to buying only leaves you fishing in the smaller pond.

He also reframes what a crash actually is: the money an investor loses does not vanish, he says, it moves from the account of whoever held the falling asset into the account of whoever was short it — a psychological reframe as much as a trading one, turning a crash from pure loss into a transfer worth being on the right side of.

He names three practical routes to profiting from a decline — selling a stock short outright, buying put options, or buying an inverse ETF, an instrument he defines as engineered to move opposite the index. In earlier videos he names the inverse ETFs he used himself: DOG to short the Dow 30, DNO to short oil, GLL to short gold, applying the same 50/150 moving-average crossover rule from Chapter 8 to the inverse instrument itself. By a later video his stated preference has shifted: he no longer uses inverse ETFs, because their risk-to-return profile is a flat one-to-one — a dollar made if the market falls, a dollar lost if it rises — and he'd rather structure a trade where the potential return exceeds the risk.

Editor's note — a loss is a "transfer" only between two counterparties

True for a specific buyer and short seller of the same shares, but a less complete picture of a crash overall: a falling market also destroys value no short seller ever collected — a retirement account that stays invested and falls has transferred nothing to anyone; the future earnings estimate behind the price has simply dropped.

12.2 The rule everything else sits under: never short a strong trend

Before any setup, entry trigger, or stop-loss placement, one rule recurs often enough to function as the chapter's spine: he does not short into strength. Responding to a market strategist publicly calling for shorts on the S&P 500, his answer is simply "never short against the trend". He gives it the same 45-degree/90-degree shape Chapter 3 used for market tops: a trend climbing at roughly 45 degrees is healthy and tends to keep going, so he won't short it; only once the slope steepens toward 90 degrees, a parabolic and unsustainable move, does he consider shorting the eventual reversal. He holds the line even when he personally believes a market is overvalued: discussing a market he thought near a top around a P/E of 21, he still wouldn't short while price kept rising, because no one can predict the exact turn — he waits for the trend to actually reverse. Years later, describing a mistake he says he learned from, he restates it almost identically: never short the market in a confirmed bull market, defined mechanically as price above a rising 200-day average, or the 50-day average above the 150-day — and warns that shorting is especially dangerous while price sits above rising 20-, 50-, and 200-day averages making higher highs and higher lows, since that's fighting the trend rather than riding it.

He allows one narrow exception: traders like himself will, on occasion, short an overbought condition inside an uptrend as a short-term counter-trend trade — with its own warning attached, since if the position isn't closed in time, price will hit support and the underlying uptrend will resume, running straight through the short.

There's a mechanical reason the rule holds even for traders who reject it. When enough short sellers are positioned against a rising market, a rally can force them to buy back shares to cap their losses, and that forced buying itself adds upward pressure — a short squeeze feeding the very rally they bet against. The asymmetry follows directly: on a downtrend, going short is the safer trade; on an uptrend, the reverse is true — what determines a position's risk isn't long versus short, but whether it runs with or against the prevailing trend.

12.3 Five setups, and the trigger inside each one

Beyond the standing rule about trend direction, Khoo names five specific situations he treats as offering the lowest risk and the highest potential reward for a short.

Deteriorating fundamentals. He shorts fundamentally weak companies showing declining margins, lost market share, slowing sales, and unsustainable debt — Michael Kors, where revenue rose while net income fell, a divergence he treats as a warning sign, and Groupon, whose losses were visible before its roughly 73 percent decline.

An uptrend reversing into a downtrend. He shorts at the start of the new downtrend once the reversal confirms, mirroring the long-side exit logic from Chapter 10: sell to close the long, then open the short.

A rally within an existing downtrend. The setup he returns to most, and the inverse of "buy the pullback": he never shorts at the bottom, since price near a low tends to bounce and take out a stop, and instead waits for a rally into resistance and shorts the moment it fails. He plots the 50-, 100-, 150-, and 200-day moving averages as layered resistance for that rally, citing JC Penney rallying repeatedly into its 150-day average as a worked example. A broken support level becomes resistance the same way, and some traders wait for the retrace back up to it before shorting the failure.

A breakdown out of a consolidation. He requires the range's support to have been tested at least three times before treating a break as shortable, with the stop placed just above that now-resistance level.

An overbought peak at the top of an uptrend. He shorts the retrace once a rising trend looks stretched — but warns that shorting purely because an oscillator reads "overbought" is an amateur mistake, since a strong trend can stay overbought a long time before it turns.

Candlestick confirmation sits alongside all five setups rather than replacing them: the bearish pin bar, tweezer top, one-black-crow, and evening star are mirror images of the bullish patterns from Chapter 9, most reliable at a resistance level on an already-falling trend. For a counter-trend short, his overbought confirmation combines stochastics above 80 with price at the upper Bollinger band, requiring either two of his three standard indicators bearish, or one plus a confirming bearish candle.

12.4 Risk on a short: the asymmetry he manages deliberately

A short has a structural feature a long doesn't: the loss is theoretically unbounded, since a share price can in principle keep rising indefinitely, while a long's downside is capped at the capital put in. Khoo states this plainly and repeatedly, giving it a Buffett framing in one video: Buffett avoids shorting even stocks he expects to fall because the trade's maximum gain is capped at 100 percent — the stock going to zero — while the loss side is open-ended.

His stated response is not to avoid shorting but to manage the asymmetry directly. A short must always carry a stop loss, without exception, and he adds a practical (not a substitute) backstop: a broker will forcibly buy back the position once losses exceed the account's capital — "the stockbroker is more afraid of you than you are of him". His own rule is to set the stop before entry, typically just above the moving averages he's treating as resistance.

He's blunt about a case where his own practice didn't hold up: he admits he used to short heavily, made money on individual trades, but gave the gains back over the long run — today he shorts mainly for entertainment, using options for defined risk rather than the underlying shares. That admission tracks his stated general preference for options over a naked short: a direct short can gap past a stop-loss order at the next available price, while an option's maximum loss is capped at the premium paid — options, he says, add "a silencer and grenade launcher" to the basic rifle of a short. He also tracks borrow cost, generally 0.1 to 5 percent a year, up to 8 percent for illiquid names, and won't short a stock whose fee exceeds about 3 percent; in Singapore, where borrow costs run around 6 percent, he avoids the underlying shares and uses other instruments. Direct short selling itself is available in Singapore, the US, Hong Kong, and Japan, but not Malaysia or Indonesia.

12.5 What makes a bear market, and what he actually does inside one

Khoo defines a bear market mechanically, as Chapter 8 defines an uptrend or downtrend: a drop of at least 20 percent from the prior high. Below that, a decline of roughly 3 to 15 percent is a normal, healthy retracement inside an intact uptrend, not a trend change.

Not every bear market is the same size or danger, and he draws a repeated distinction between the two kinds. Citing data he attributes to Pension Partners rather than his own research, he says that across the 90 years since 1929 there have been 21 bear markets, 11 of them — 52 percent — accompanied by a recession, with non-recessionary bear markets averaging a 29 percent decline against 42 percent for recessionary ones. Duration follows the same split: three months to 31 months historically, averaging about 12 months without a recession and 17 with one, with an overall average drawdown of 36 percent, ranging from 20 percent up to over 54 percent in a major crisis. He names the milder category a "baby bear market" — short, non-recessionary, businesses still fine — and says he expected exactly that going into the late-2018 decline, a 20-to-30 percent baby bear rather than a full 50 percent one, reasoning the yield curve hadn't yet inverted and even a mid-year inversion would put the earliest plausible recession a year out. Elsewhere he cites an unnamed-source statistic that even after inversion, the market has on average kept rising roughly six more months before peaking — an inversion is a warning, not an immediate trigger.

He states the sharper, non-price-based test he leans on to call a bear market "real" rather than a correction: whether corporate operating earnings are declining, not just growing more slowly. He points to earnings turning outright negative in the dot-com crash, 2008, and the 2020 recession, against a period he discusses where earnings were still positive and growing — just slower — arguing that distinction, not a headline GDP figure or a price drawdown alone, is what separates a genuine bear market from a scare.

Editor's note — why "operating earnings negative" is a stricter bar

A 20 percent price drop can happen for reasons unrelated to profitability — a sentiment shock, a rate-driven valuation reset. Operating earnings move more slowly and reflect what businesses actually collect from customers, so a genuine decline there is a harder, lagging confirmation the economy is contracting rather than that the same earnings stream has simply been repriced at a lower multiple. Earnings data is reported quarterly and revised afterward, so this test necessarily confirms well after the price-based threshold has already fired — it's a check on severity after the fact, not an early-warning tool.

Bear markets, in his account, don't end because of good news — they end because bad news arrives and the market simply refuses to fall any further, evidence he reads as panic sellers exhausted and only institutional buyers remaining. One dated recovery marker he offers: in every bear market he says he's studied across the last century, once the market recovers more than 55 percent of its losses, it has never gone on to make a new low — only new all-time highs. He hedges the rule immediately: a new low after a 55 percent recovery would simply be a first, and a severe enough shock — he names a second pandemic wave as a live possibility at the time — could still do it.

He's candid about a timing approach that didn't hold up under his own testing: backtesting a moving-average exit-and-re-entry strategy across the last 11 bear markets, he found it left a trader better off in only 2 cases and worse off in 9, a result he reports plainly rather than reworking to look better in hindsight, though he doesn't detail the backtest's methodology. He separately warns against selling and waiting to rebuy on different grounds: protracted, multi-year bear markets happen only about once every twenty years, while most last one to four months, so a seller risks buying back higher once the reversal comes — illustrated by the 2018 bear market, a three-month decline where a moving-average re-entry signal would have triggered a buyback above the original sale price.

Both "21 bear markets" and "2 of 11" are small samples in the statistical sense — swap one or two borderline episodes in or out of either count and the percentages move meaningfully. That doesn't make the figures wrong; like Chapter 3's base rates, they're historical color that shapes expectations, not precise probabilities to size a trade against.

For an investment portfolio, as opposed to a trading account, he lays out a four-step bear-market plan. Step one: diversify into genuinely good companies, trimming weak holdings and weighting toward businesses resilient through a recession. Step two: treat the decline as opportunity — look for strong companies at a discount, funding the buying by spending less and saving more, adding at identified support levels rather than calling the exact bottom. Step three, aimed at the trader rather than the buy-and-hold side of the same portfolio: since even excellent companies can keep falling another 10 to 20 percent even though they'll recover long term, use options to profit from that near-term decline rather than sit through it passively. On whether to sell at all, he narrows the legitimate reasons sharply — low-quality or overvalued holdings, where a long recession could genuinely put the business under — and treats selling a sound company purely because the market fell as a mistake outside that list.

12.6 Recognizing a bubble: the seven-sign checklist

Bear markets are one hazard; bubbles are the setup that precedes the sharpest of them, and Khoo organizes his approach into seven named warning signs distilled from a set of reference cases: Dutch tulip mania in 1637, the South Sea bubble of 1720, Japanese property and stocks in the 1980s, the dot-com bubble of 2000, and US real estate leading into 2008. He defines a bubble as the price of an asset rising to unsustainable levels far exceeding its fundamental value, driven by speculative demand — buying purely to sell to someone else at a higher price rather than for what the asset is worth — a mechanism he names the greater fool theory and compares to a Ponzi scheme: it works only as long as a "greater fool" keeps showing up to buy at the next price.

Sign one — overvaluation. Price running far beyond fundamental intrinsic value to an unsustainable level; he compares it to a rubber band stretched too far and too fast, which snaps back. For stocks he puts a rough number on it — merely "expensive" above intrinsic value, a bubble once running 50 to 100 percent above it — and for housing, when monthly mortgage payments run three to four times the rent the same property could collect.

Sign two — "this time is different." Participants telling themselves the normal rules of valuation no longer apply, as in dot-com's replacement of profit and revenue with "eyeballs" and "clicks": Yahoo at a P/E of 1,062, AOL at 217, Pets.com at a price-to-sales ratio of 646, Internet Capital Group at 800 while still losing money.

Sign three — the "auntie indicator." When relatives with no financial background start texting him about buying whatever asset is running, he treats a crash as likely near — retail arrives at maximum optimism only after heavy media coverage, exactly when professional money is already selling into it; a related, earlier stage is amateurs swapping tips driven by fear of missing out.

Sign four — sensationalized media, turning ordinary people into overnight-millionaire stories. Sign five — rising leverage, speculators using borrowed money and margin, which he says has fueled every bubble in his reference set, from dot-com margin debt to 2007–2008 housing over-leverage to the borrowing behind the 2020 bond-market run. The psychology is a false sense of security — once prices have only risen, borrowing to amplify the position feels safe rather than risky; widespread margin buying is itself a further top signal for the same reason.

Sign six — dominance of everyday conversation, at work and at parties, with people talking about quitting their jobs over it — a sign he turns into a direct question: right now, what's the one asset everyone keeps bringing up?. Sign seven — skeptics dismissed as people who "just don't get it". What makes the combination dangerous, he sums up, is that people inside a bubble don't realize they're in one — hypnotized by the music, always finding a fresh justification, recognizing it only once it's too late.

The checklist has a technical companion using the same 45-degree/90-degree language from Section 12.2: a healthy uptrend rises around 45 degrees, and a bubble typically finishes near-vertical, close to 90 degrees — extreme greed rather than sustainable demand. He points to gold, the TLT Treasury ETF, and the ARK innovation ETF as recent examples that ran 45 degrees before going parabolic and falling, and separately flagged cloud computing, ARK's holdings, alternative energy, and EVs as running too far too fast — while qualifying that the underlying industries have genuinely strong long-term futures; his objection is to the pace of the near-term move, not the businesses.

12.7 The honest caveat: double climaxes and a pattern that resists disproof

Khoo does not let the checklist stand as a clean, mechanical top-calling tool, and the caveats he attaches to it are worth reading as carefully as the seven signs themselves.

The first caveat concerns the parabolic 90-degree move that Section 12.6 treats as the technical confirmation of a bubble's late stage. He notes the pattern can happen more than once before the real top forms — a parabolic run, a fall, then a second parabolic climax, a "double climax," before the actual decline begins. He extends the same idea to counter-trend shorts generally: after a parabolic move plus a powerful reversal candlestick, an experienced trader can take a counter-trend short, but frames even that as a short-term, high-skill exception rather than a reliable top call.

The second caveat is broader and, in his own words, the central limitation of the whole exercise: you can know a bubble will eventually burst, but never when — and no matter how high a price looks, it can always go meaningfully higher first. He applies that to a live call, not just a historical one: discussing Bitcoin, he states flatly that he considers it to be in a bubble, and in the same breath concedes it could still rise another 100 percent before it turns. He states directly why he doesn't short an asset he believes overvalued: expensive can always become more expensive, and identifying the exact top is the actual problem the checklist doesn't solve — consistent with the standing rule from Section 12.2 that he does not short a trend that is still climbing.

He is explicit about what the checklist is actually for, and it's narrower than top-timing: the value of studying past bubbles, in his own framing, is that the patterns recur, so recognizing them lets a trader stay careful and manage risk — not that recognizing them lets anyone call the exact top. He also names the diagnostic's blind spot directly: bubbles are genuinely hard to recognize from the inside, and mostly become obvious only in hindsight.

Editor's note — why "it can double-climax" makes the checklist hard to falsify in real time

This is the same structural problem Chapter 3 found in the bottom-calling indicators, applied to the top side of the cycle. A sign like "media sensationalizing extraordinary returns" is a testable, in-the-moment observation. But once the framework also allows a first climax, a pullback, and a second climax before the "real" top, almost any sequence becomes retroactively consistent with it: a pullback that resumes upward reads as "the first climax before the double," and a move that keeps going without pulling back reads as "still building toward it." No observation, in principle, rules the framework out ahead of time — the philosopher Karl Popper's definition of an unfalsifiable claim. That doesn't make the underlying pattern unreal — double-topping is a documented feature of real bubbles, including the dot-com top, which formed across a series of lower highs from March to September 2000 rather than one vertical spike. It means the checklist should be read exactly as Khoo frames it: a prompt for caution, not a signal that tells you, in the moment, when the top has actually arrived.

What he doesn't say

He does not quantify a win rate or historical hit rate for any of the five short setups in Section 12.3 — they're given as rules and worked single examples, not backtested statistics. He does not reconcile his two stated positions on inverse ETFs — the tickers he names using himself in earlier material against his later preference to avoid them entirely — beyond what the dates imply about a changed view over time, nor does he revisit his own admission of giving back short-selling profits over the long run with detail on which setups cost him the money. On bear markets, he never states how a reader should combine the 20-percent price threshold, the yield-curve lead-time statistic, and the operating-earnings test into one decision about whether a given decline is a correction or something larger — the three are given separately, without an instruction for weighing them against each other. And on bubbles, he never states how many of the seven signs need to be present before he personally treats an asset as being in a bubble rather than merely expensive — it's a watch-list, not a scored test with a threshold.

Chapter summary

  • Khoo learns to short because he wants to profit in both directions of the market, not only on the way up, and treats a bear-market loss as a transfer of wealth to whoever is positioned short rather than a pure destruction of value.
  • The rule that governs every short setup is the same: never short a strong, still-climbing trend — he waits for a 45-degree uptrend to break down into something closer to a 90-degree parabolic reversal, or for a confirmed trend change, before shorting.
  • His five lowest-risk short setups are deteriorating fundamentals, a confirmed uptrend-to-downtrend reversal, a failed rally within an existing downtrend, a consolidation breakdown, and an overbought retrace at the top of an uptrend — each with its own specific entry trigger.
  • Because a short's loss is theoretically unlimited, he treats a stop-loss as mandatory on every short, prefers defined-risk options over the naked position when practical, and by his own admission gave back short-selling gains over the long run before shifting toward that more defined-risk approach.
  • He defines a bear market as a 20 percent drop from the high, splits the historical record into recessionary and non-recessionary bear markets with different average depths and durations (drawn from a source he credits rather than his own research), and treats operating earnings turning negative as the sharper bar separating a real bear market from a scare.
  • His four-step bear-market plan for an investment portfolio is: diversify into resilient, high-quality companies; buy the discount at identified support levels; use options to manage the possibility of further near-term decline; and sell only low-quality or overvalued holdings, not sound businesses caught in the general fall.
  • His seven bubble warning signs — extreme overvaluation, "this time is different" reasoning, the "auntie indicator," sensationalized media coverage, rising leverage, dominance of everyday conversation, and dismissal of skeptics — are drawn from historical episodes from tulip mania through the 2008 housing crash.
  • He states the checklist's honest limit himself: a bubble's parabolic top can repeat as a "double climax" before the real decline starts, and no matter how overextended a price looks, it can always go higher before it turns — which is why he uses the framework for caution rather than as a tool for calling the exact top.

Try this

Pick one asset currently dominating conversation among people you know who don't normally discuss markets — Khoo's own "auntie indicator" test — and check it against all seven signs from Section 12.6, not just the one that prompted the check: overvaluation, "this time is different" reasoning, rising leverage, and skeptics being mocked rather than answered. Write down what you find without acting on it — the exercise, in his own framing, is for building the habit of noticing, not for producing a signal to trade.

Chapter 13

Forex: A Second Book, Under-Documented

In this chapter: what he trades forex for, the mechanics and setups he's put on video, and the one clear place where his own numbers for forex disagree with the numbers he teaches for stocks — an inconsistency he states himself rather than one this book is imposing on him.

13.1 Why forex, alongside everything else

Forex is one leg of a portfolio he describes running in parallel with long-term investing, stock swing trading, and options: a long-term investment portfolio, daily stock trading, intraday forex trading, and options income, all at once. He states his own division of labor plainly — he day trades forex, swing trades stocks, and invests in stocks — treating each as a distinct activity with distinct rules. Forex intraday trading, he says, is a small proportion of his overall portfolio that supplements income rather than anchoring it.

The appeal he states explicitly is structural, not that forex is easier. It's the biggest market in the world — he cites roughly three trillion dollars traded daily against roughly 500 billion for the entire stock market, which gives the liquidity to enter and exit quickly. It's a 24-hour market five days a week, so it fits around a day job, or around the hours the stock market is closed. He frames this as complementary rather than a replacement: he trades stocks when the stock market is open and forex when it isn't, so there's always something to trade — he compares it, half-jokingly, to a fast-food chain that stays open around the clock. He needs less than a million dollars in cash to take a position of that size because of leverage, which he says is the only reason the returns he describes are possible at all.

He also names a caution here that his stock-market chapters don't carry in the same form: less than 10 percent of people make money trading forex, and 90 percent will lose money even with the best courses, books, and strategies available. That statement sits in the same videos as his own results claims, and the book treats it the way it treats his other self-reported numbers — as his claim, not as independent verification.

Editor's note — why forex draws so many undercapitalized beginners

Forex brokers routinely offer leverage of 50:1, 100:1, or higher on retail accounts, which lets someone open a position with a few hundred dollars that would otherwise require tens of thousands. That accessibility is also why the failure rate he cites is unsurprising: the same leverage that makes forex approachable on a small account also multiplies the damage of an undersized stop or an oversized position. His own account-sizing procedure below is the mechanism that keeps leverage from doing that damage — the arithmetic exists specifically because the market invites people to skip it.

13.2 The mechanics: pips, lots, spreads

Forex quoting has its own vocabulary, and he's put a fair amount of pure-mechanics content on the channel — more procedural than strategic. In a pair, the first currency is the base currency and the second is the quote currency; the base currency always carries a value of one. Every pair except those involving the Japanese yen is quoted to four decimal places, so one pip equals 0.0001; yen pairs are quoted differently, to two decimal places. A fifth decimal place, where brokers show one, is a fractional pip and he says it's too small to matter.

Position size is denominated in lots: one standard lot is 100,000 units of the base currency, a mini lot is 10,000, a micro lot is 1,000, and a nano lot is 100. Profit or loss works out to pips captured, multiplied by lots traded, multiplied by the dollar value per pip per standard lot. For most actively traded pairs — he names EURUSD, GBPUSD, and AUDUSD — that dollar value per pip is usually about $10 per standard lot. He notes the exact value drifts and should be checked against a pip-value table rather than assumed. Worked example: one standard lot of EURUSD bought at 1.3510 and sold at 1.3550 captures 40 pips, or $400 at $10 a pip. Without leverage, $400 on a $135,100 position is a 0.29 percent return — which he offers as the reason every forex trader uses leverage, not as an incidental detail.

His account-sizing formula, stated directly: lot size equals net liquidation value times risk percent per trade, divided by stop-loss distance in pips times the dollar value per pip per standard lot. Worked example: a $10,000 account risking 1 percent with a 20-pip stop and $10-per-pip value gives 0.5 lots, or 50,000 euros of EURUSD exposure.

Two mechanical points he's explicit about, both easy to get wrong on a live platform:

  • Forex orders trigger off the bid and ask, not off the last transacted price the way stock orders do. You always buy at the ask and sell at the bid, and the ask is always higher than the bid — that gap is the spread, and the instant you buy and sell you've paid it. A candlestick's charted price on MetaTrader 4 is the bid; the ask is hidden until activated, which he offers as the explanation for orders filling "before the candle got there" — the ask line hit it first, even though the visible candle hadn't. He dismisses the broker-conspiracy explanation for this outright, arguing that profitable traders use the same brokers as unprofitable ones. He says spreads of a pip or less aren't worth worrying about; the trouble starts once a spread reaches one to five pips. Liquid pairs like EURUSD and USDJPY carry very thin spreads; more exotic pairs like EURNZD or GBPNZD can run two or three pips.
  • He avoids holding a position through the daily broker rollover, when spreads widen — 5 p.m. Eastern for many brokers, 5 a.m. Singapore time in his own frame of reference — and says he always exits before then.

13.3 Sessions and pair selection

He maps the trading day by session, in Singapore time, which he says to flip for Eastern time: Sydney opens around 6 a.m. and activates the Australian dollar; Tokyo runs roughly 8 a.m. to 4 p.m. and activates the yen; London opens around 4 p.m. and activates the euro and the pound; New York runs 9 p.m. to 5 a.m. Singapore time, and traders working the most liquid pairs, like EURUSD, typically start watching from around 4 p.m. onward as London and New York overlap. Spreads run thinnest during the London and New York opens and can widen outside those active windows. Before trading, his morning routine starts with Forex Factory to check for scheduled major news; if something significant is due, he says he steps away from his normal strategy for that window and trades a dedicated news-scalping strategy instead.

He organizes his charting platform by currency rather than by pair — one saved MetaTrader 4 profile per currency, so a single click opens every chart involving that currency at once. He trades the eight major currencies and their pairs, avoiding exotics — he names Zimbabwe and Singapore dollars as examples of pairs he stays away from — and says the most successful traders confine themselves to the seven most common, most liquid pairs, which carry the highest volume, greatest volatility, and tightest spreads.

His central pair-selection tool, which recurs across multiple videos, is a currency strength meter — a proprietary tool built by his company and given free to course students. The rule stated flatly: buy the strongest currencies and sell the weakest, at the same time. On the meter, a reading of 60 or above marks a currency as strong (shown green), below 40 marks it weak (red), and the band between is neutral (yellow) — he only wants to trade the green against the red, avoiding neutral entirely. He warns that currency strength is timeframe-dependent — a currency strong on the hourly chart can be weak on a lower one — so there's no single "correct" timeframe to read it on. His stated process: read strength on the four-hour or hourly chart, then drop to a lower timeframe — the 15-minute is named repeatedly — to hunt the actual entry.

He explicitly avoids pairing two strong currencies or two weak currencies against each other. EURGBP, with both legs strong, is his named example of a pair that fights itself and goes sideways; CADJPY, with both legs weak, is his example of the mirror case. Applied to the roughly 49 pairs the eight majors produce, the strength filter typically narrows the day's watchlist to around six focus pairs — though he's explicit that six was simply that particular day's reading, not a fixed target.

A second, related filter is cross-pair correlation. Before taking a trade he wants to see the other pairs of both currencies involved moving in agreement — all of a currency's pairs showing the same direction gives him more confidence the whole currency is moving, not just the one chart in front of him. He'll still take the trade if only one of the two currencies shows that internal agreement, but not if neither does. If he ever holds two positions at once, he requires they not be correlated — his example is long EURUSD and short USDJPY, both of which are bearish bets on the dollar and would double the same exposure rather than diversify it.

13.4 The trend filter: 18/50/200, not 20/40 or 50/150

His forex moving-average setup is a distinct convention from the ones documented for stocks elsewhere in this book. Where the stock chapters describe 20/40 and 50/150 crossover pairs, his forex material uses a three-line stack: the 18, 50, and 200 moving averages, with the 18 and 50 exponential and the 200 simple. He states this directly as a course-level distinction: the advanced forex strategies use mostly the 18, 50, and 200, not the 6-period average that features heavily in the impulse-pullback and impulse-pin-bar strategies of his basic course.

A clear uptrend, on this filter, is the 18 above the 50, the 50 above the 200, with all three sloping upward. A clear downtrend is the mirror — 200 on top, then 50, then 18, all sloping down — which he demonstrates on USD/CHF 15-minute candles. Consolidation is read the opposite way: the averages moving sideways and crossing back and forth over each other rather than stacking in sequence, which he contrasts with the clean stacked arrangement he calls a "rainbow".

He also treats the moving averages as dynamic support and resistance once a trend is established, describing price on an uptrend repeatedly bouncing off the 18-period average as "surfing the 18 moving average" — his cue to wait for a pullback to the average and enter on the bounce rather than chase the move. The same idea generalizes across a currency's whole family: different currencies find support at different moving averages at different times — sometimes the 18, sometimes the 50 — and he says trend, support levels, and candlestick patterns aren't sufficient on their own; strength has to agree too.

13.5 The setups he names

He names a small, consistent set of forex strategies across the videos, rather than a large or shifting one. Taken together with the "normal" label he applies to three of them, this reads as a fairly settled toolkit rather than something still being iterated in public:

  • Bull flag / impulse pullback. His primary forex strategy, which he describes as "surfing off the moving averages". After the moving averages signal a new short-term trend, he does not enter at the very start of the move; he waits for a pullback. A worked long example: enter 0.9770 with an 8-pip stop at 0.9769 — sized so the 8 pips equals 2 percent of capital — and a 12-pip profit target. He says he found 1.5R to be the optimum target specifically for this strategy on 15-minute candles.
  • Impulse pin bar. After a moving-average crossover, a pin bar takes out the stop losses of traders positioned the wrong way, "fooling" them, before the real move begins.
  • Trend continuation. A pullback entry in the direction of an established trend; he says this strategy alone can get him roughly five to seven R a month, describing himself as "pretty daring" on it, risking up to five percent of capital per trade. He walked one such trade live on AUDJPY.
  • Moving-average bounce. A currency on a strong trend tends to retrace and bounce off its moving averages; the strategy goes long on pairs in a strong uptrend and short on pairs in a strong downtrend as they bounce. He's explicit this doesn't happen every time and isn't always reliable, which is why he teaches criteria for when to trust a bounce and when not to. Stated numbers: 10 to 15 setups a month, a profit ratio around 1:2, on 15-minute candles, working across every currency pair. A prerequisite is confirming the wave structure first — without the right swing-high/swing-low structure, he says the bounce isn't tradeable.
  • Range trading. In sideways, choppy markets: buy at the bottom of a range, at a demand zone or support level, and sell at the top, at a supply zone or resistance level. He anticipates the obvious objection — that support and resistance sometimes just break — and frames the method as being about avoiding the trades where that's likely, not eliminating the risk entirely. He trades the range with a directional bias rather than mechanically both ways: with a long bias, only buy at support; with a short bias, only sell at resistance. Stated frequency and payoff: 10 to 15 setups a month, risk-to-return of roughly 1:1.5 to 1:2, working across all forex pairs.
  • News scalping. Not his own invention — he credits an old friend and colleague, Thomas, with the original idea, which they later refined together. The approach: enter the instant a scheduled release (interest rates, GDP, non-farm payrolls) hits, exit within roughly 60 seconds to a few minutes. He does not attempt to predict which direction the release will move price. Mechanically, he straddles the news — a buy stop 12 pips above the current price and a sell stop 12 pips below it, placed exactly three minutes before the release, no earlier and no later — and once one side triggers, cancels the other. Rather than a fixed target, he trails the stop up toward the current price while the move is running, to capture as much of the spike as possible. A worked example: risking 12 pips to make 58, which he calls roughly 1R to 4.8R in about 60 seconds; he notes some news trades risk 12 pips for as much as 120 in under two minutes. He acknowledges many traders lose money on this approach because price can spike one way on release and then reverse hard the other.
  • Fibonacci confluence. Presented as a supporting tool, not a standalone method: in an uptrend, price tends to retrace to the 0.618 level or, failing that, at least the 0.5 level, before the next impulsive move. He's explicit that Fibonacci by itself isn't reliable enough and has to line up with a candlestick pattern, a moving average, or a horizontal support level before he'll act on it. A retracement beyond 61.8 percent tells him the trend may already be broken, and he calls that setup risky enough to skip.

He is equally explicit about what he doesn't trade: breakout entries — buying the break of resistance or shorting the break of support — are "not his cup of tea" in forex, and he says outright that he doesn't use them, distinguishing himself from traders who do. The broken-resistance retest buy (and its mirror, the broken-support retest short) gets the same treatment — a strategy other traders use that he says he doesn't use that often.

Entry discipline that applies across these setups: never chase price. If it's already moved without you, stated flatly for both directions — long or short — the instruction is to let it go, not to jump in late. He justifies waiting for a pullback fill on the grounds that forex "doesn't move in a straight line" — price fluctuates on the way to any target, which is what a limit order at a retracement level is there to exploit.

13.6 The R-target inconsistency, in his own words

This is the spine of the chapter, and it's worth stating exactly as he states it rather than smoothing it into consistency it doesn't have.

He teaches a 2R profit target for stock trading elsewhere on the channel. For forex, he says directly that he uses 1.5R instead — not 2R — and names the reason himself: forex prices move more erratically than stocks, and a 1:2 target simply doesn't get hit often enough, which drags down his win rate if he insists on it. He repeats the same comparison elsewhere, framing it as "1.5R for forex" against "1 to 2 for stocks," again citing forex's greater volatility and the win-rate cost of holding out for a bigger multiple. And separately, describing the impulse-pullback bull flag specifically, he says 1.5R is what he's found through experience to be the optimum target on that strategy, on 15-minute candles.

This is a genuine, acknowledged inconsistency, not an interpretive stretch by this book. He teaches one risk-reward convention for stocks and a smaller one for forex, and states his reasoning for the gap in his own words. It's worth reading the other numbers around it as the practical texture of what "1.5R" means across his stated forex setups, rather than as a second, competing rule: the range strategy runs 1:1.5 to 1:2; the moving-average bounce runs roughly 1:2; the impulse-pullback worked example is an 8-pip stop against a 12-pip target, which is exactly 1.5R; and the Trend Rider strategy he later teaches his student community risks 1R to make 1.4R, close to but not identical with the 1.5R figure, with 1R stated in that example as $400. None of these contradict the 1.5R-vs-2R claim; they're simply the range of concrete numbers that sit under it. He also, separately and outside this specific R-target framing, describes a cautionary example — not his own trading — of a forex trader running a 90 percent win rate on a 100-pip stop against a 15-to-20-pip target, which inverts the risk-reward relationship entirely; he offers it as a warning about how a high win rate can mask a structurally bad trade, not as a method he endorses.

He also names a rough win rate for his own core forex strategies — the impulse pullback and impulse pin bar — at about 55 to 60 percent on average, sometimes higher, and states trading volume of roughly 20 to 40 trades a month in currencies on 15-minute candles.

13.7 Trader classifications and holding-period conventions

Separately from the specific setups, he lays out a general taxonomy of forex trading styles by holding period, which functions as a reference point for where his own trading sits:

  • Position traders hold for weeks to months, riding the larger trend, leaning mostly on fundamental news, targeting 500 to 2,000 pips per trade.
  • Swing traders target 50 to 150 pips per trade, hold less than a week, and work off hourly and four-hourly charts.
  • Day traders enter and exit within 24 hours, taking one to four trades a day, targeting 20 to 40 pips, on 15-minute, hourly, and four-hourly charts.
  • Scalpers watch bar by bar and exit within one or two minutes, sometimes seconds, targeting 5 to 10 pips.

He says he's traded in every one of these roles over the years, and that day trading is what's been most productive for him personally. His stated preferred timeframe is the 15-minute chart, which recurs across nearly every setup description above; he also uses the hourly and four-hour charts, and says he finds the daily timeframe too slow for his patience. A trade on his core strategies typically runs anywhere from 15 minutes to three or four hours, though he also describes an average of one to two hours, rarely more than two. A discipline he states as a hard rule: he always closes his forex positions before the weekend and never carries one over — one demonstrated example is a CADJPY position closed for a modest $7 profit specifically so it wouldn't be left open overnight into Friday close.

What he doesn't say about this

Coverage on forex is genuinely thinner than on stocks or options, and rather than pad around the gap this book states it plainly. He does not, anywhere in the archive drawn on for this chapter, give a full session-by-session correlation table across all eight majors, or a complete published list of which pairs he treats as "correlated" beyond the two or three worked examples he shows on camera. He does not reconcile the 1.5R figure with the Trend Rider's 1:1.4, beyond both existing as separate, later strategies taught to his student community — whether 1.5R is a general house target he's since refined per-strategy, or whether the two figures simply belong to two different systems taught years apart, isn't something he addresses directly. He does not state a maximum number of open forex positions at once, only that any simultaneous positions must not be correlated. And while he names the currency strength meter as central to his pair selection, the meter itself is proprietary — built by his company, not explained mechanically on camera — so a reader can adopt the buy-strong/sell-weak rule but cannot reconstruct the underlying calculation from what he's shown.

Chapter summary

  • Forex is one part of a parallel portfolio — investing, stock trading, forex, and options run together — not a stand-alone system; he day trades forex, swing trades stocks, and invests in stocks as three distinct activities.
  • Mechanically: pips, lots, and the bid/ask spread work differently from stocks — orders trigger off bid/ask rather than last price, and position size is set by a formula (capital × risk% ÷ (stop in pips × pip value)).
  • Pair selection runs through a proprietary currency strength meter — buy the green (strong, 60+), sell the red (weak, below 40), avoid the neutral yellow — combined with a check that a currency's other pairs are moving the same direction.
  • His forex trend filter is the 18/50/200 exponential/simple moving-average stack — a distinct convention from the 20/40 and 50/150 pairings used for stocks elsewhere in this book.
  • He names a consistent small set of setups — bull flag/impulse pullback, impulse pin bar, trend continuation, moving-average bounce, range trading, news scalping, Fibonacci confluence — and is equally explicit about what he avoids: breakout entries and resistance/support retests.
  • The chapter's spine: he uses a 1.5R profit target for forex against the 2R he teaches for stocks, and states the reason himself — forex's greater volatility means a bigger target simply doesn't get hit often enough.
  • News scalping straddles a scheduled release with orders placed exactly three minutes ahead, trailing the stop rather than fixing a target, and he attributes the strategy's origin to a colleague, not himself.
  • Coverage here runs out faster than in the stocks and options material — no full pair-correlation table, no reconciliation between the 1.5R figure and later per-strategy variants, and no mechanical breakdown of the strength meter itself.

Try this

He suggests, for the currency-strength approach, matching a green (strong) currency against a red (weak) one and only then checking that pair for a trend, retracement, or breakout setup on the lower timeframe. A reader without access to his proprietary strength meter can still apply the underlying logic he states: before taking any forex setup, check whether the currency's other pairs are moving the same direction — if EUR/USD looks like a buy, pull up EUR/JPY, EUR/GBP, and EUR/AUD and see whether the euro is strong against all of them, not just the dollar.

Chapter 14

Risk and Position Sizing

In this chapter: how Adam Khoo decides how much of his account to put on any single trade, how he limits what several trades at once can do to him, and what actually happens — in his own numbers, restated across a decade — when a losing streak arrives.

14.1 Risk management comes before the trade

Across the trading half of the channel, Khoo returns to one framing more than any other: the instrument you trade is not what makes trading risky. He states directly that there is no such thing as a risky instrument — risk comes from the risk per trade, the position sizing, and the leverage used, not from the asset itself, and he illustrates the point by comparing someone risking ten percent per trade on stocks against someone risking one percent on forex, which makes the "safer" asset the riskier bet in practice. He defines money management, in as many words, as position sizing: knowing how much to risk per trade and keeping that risk both consistent and small. And he defines risk per trade itself narrowly — it is the maximum loss on a trade, fixed by wherever the stop-loss sits, on the assumption that a stop-loss is always placed.

That last condition matters and recurs throughout this chapter: nearly everything below applies to the stop-loss-mandatory trading book described in Chapter 2, not to the no-stop-loss investing book. When Khoo talks about position sizing, R-multiples, and risk percentages, he is talking about trading. The investing book has its own risk logic — the tranche-buying-into-drawdown sizing covered in Chapter 6 — built on a different premise entirely.

Editor's note — why "risk" and "position size" are not the same thing

Khoo's arithmetic throughout this chapter depends on keeping two numbers separate: the size of a position (how many shares or lots you hold, i.e., how much capital is deployed) and the risk on that position (how much you'd actually lose if the stop is hit). A $5,000 account might buy $1,884 of stock, but the number that matters for his sizing rules is the $100 that gets lost if the stop is hit — not the $1,884 deployed. Conflating "how big is my position" with "how much am I risking" is the most common source of accidental over-risking, since a wide stop on a large position risks far more than a tight stop on the same position.

14.2 The R system: expressing risk as a unit

The organizing convention across the entire trading corpus is R (the unit of risk) — one R equals whatever percentage of capital is set as the risk per trade. He says successful traders think in R multiples specifically to mentally disassociate the trade from a dollar figure: a loss is "one R," a win might be "one point five R," rather than a specific sum of money. He translates the unit into account terms repeatedly and consistently: if you risk 1 percent per trade, a 1R loss costs 1 percent of capital and a 2R win gains 2 percent. He defines one R, in a stock-day-trading context, as the amount risked per trade, capped at 1 percent of total capital, and in a separate lesson calls 1 percent the suggested figure with 2 percent as the ceiling for a more aggressive trader, still labeling that ceiling "1R" for the purposes of the arithmetic.

The position-sizing formula he teaches is the same formula restated across at least four separate lessons, in stocks and in forex: the number of shares (or lots) equals the risk percentage times capital, divided by the risk per share — the distance from entry to stop. In forex the formula is restated in pip terms: net liquidation times risk per trade, divided by the stop-loss distance in pips times the dollar value per pip. He works this arithmetic on camera more than once with real numbers: a $5,000 account risking 3 percent gives $150 of total risk, divided by a $3.90 per-share stop distance, for a 38-share position; the same account risking 2 percent gives $100 of risk, divided by a 33-cent stop distance, for 303 shares — a roughly $1,884 position — while he stresses on screen that the number that matters is the $100 at risk, not the $1,884 deployed. One version of the formula sizes off "balance capital," meaning whatever the account is currently worth, so position size automatically shrinks after a losing stretch and grows after a winning one.

14.3 How much to risk per trade: a range, not a constant

Here the material does not converge on one number, and the book reports the range as he actually stated it rather than picking a favorite. In a 2016 video he states the risk per trade as fixed at 2 percent of capital, applied identically to every position, framed as an invariant of the approach in that lesson. Starting in 2017 and continuing at least through 2020, the more common formulation is a range, not a single figure: never more than 1 percent to a maximum of 3 percent of net liquidation on any one trade, with an explicit progression — start at 1 percent, move to 2 percent, and only reach 3 percent once you are experienced and confident, with the upgrade gated on experience rather than on conviction about any particular trade. He states the same 1-to-3-percent band elsewhere as the maximum range, again with 1 percent as the recommended starting point. Once a figure is chosen, he is consistent on one point across every version of this material: apply it uniformly to every trade, and change it only by deliberate decision, never trade-by-trade based on how confident or fearful you feel in the moment. He names inconsistent risk-per-trade sizing — sizing up when confident, down when fearful — as a direct cause of losing outcomes even inside a system with a good win rate and a good risk-to-reward ratio, and works a worked example of a trader who nets a 60 percent win rate and a 1-to-2 risk-to-reward ratio over ten trades yet still ends down 8 percent purely from varying the risk size. He separately flags cutting risk after a losing streak — dropping from 1 percent to 0.5 or 0.3 percent — as its own failure mode, since the smaller size then can't earn back the prior losses once the winning trades return.

Above the 1-to-3-percent range, his own commentary turns skeptical rather than aspirational. He notes he has trader friends who risk 10 percent per trade — one of them took $10,000 to roughly $8 million — but says plainly he does not have the stomach for that approach himself, offering it as an outlier case rather than a recommendation. Elsewhere he reports that some of his own traders, in practice, risk up to 2 to 3 percent per trade while doing 15 to 30 trades a month across stocks and forex — describing what they actually do, not repeating the 1 percent beginner's figure.

Editor's note — reading "1 to 3 percent" as an evolving convention, not a contradiction

The move from a flat 2 percent (2016) to a graduated 1-to-3-percent band (2017 onward) reads less like a reversal and more like the same idea stated with more nuance over time — an early single-figure rule of thumb later replaced with an experience-gated range. The book flags it because a reader assembling "the rule" from a handful of videos could land on either version and reasonably believe it was the whole of his position: it is safer to report both and let the dates speak.

Underneath the range, the reason he gives for staying near the bottom of it is emotional as much as mathematical. He works the arithmetic that 3 percent risk per trade compounds, in theory, to roughly 39 percent a month — 13R at 3 percent — but says this comes with far more portfolio volatility than most people can actually tolerate emotionally, which is why he pushes beginners toward the lower end regardless of the theoretical upside. He makes the same point from the loss side: risking 10 percent per trade and hitting twelve losses in a row wipes out the account entirely, which is the reason he caps his own recommendation at 1-to-3 percent rather than anything higher, however good a strategy's theoretical edge. The two arguments — theoretical return versus tolerable volatility, and survivability against a losing streak — are the twin justifications he gives for staying inside the range, independent of which specific number within it he happens to be using in a given video.

14.4 Portfolio-level limits: what several trades at once can do

Because risk is cumulative, Khoo extends the same logic from a single trade to several open at once. Each concurrent position sized at 1 percent of the same net liquidation means two open positions together risk 2 percent, on the assumption the first position hasn't yet moved. He suggests never carrying more than roughly 10 percent of cumulative risk units open at any one time — at 1 percent per trade, no more than ten concurrent trades — framed explicitly as a guard against everything losing at once in a crash. He states a portfolio-risk ceiling directly: limit total portfolio risk to a maximum of 10 percent, or at the very most 20 percent for someone with a genuinely high risk appetite, but not beyond that. In his own forex practice he does not usually run more than two concurrent trades — most of the time only one, opening the next only once the first closes — reasoning that two trades at 2 percent each already puts 4 percent at risk simultaneously.

Concurrent positions, in his statement of the rule, must also not be correlated: no more than two stocks from the same sector or industry, since he calls holding five bank stocks at once "stupid" — a single sector move would stop out every position together. The same logic extends to hedging: he does not recommend buying a put option against every individual holding, because most stocks move with the S&P 500 anyway, so a single index put can hedge an entire correlated portfolio at once.

The arithmetic behind the 10-percent ceiling is drawdown recovery math, which he works through directly: a 10 percent drawdown needs only an 11 percent gain to get back to even, a 30 percent drawdown needs 42.85 percent, and a 50 percent loss needs a full 100 percent gain just to recover. That asymmetry — losses get proportionally harder to recover from as they get larger — is the stated reason the portfolio cap exists at all.

Editor's note — why recovery math is asymmetric

A 50 percent loss requires a 100 percent gain to break even because the recovery percentage is calculated on the smaller, post-loss capital base, not the original one: losing half of $100,000 leaves $50,000, and doubling $50,000 only returns you to $100,000. This same asymmetry is why professional risk management in most trading and fund-management contexts treats drawdown size, not just win rate, as the primary risk metric — a strategy that wins often but occasionally takes a very large loss can still be a poor bet even with a high win rate, because the occasional large loss requires a disproportionately larger recovery.

14.5 Losing streaks: the numbers he gives do not agree

This is the densest and most internally inconsistent material in the corpus, and the book reports it as he actually gave it, in date order, rather than collapsing it into one figure.

The recurring premise, restated across at least six years of videos, is that losing streaks are statistically inevitable for any strategy, regardless of its quality, and that the entire purpose of small per-trade risk is to survive the streak rather than avoid it. But the length of streak he attaches to that premise varies substantially by video:

  • 2018: "maybe five or six losses in a row," producing a drawdown of six percent or even 18 percent depending on the risk-per-trade assumption, described as temporary and recoverable by continuing to trade.
  • 2019: losing streaks "of five or ten trades in a row" happen to everyone, including the best traders, used to argue against risking 10 percent per trade. In the same year, a separate video states that a 50 percent win rate system can statistically produce a 16-trade losing streak, and that a 60 percent win rate system can still produce a 12-trade losing streak, with winning streaks of matching length.
  • 2022: a probability table is cited on screen showing a 50 percent win rate strategy will, at times, produce a 16-trade losing streak — and a 16-trade winning streak — though the table's sample size and underlying assumptions are not stated on screen. The same video works 16 consecutive losses at 1 percent risk out to a 16 percent drawdown (called within most people's comfort zone) versus an 80 percent drawdown at 5 percent risk (called account-busting).
  • 2023: stated as principle two of his trading psychology — a winning strategy will still produce losing streaks of five to ten trades in a row, alongside winning streaks of similar length.

So across the archive, the loss-streak length he attaches to ordinary trading ranges from five-to-six, to five-to-ten, to twelve, to sixteen, depending on the video and which win-rate assumption is being illustrated. These are not necessarily contradictions in the strict sense — a 50 percent win-rate system genuinely can statistically produce longer losing streaks than a 60 percent one, and "five or six" versus "sixteen" may simply be different points on the same probability curve applied loosely in conversation rather than pulled from a consistent reference table. But no single video reconciles the different figures, and a reader assembling "the" loss-streak length from any one clip would get a different number than a reader who watched a different clip. The book reports the full range, with dates, rather than averaging it into a false constant.

The drawdown arithmetic downstream of these streak lengths is consistent within any given video but shifts with the assumed streak length feeding it. Working from a 16-consecutive-loss assumption: risking 1 percent per trade implies an expected drawdown around 16 percent; risking 2 percent implies roughly 32 percent; risking 3 percent implies close to 50 percent. A separate pass through the same logic, assuming four losses in a row rather than sixteen, gives 4 percent drawdown at 1 percent risk, 12 percent at 3 percent risk, and 40 percent at a 10-percent risk he says should never be used — a very different headline number for the same risk percentage, because the assumed streak length is different. He also gives a direct mapping from personal loss tolerance to recommended risk: if you can only stomach a 10 percent drop, risk 0.5 percent per trade; if you could tolerate a 50 percent loss, you could risk up to 3 percent — again keyed to the 16-consecutive-loss assumption in that video.

Editor's note — why streak length genuinely depends on win rate and sample size

In coin-flip-style probability, the expected length of the longest losing streak within a sample of trades grows with the number of trades considered and shrinks as win rate rises above 50 percent — so it is mathematically true that a 50 percent win-rate system can produce longer losing streaks than a 60 percent one, and that a streak's expected length depends on how many total trades you're looking across (10 trades will rarely show a 16-loss streak; 500 trades might). None of the cited videos state the total number of trades the streak-length figure is drawn from, which is exactly the information needed to judge which number applies to a given trader's situation — a real gap in the material, not an error to paper over.

14.6 A note on entry filters as risk control, and where his own numbers drift

Position sizing controls how much is lost if a trade goes wrong; the entry filters covered in depth in Chapter 8 and Chapter 9 control how often a trade is entered in a weak position to begin with, which is itself a form of risk management. Two of those filters are worth flagging here because the specific numbers he attaches to them are not stable across the archive, in the same spirit as the loss-streak figures above.

The short-term trend filter is a moving-average pairing, and he states two different pairings for it.
