In several 2017 lessons he defines the short-term uptrend as the 20 exponential moving average crossing above, or sitting above, the 40 EMA, and gives a worked trade where the 20-crossing-below-40 exit rule closes the position. Elsewhere, in what he calls the first entry rule of his trend-following swing system, the same short-term-uptrend condition is stated as the 20 EMA above the 50 moving average instead of the 40. Both pairings are presented as his working definition of a short-term uptrend within their respective videos; the book reports both rather than resolving them into one.

Similarly, his oversold confirmation signal for a bounce entry is stated at two different stochastics thresholds. In multiple 2017 videos he treats stochastics below 20 as oversold and due to bounce, and uses it as one of several confluence signals for a long entry. In another 2017 video, the entry rule for the same kind of setup is stated as stochastics below 30. As with the moving-average pairing, this is reported as a documented range across dated instances — 20 in most of the cited material, 30 in at least one — not averaged into a single number.

14.7 Leverage: what it is and where he draws the line

Khoo defines leverage plainly: putting up only a fraction of a position's value — for example, $1,000 to control $10,000 of shares, a 10 percent deposit — so that a 1 percent move in price becomes a 10 percent move on the capital actually committed. He calls it a double-edged sword directly: a 24 percent favorable move becomes 48 percent at two-times leverage, but a 24 percent adverse move becomes a 48 percent loss the same way, and at ten-times leverage a 10 percent adverse move wipes out the entire capital. Leverage lets you buy a million dollars of stock with $100,000, but it is, in his words, "extremely risky" if the trade goes the wrong way, and he states it must be paired with a risk-management strategy — a claim that in the same breath he extends to say leverage plus risk management can "double your money quite easily within a year," which the book flags as his stated claim, not a verified result.

On his own forex leverage he is specific and consistent: brokers commonly allow 1-to-100 leverage, but he personally never uses more than 1-to-20, usually running 10-to-15 times, and separately states the same ceiling — brokers offer 40 or 100 times, but he uses a maximum of 20 times in most of his own trades. Margin accounts, in his description, typically let a trader control two dollars of shares for every dollar of capital, occasionally up to five dollars with some brokers, so a 50 percent margin turns a $1,000 position into $500 of required capital; a separate video describes a "usual" margin account as capping leverage at two times, still funding 50 percent of the position, with interest charged on the borrowed portion.

The leverage line he draws hardest is between the two books. In investing, he states repeatedly across several years that leverage and borrowed money should never be used — invest only with cash you will not need for several years, because a margin call in a downturn can force you to sell at the worst possible time. In trading, by contrast, he permits margin, and gives the reasoning explicitly: a stop-loss is what makes it safe, because you are risking only a small percentage of net liquidation value, not of the leveraged funds themselves — though he never states numerically what "small percentage" means in that specific passage, leaving the reader to import the 1-to-3-percent figure from elsewhere in the corpus. In one interview segment, a guest describes generating essentially all of his trading profits on broker margin taken against his long-term stock holdings — a structure that sits uneasily against Khoo's own stated investing-never-uses-leverage rule — and Khoo's only recorded caution in response is to always keep a margin of safety, without flagging the tension directly.

14.8 The return arithmetic — and why it is a claim, not a track record

A large share of the "risk management" material in this corpus doubles as return-arithmetic demonstration: given a win rate, a risk-to-reward ratio, and a risk-per-trade percentage, what does the resulting return look like over some number of trades? These are worked illustrations, and the book reports them as such — several are explicitly tagged in the sourcing as illustrative rather than verified performance.

Representative examples: a simulator run over 100 trades at a 55 percent win rate, 1:2 risk-to-reward, and 2 percent risk produces a 159 percent return over roughly five months in his telling; 50R across 100 trades at 2 percent risk per trade is described as a 100 percent return, extrapolated to around 240 percent annualized at 20 trades a month, on an assumed 60 percent win rate with 1.5R average wins; risking 1 percent per trade with a 60 percent win rate and a 2R:1R payoff is said to produce roughly an 80 percent return across 100 trades in a simplified model. He also reports what reads as a real result rather than a model: an October month totaling 14.7R, which he translates to roughly 14.7 percent for a 1-percent-risk trader, or 28-percent-plus at 2 percent risk — a single month's figure, not annualized or averaged against other months in that same video.

These numbers should be read for what they demonstrate — how R-based risk sizing compounds arithmetically — not as a guarantee or even a typical outcome. He states this qualification himself in places: the 50R-to-50-percent conversion is offered as "not bad... even at a 50 percent win rate," explicitly following only from the illustration's assumptions, not as a performance claim. Elsewhere, he demonstrates the opposite case deliberately: a trader with a 76 percent win rate across 13 trades — 10 wins, 3 losses — still nets a loss overall, because risk was raised to 10 percent after each win and cut to 1 percent after each loss. Win rate alone, in his own worked counter-example, does not determine outcome; sizing discipline does.

Editor's note — self-reported returns belong in this chapter as claims, not facts

None of the return figures above are independently verifiable from the material available — they are numbers stated on camera, in some cases from a simulator with assumptions chosen for the demonstration, and in at least one case (the 55%-win-rate, 159%-return example) the on-screen narration and the automatic transcript disagree about the resulting account balance, which this project's own sourcing flags as a discrepancy. Chapter 1 covers his broader track-record claims in more depth; this chapter's arithmetic should be read the same way — as a demonstration of how the R system compounds, not as audited performance.

What he doesn't say about this

He does not state, in any video in this project's corpus, the sample size or number of trades behind the loss-streak probability table he cites in 2022, which is precisely the number needed to judge whether "16 losses in a row" is a live risk for a trader doing ten trades a month or an edge case relevant only over hundreds of trades. He does not reconcile the 2016 "fixed 2 percent" statement with the 2017-onward 1-to-3-percent graduated range, or the four-consecutive-losses arithmetic in one video against the sixteen-consecutive-losses arithmetic in another — both stand in the archive as stated. He does not give a numeric floor for what "a small percentage of net liquidation" means when he permits margin for trading, leaving that connection to the reader to make from the position-sizing material elsewhere. And while he states clearly that leverage should never be used in investing, he does not revisit or correct the guest interview in which margin against long-term holdings is described approvingly in his presence — the tension is left unresolved on screen.

Chapter summary

  • Risk, in his framing, comes from position sizing and leverage, not the instrument traded — the same asset can be low-risk or high-risk depending entirely on how much is put on it.
  • Position size is calculated from a fixed formula — risk percentage times capital, divided by the stop distance — never from conviction in the setup.
  • How much to risk per trade is stated inconsistently across the archive: a flat 2 percent in 2016, versus a graduated 1-to-3-percent range (starting at 1, rising with experience) from 2017 onward — reported here as a range across dated instances, not a single rule.
  • Risk compounds across open positions: two 1-percent trades together risk 2 percent, correlated positions in the same sector should be avoided, and total portfolio risk should stay near 10 percent, at most 20.
  • Losing-streak length is the least consistent figure in the corpus — stated variously as five to six, five to ten, twelve, and sixteen trades in a row across videos from 2018 to 2023, with matching drawdown arithmetic that shifts depending on which streak length is assumed.
  • Leverage is permitted, within stated personal limits (a max of roughly 20x in forex), only when paired with a stop-loss and small per-trade risk — and is ruled out entirely for the no-stop-loss investing book, a line he draws consistently for years even though one interview guest's own practice sits against it.
  • Return figures built from win rate and R-multiples — 100%+, 150%+, even 240% annualized in some illustrations — are worked demonstrations of the arithmetic, not track records, and at least one such demonstration contains an internal numeric discrepancy in this project's own sourcing.
  • A high win rate does not guarantee a profitable outcome on its own: his own worked example shows a 76 percent win rate still losing money once risk-per-trade is varied by emotion rather than held constant.

Try this

Take the position-sizing formula as he states it — number of shares equals risk percentage times capital, divided by the per-share risk (the distance from your entry to your stop) — and run it on a real setup you're watching, at three different risk percentages: 1, 2, and 3 percent of a hypothetical account. Compare the resulting position sizes and, using his drawdown arithmetic, work out what five losses in a row and sixteen losses in a row would each cost you at each risk level. The exercise is his own arithmetic, not an invented one — the point is to see, in your own numbers, why the range he gives matters more than any single figure inside it.

Chapter 15

Reading Macro

In this chapter: the diagnostic checklist Khoo runs when a stock or the whole market drops for reasons that have nothing to do with the company itself, the bar he sets before he'll call a decline a "real" bear market, and how he treats interest-rate and inflation data as one input among several rather than as a forecasting tool in its own right. This chapter is deliberately not a record of his specific market calls — Chapter 3 already shows how often those calls sit next to his own disclaimer that no one can make them reliably, and the ledger behind this book shows his hit rate on individual price targets was mixed at best. What survives that record, and what this chapter extracts, is the decision procedure underneath the calls.

15.1 The question he says not to ask

Confronted with a falling market, the instinct most investors reach for first is "why is this happening." Khoo's stated position on that instinct is unusually blunt: asking why the market is going down is not the important question for a trader or investor, because there are countless possible causes at any moment — what matters is recognizing that the move is happening and having a plan for how to act on it. That is not an argument that causes don't exist or don't matter; the rest of this chapter is built from cards where he works through causes in detail. It is an argument about sequencing — that diagnosis is a tool for deciding what to do next, not an end in itself, and a reader who gets stuck trying to identify the one true cause of a drop before acting has misused the exercise he's about to hand them.

The taxonomy itself is stated directly, and he frames it as reusable machinery rather than commentary on any one event. Non-company-specific reasons a stock's price can fall, in his account, sort into five buckets: macro or geopolitical events, a broad market correction or bear market, sector rotation, industry-wide news that isn't specific to the one company, and pure market-maker manipulation. The taxonomy's first job is negative — it exists to separate "something is wrong with this specific business" from "something is happening to the market or the group this business happens to sit in." Only the first category, in his framework, should change what an investor thinks the company is worth.

He turns the taxonomy directly into an action rule rather than leaving it as classification for its own sake: when a stock drops for a non-company-specific macro reason — a rate hike, a weak GDP or inflation print, sector rotation, a broad correction — he calls that a "no-brainer" and adds to the position if he doesn't already hold a full-sized stake in it. The logic only holds, by his own framing, if the drop genuinely traces to one of the five external buckets rather than to news specific to the company — which is why the taxonomy has to come first and the buying decision second, not the other way around.

Editor's note — why the ordering matters more than the categories

The five buckets themselves are not exotic; most attentive investors could generate a similar list. What makes the framework useful is that it's applied before a buy-or-hold decision rather than invoked afterward to justify one. It is easy, after the fact, to tell yourself a drop was "just macro noise" — confirmation bias runs in exactly that direction, because "macro noise" is also the story that lets you keep a losing position without having to admit the thesis broke. The discipline in Khoo's version is that the classification step is supposed to happen with the same rigor whether the answer turns out to favor buying more or cutting the position — the taxonomy is only doing real work if it's capable of returning "no, this is company-specific" as an answer, not just "yes, buy the dip."

15.2 Cyclical or structural: the second-stage question

Once a decline is diagnosed as coming from outside the company, Khoo runs a second, finer-grained diagnostic on the underlying cause itself: is it cyclical — will it resolve on its own once conditions turn — or structural, meaning something has permanently changed about the business or the environment it operates in. He applies this explicitly when judging whether a market drop is a real trend change: he checks whether the underlying cause is temporary or permanent, whether the economic and company fundamentals behind it are strengthening or weakening, and what the technical charts are showing, treating those three checks together rather than any one alone.

The distinction recurs across individual company cases in the corpus, which is useful precisely because it shows the rule being applied rather than only stated. He attributes Meta's, Alphabet's, and Snap's advertising revenue declines to an industry-wide, cyclical pullback in advertiser demand tied to recession fear and a strong US dollar — not to any company-specific loss of competitive position — and treats the shared, cross-company pattern as the evidence for "cyclical" rather than "structural". He reasons the same way about a slowdown in cybersecurity spending that hit Fortinet and Palo Alto Networks, judging it a short-term cyclical dip rather than a structural change in demand, since the long-term security need itself hadn't gone away. Where he judges the opposite — that a slowdown is structural, not cyclical — he acts differently: he cites his own exit from Tencent as driven specifically by new Chinese gaming regulations that threatened to permanently impair the business, distinguishing it explicitly from China's broader economic slowdown, which he treats as the ordinary cyclical kind that recovers.

He extends the same either/or question to sectors and macro themes, not just single companies. Deciding whether growth in power-demand stocks reflects a temporary spike or a durable multi-decade shift, he frames the entire investment case as resting on answering that one question correctly, and he applies the identical cyclical-versus-permanent framing to a rise in unemployment, distinguishing the ordinary kind that recedes once a recession ends from what he argues, as his own view, is now a structural shift driven by automation.

Editor's note — a real-world example of the cyclical/structural line being drawn wrong, in both directions

The cyclical-versus-structural question is easy to state and genuinely hard to answer in real time, and the historical record has cases of it being misjudged in each direction. US retailers' brick-and-mortar sales declines in the early-to-mid 2010s were, for years, widely described in financial media as a cyclical effect of a still-recovering post-2008 consumer — only for it to become clear over the following decade that e-commerce substitution was a structural, permanent shift, and several major chains that waited for a cyclical rebound went bankrupt waiting for it. The opposite error also happens: shipping and freight rates spiked during 2021–2022 and were treated by some analysts as evidence of a permanent reordering of global supply chains, when much of the spike proved to be a cyclical pandemic-era bottleneck that unwound within about two years. Neither case is drawn from Khoo's own material — they're supplied here because the corpus doesn't offer a worked example of him getting this particular call wrong, and a reader should know going in that this diagnostic, however disciplined, is a judgment call with real historical failure cases on both sides, not a mechanical test with a guaranteed right answer.

15.3 The bar for a "real" bear market: operating earnings, not GDP

Khoo draws a specific, quantified line between an ordinary correction or short "baby bear market" and what he treats as a genuine, recessionary bear market, and the line is corporate operating earnings rather than the popular media shorthand for a recession. He argues directly that the commonly cited "two consecutive quarters of negative GDP growth" definition of recession is outdated, pointing instead to the NBER's broader definition — a significant, economy-wide decline lasting more than a few months — with falling corporate profits and rising job losses as the markers that actually matter. He sharpens that into an explicit, checkable rule: a true bear market requires declining corporate operating earnings, citing that earnings went negative during the dot-com crash, the 2008 financial crisis, and the 2020 pandemic recession, while noting that in the 2022 decline operating earnings were still positive and merely slowing rather than falling.

This bar does real classificatory work elsewhere in the corpus. He describes his own "baby bear market" category — a short, non-recessionary decline of roughly 20 to 30 percent where fundamentals remain intact and the drop is better read as an emotional sell-off that recovers quickly, contrasted with recessionary bear markets that have historically run closer to 35 percent on average. He characterizes the 2022 decline, in particular, as unusual by his own framework precisely because it lacked the ingredients his rule requires — no recession, no corporate earnings contraction — attributing it instead to the Fed raising rates unusually quickly. And he restates the underlying logic as a single sentence more than once: it isn't the level or even the pace of a rate move that determines whether a decline turns into a real bear market, but whether earnings actually roll over as a result.

Editor's note — why "operating earnings negative" is a lagging confirmation, not an early warning

There's a structural tradeoff built into this bar that's worth naming plainly. Corporate earnings are reported quarterly and with a lag — by the time aggregate operating earnings for the S&P 500 are confirmed negative, the market has typically already priced much of the decline in, because equity prices are forward-looking and tend to fall in anticipation of an earnings contraction rather than only in response to a confirmed one. That makes "operating earnings negative" a reasonably reliable way to confirm, after the fact, whether a decline was the recessionary kind — which is exactly the classificatory use Khoo puts it to above — but it is a poor candidate for calling a bear market's start in real time, since the confirming data arrives well after price has already moved. The bar is best read as an instrument for correctly labeling a decline already underway, not as an early-warning system for a decline that hasn't happened yet — a distinction the material doesn't spell out explicitly but that follows from how the indicator itself is constructed.

15.4 Rate and inflation data as input, not oracle

A large share of the macro material in the corpus concerns how Khoo reads Federal Reserve policy, Treasury yields, and inflation data — and the dominant pattern across a decade of that material is that he treats each of these as one input into a broader read, revisits and revises his own interpretation of the same indicators repeatedly, and explicitly rejects treating any single one as decisive on its own.

The yield curve is the clearest case, because he returns to it across years and visibly changes how much weight he puts on it. He states, at various points, that an inverted yield curve has preceded every one of the last seven to nine US recessions with a hit rate as high as 90 to 100 percent, and elsewhere gives a lower, more qualified figure — 8 inversions in the last 30 years preceding 6 recessions, a 75 percent hit rate on a sample he himself calls very small. He adds a specific timing refinement more than once: what matters is not the moment the curve first inverts but the moment it re-inverts, crossing back above zero, and he walks through four historical cases — 1989, 2001, 2007, and 2019 — where the lag between re-inversion and the recession's actual start ranged from about two months to nearly a year. He is explicit that the lag itself is inconsistent enough, ranging from roughly 1.6 months to 1.8 years across the historical inversions he tracked, that the yield curve makes a poor tool for timing an actual exit from the market even when its recession-calling record holds up. And in at least one later video he steps back from the indicator's usefulness altogether, noting that even the world's top economists using every indicator available could not predict a recession consistently, pointing to the widely-held 2023 recession call — based substantially on this exact signal — that never happened.

He reads Fed policy itself the same layered way rather than as a single green or red light. He distinguishes explicitly between the direction of a rate move and its speed, arguing more than once that rate hikes themselves are not inherently bearish — citing periods where the market rose through hiking cycles — and that what actually damages the market is hiking too aggressively, "stalling" the economy the way overbraking stalls a car. He runs the same disaggregation on rate cuts: he rebuts the popular claim that a first rate cut reliably crashes the market by pointing out that in 2000 and 2008 the market crashed first, forcing the Fed's hand, rather than the cut causing the crash — and separately lists historical cases where a first cut preceded a rally rather than a decline. He treats the Fed's own forward guidance, and market tools like the CME FedWatch probability tool, as one more data point to track rather than a certainty — repeatedly citing specific implied-probability figures for upcoming meetings while noting they shift, sometimes sharply, as new data arrives.

Inflation data gets the same treatment: read for what it implies about Fed behavior, not as a standalone forecasting signal. He lists a cluster of indicators he watches to judge whether inflation has peaked — falling oil prices, a falling dollar, a falling 10-year yield, falling housing prices, and companies announcing hiring slowdowns — treating the combination, not any one reading, as the signal. He also flags a specific measurement caveat rather than taking the headline number at face value: he notes that the shelter and rent component carries an outsized weight in core CPI while lagging real-time private rent data by six to nine months, which he uses to argue that a sticky-looking core inflation print can overstate current price pressure.

Editor's note — the difference between a diagnostic and a crystal ball

None of this material amounts to Khoo claiming he can predict where rates or inflation are headed with precision — quite the opposite, several of the cards above show him revising a prior read once new data arrived, or citing the market's own probability tools rather than his personal forecast. The useful pattern to take from this section isn't any specific number he cited at any specific date (several of which the ledger behind this book shows were later wrong, including his own admitted surprise that the 10-year yield kept rising after he'd expected it to have peaked ). It's the layering habit itself: no single indicator, including the one most people reach for first, gets treated as sufficient on its own, and a reading is always checked against at least one other line of evidence — earnings, price action, or a second macro series — before it changes what he does.

15.5 Sector rotation as a read on market sentiment

Khoo treats sector rotation — the shift of money between defensive and cyclical sectors — as both a portfolio-management input and a diagnostic signal about what the market as a whole is pricing in. He defines cyclical sectors as the ones whose profits swing sharply with the economy: industrials, materials, energy, financials, and consumer discretionary among them, with technology treated as a partial exception because he considers it in a secular bull market even though it is technically classified as cyclical. His stated position on how to trade cyclicals themselves is straightforward: buy them only when they are cheap, near the bottom of their own cycle, and take profits once they catch back up to the top rather than holding through the full downswing.

Beyond position sizing, he reads which sectors are leading a given rally or sell-off as a signal about the market's own recession expectations. When defensive sectors — staples, utilities, healthcare — lead a rally, he reads that as a bearish tell, because it implies the market is pricing in a recession even while headline prices rise; when cyclical sectors lead instead, he reads that as bullish, because it implies the market doesn't expect a serious downturn. He applies a version of the same weighing-machine idea more broadly, describing the market as a "weighing machine" that prices fundamentals correctly over the long run but a fickle "voting machine" in the short run, where money moves between sectors on shifting sentiment rather than changed fundamentals — the same distinction that underlies his instruction, noted in Section 15.2, to check whether an underlying move is temporary or permanent before reacting to it.

He also uses observed rotation directly as a diagnostic of what kind of correction is underway, treating a market where cyclicals rise while an index average falls as evidence the decline isn't a broad, correlated sell-off driven by systemic fear, and, in the same spirit, is careful to state that reading strength in undervalued cyclicals is not the same as a call to abandon technology or growth positions altogether.

15.6 Watch what they do, not what they say

Chapter 3 documents Khoo's general distrust of forecasts, including his own, and the corpus offers macro-specific versions of the same instinct — cases where he explicitly favors verifiable positioning data over public commentary. He cites, as an example rather than a template, the FedWatch tool's market-implied probabilities as more trustworthy in the moment than a Fed official's own stated intentions, at one point noting the market priced in a 69 percent probability the Fed would pause even after the Fed chair himself said publicly that further hikes were coming. He treats mainland Chinese investors' actual net-buying flows as a more informative signal than the foreign-investor selling and accompanying headlines during a 2023 China sell-off, contrasting roughly 1.7 billion dollars of foreign selling against roughly 3.6 billion dollars a week of mainland buying as evidence the panic was one-sided.

The same instinct shows up in how he treats disclosed positioning from other investors as more informative than their public commentary. He recounts Michael Burry building a large short position in mortgage-backed securities years before the 2008 crisis, sitting on tens of millions in unrealized paper losses for roughly two years before the position paid off — a case he cites as evidence for the value of conviction independent of near-term price action, not as a call to imitate the trade. He also notes, without resolving the tension himself, that Cathie Wood publicly and completely exited China just before a rally following a Politburo speech, while Michael Burry held over 70 percent of his own portfolio in Chinese equities at the same time — framing the contradiction between two visible "experts" as an open question for the reader rather than a verdict.

Editor's note — Grantham and Druckenmiller as further cases, since the chapter deliberately avoids scoring Khoo's own calls

This chapter, unlike Chapter 3, is not the place to tally Khoo's own price targets against what actually happened — that record exists in the corpus and is uneven, and the book states that plainly elsewhere rather than pretending it isn't relevant here. But the "watch positioning, not commentary" principle he applies to Burry and to the FedWatch tool has two further, well-documented outside cases worth adding as background. Jeremy Grantham, whose specific and repeated crash calls Chapter 3 tallies directly, is a useful contrast case precisely because his firm GMO's own disclosed asset-allocation shifts have, at points, moved toward more constructive equity positioning even while Grantham's public commentary stayed heavily bearish — the same gap between a public statement and a disclosed position that the chapter elsewhere treats as informative. Stanley Druckenmiller is a third frequently cited case in financial media: his firm's quarterly 13F filings have shown him building and then substantially unwinding large technology positions within single-quarter windows, on a timeline a same-quarter public interview would not necessarily have signaled. As with Burry in Chapter 3, none of this proves any of the three were being misleading — disclosed filings and public commentary are produced for different audiences and filed on different clocks, so some gap is structurally normal — but it is a real, checkable illustration of the same discipline this chapter's macro material keeps demonstrating: a regulatory filing or a market-implied probability carries different evidentiary weight than a quote, and treating them identically is the error to avoid.

What he doesn't say

He does not, anywhere in the material gathered for this chapter, state a weighting scheme for combining his various macro inputs — yield-curve reading, Fed-policy stance, inflation trend, sector rotation, and operating earnings — into a single call; each is discussed and applied case by case, and the chapter's synthesis of them into a layered "diagnostic" is the book's own organization of material he presents piecemeal across a decade of separate videos, not a framework he lays out in one place himself. He never states a quantified false-positive or false-negative rate for the operating-earnings-negative bar itself — how often earnings have declined without a recession following, for instance — beyond the specific historical episodes he names. He does not address, in the cards gathered here, how an investor should act during the lag between a macro cause being correctly diagnosed and the operating-earnings data that would confirm the diagnosis, beyond the general instruction (documented in Chapter 7) to keep buying in tranches through investing-book declines. And while he repeatedly urges watching what other investors and institutions do rather than what they say, he never turns that same lens on his own public forecasts versus his own disclosed trading activity — the parallel case for himself, as Chapter 3 notes, is one he leaves for the reader to draw rather than drawing explicitly.

Chapter summary

  • Confronted with a price drop, his first move is not to ask "why," but to classify the cause into one of five non-company-specific buckets — macro or geopolitical events, a broad correction or bear market, sector rotation, industry-wide news, or manipulation — before deciding whether it changes anything about the underlying business.
  • When a drop is classified as non-company-specific macro noise, he treats adding to the position as a "no-brainer," conditional on the classification being correct rather than assumed.
  • The second-stage question is whether the cause is cyclical (temporary, self-resolving) or structural (permanent), checked against whether fundamentals are strengthening or weakening and what price action shows.
  • He sets a specific, checkable bar for a genuine recessionary bear market — declining corporate operating earnings, not the popular "two negative GDP quarters" shorthand — and classifies past declines against it, including arguing 2022 fell short of that bar.
  • He reads interest-rate and yield-curve data as one layered input among several rather than a standalone signal, revising his own confidence in the yield curve's reliability across years as its record produced both hits and a widely-shared false 2023 recession call.
  • He explicitly separates the direction of a Fed rate move from its pace or the underlying condition that caused it, rejecting simple "hikes are bearish" or "the first cut always crashes the market" narratives with historical counterexamples on both sides.
  • He reads sector rotation as a sentiment gauge as much as a portfolio tool — defensive sectors leading a rally reads as the market pricing in recession, cyclicals leading reads as confidence a downturn won't be severe.
  • Across several cases, his stated preference is to weight disclosed positioning and market-implied probabilities above public commentary, including official statements — a discipline with real, checkable parallels outside his own channel in cases like Burry's, Grantham's, and Druckenmiller's documented gaps between what they said and what their filings showed.
Chapter 16

The Enemy Is Not the Market

In this chapter: why the channel's densest single body of material is behavioral rather than technical, the specific failure-mode behaviors he catalogues during drawdowns, his own recurring metaphors for the same handful of psychological rules, and two of the most consequential findings in this book — his own admission that years of a habit he later abandoned may have cost him being a billionaire, and a documented, unresolved contradiction between his stated rule that no one's predictions should be trusted, including his own, and his own repeated habit of giving one anyway.

16.1 Why this is the biggest topic on the channel

Psychology, discipline, fear-and-greed, patience, confidence, and the failure modes around losing streaks generate more cards in the ledger than any other cluster of subjects on the channel — more than valuation, more than technical setups, more than risk arithmetic. That is not an accident of how the ledger was built; it reflects where he himself keeps returning. He states outright that trading psychology is not a soft add-on but the discipline to follow a system consistently with no deviation, and he frames the entire game as rigged against the untrained mind: the human brain is wired in a way that causes the average person to lose money, so a winning trader has to learn to think and act contrary to instinct.

His clearest statement of the mechanism runs as a simple formula he teaches repeatedly: event plus response equals outcome, or E + R = O. The event — a losing trade, a crash, a piece of news — is not itself what determines the result; the response is. He illustrates this by noting that some of the most successful and inspiring people lived through the worst events, which he takes as evidence that the event never determined the outcome on its own. He extends the same logic to why talent alone doesn't predict success: he says he has known people with tremendous talent, knowledge, and qualifications who achieved little because they lived in poor emotional states.

He does not treat emotional states as things that simply happen to a person. He argues that emotions are created — through self-talk, mental imagery, and physiology — and that because you create them, you can change them quickly once you learn how. States like laziness, stress, frustration, and boredom he calls unresourceful because they stop a person from acting; states like excitement, confidence, and motivation he calls resourceful because they drive action and produce peak performance. The practical instruction that follows is a division of the world into what is and isn't controllable: you cannot always control the events that happen to you, so he says there is no point dwelling on them, but you can always control your own thoughts, emotions, decisions, and actions — and that controlling the inside world is what changes the outside one.

Editor's note — E + R = O and locus of control

The formula he teaches is a plain-language version of a distinction psychologists call "locus of control" — whether a person attributes outcomes primarily to external forces (the market, luck, other people) or to their own responses and choices. Decades of research across athletics, sales, and clinical settings link an internal locus of control with better resilience after setbacks, though it is not a guarantee of better decisions — an internal locus can just as easily produce overconfidence as it can discipline. He presents the formula as settled and universally beneficial; the research literature treats it as one useful lens among several, not a complete account of performance.

16.2 The catalogue: what a losing streak actually does to a trader

Rather than a single warning about "emotional trading," he gives what amounts to a field guide to the specific behaviors that show up after five, six, or seven losses in a row. He states that most people at that point start intervening in ways their system never called for: removing stop losses, taking profits early, and abandoning the system altogether. He separately names the overcorrection in the other direction — traders who lose so much confidence after a losing run that they stop taking trades entirely, which he says is the moment they guarantee they will keep losing, because a system's edge only pays out if it keeps getting executed.

He lists rushing into new, lower-quality trades to "win the money back quickly" as one of the most common mistakes after a losing streak, and names this explicitly as revenge trading in multiple videos spanning several years: traders holding or re-buying a losing position purely to make it pay them back, taking trades that don't meet their own rules in an attempt to recover losses, and doing so even when no valid setup exists. He distinguishes this from ordinary risk-taking by pointing to the emotional signature: gamblers, in his framing, feel excited and confident on a win and angry, betrayed, and vengeful on a loss, while a trader is supposed to stay emotionally even through both.

Overtrading gets its own separate treatment. He says that after losses, some traders take trades outside their system entirely or increase risk to make the loss back, which he says usually compounds the damage. He also documents the mirror failure after a run of wins: traders raising risk from something like 1 percent to 5 percent following five wins in a row, then taking four losses at the higher risk for a 20 percent hit to the account — confidence from a winning streak converted directly into oversized risk on the very next drawdown. He names a third, slower failure mode too: jumping between strategies and asset classes — stocks to crypto to options and back — after short losing runs, never staying with one system long enough to find out if it works.

His own prescription for good execution is deliberately unglamorous. He says good trading should be boring, and that if trading is exciting to you, that excitement belongs at a theme park, not at your screen. On days when nothing in his system lines up, he says the correct action is no action — take no trades, or go do something else entirely, because "not doing something is doing something".

Editor's note — why losing-streak length matters for how you read this catalogue

Chapter 14 documents that his own stated estimates of how long a losing streak can run vary across videos, from five to six losses at the low end to twelve to sixteen at the high end, without him reconciling the discrepancy. The behaviors catalogued here — abandoning stops, revenge trading, quitting — are described as responses to "five, six, or seven losses" in some videos, a number near the low end of his own quoted range. A reader trying to build resilience against a losing streak should plan for the wider range he elsewhere states, not just the shorter one used in these particular psychology videos.

16.3 Fear, greed, and the metaphors he reaches for again and again

Across roughly a decade of videos, he returns to a small set of images rather than inventing new ones each time, which is itself informative — these are evidently the frames he has found actually change behavior. The most frequent is "never chase the girl": don't buy into a stock that is already running, because chasing it is like chasing someone who is running away, and instead you let it come to you by waiting for a pullback to your level. He extends the same image when discussing his own buying discipline during panics, saying he never chases a rally and instead waits for the fear and panic that bring a stock back to him.

A second recurring image addresses the opposite fear — the fear of buying a falling stock. He rejects the "falling knife" framing for a business he considers fundamentally sound, arguing instead that a great business dropping in price behaves more like a rubber ball, or in a more recent formulation, a tennis ball, that will bounce back once it hits the floor, rather than a knife that keeps cutting on the way down. Earlier videos show the more conventional, cautionary use of the same "falling knife" phrase for stocks that don't meet his quality bar: on those, cheap can get cheaper, so he waits for a confirmed reversal before buying rather than guessing the bottom. The two uses are not contradictory once the condition is preserved — the knife metaphor applies to ordinary stocks in a downtrend; the bouncing-ball or tennis-ball metaphor is reserved for businesses he has already screened as high quality.

A third image explains why a stock that "should" keep running often doesn't: the bucking horse. He describes the market as wanting to run up eventually, but jerking and throwing riders off along the way, and says once too many people have climbed on, the horse collapses and the cycle resets. He pairs this with a related car-up-a-hill image — as more people climb aboard a rising stock, the car eventually rolls back down and throws them off, and only once enough riders have been thrown off can the climb resume.

A fourth image, breathing, explains why he waits for pullbacks rather than buying strength: prices move in a wave pattern like breathing, where what goes up must breathe out before it can breathe in and rise again. He says explicitly that he never likes to buy during a "breathing out" (an expansion move) and instead waits for the "breathing in".

Editor's note — the ex-girlfriend framing of "Mr. Market"

He extends the chasing metaphor into an elaborate riff where he personifies the market as an ex-girlfriend who "cut loss" on him for spending on his own growth instead of on her. This is a colorful restatement of Benjamin Graham's classic "Mr. Market" personification — an imaginary, moody business partner who offers you a price every day, sometimes euphoric, sometimes despondent, that you are free to accept or ignore. Graham's original point, and the point this metaphor is doing double duty for here, is that the market's daily mood is not a signal about the underlying business's actual value.

16.4 Discipline as identity, not willpower

He treats rule-following less as an act of willpower in the moment and more as a matter of who a person has decided to be. He says the biggest enemy to trading success is not the market but the trader, and traces the losing pattern to two underlying needs — the need to avoid being wrong and the need for pleasure — which together make people refuse to cut a loss until it has grown enormous. He describes losing traders as people who blame external causes for their results — the market, the Federal Reserve, an election — and states that as long as blame stays external, the trader stays powerless to change the outcome, whereas taking ownership of one's own decisions is what actually turns a losing pattern around. Successful traders, in his framing, take responsibility, learn from mistakes, and move on without dwelling — he cites Dory from Finding Nemo ("just keep swimming") as his model for this.

He connects this directly to self-talk: the average person, he says, talks to themselves roughly 60,000 times a day, and that internal monologue is what needs retraining into something empowering, for instance replacing "I can't afford it" with "how can I afford it," which reopens the search for solutions instead of shutting it down. The starting point for change, in his account, is identifying which "program" currently runs a person — a fear program, a self-doubt program, a depression program — before trying to install a better one.

He is explicit that a system's statistical edge is worthless unless it is executed without deviation: winning traders take every valid signal without questioning, hesitation, or reservation, and the habit of asking "what if this trade fails" before taking it is, on his account, what separates a losing trader from a winning one over many trades. He generalizes the same point beyond trading, saying that everyone shares the same basic neurology, so whatever one person has achieved with discipline is achievable by anyone else — a matter of strategy rather than innate ability.

Editor's note — the limits of "discipline as identity" framing

Reframing rule-adherence as identity rather than willpower is a well-documented technique in behavior-change research (it shows up under different names in habit literature and in clinical work on addiction and recovery) — people who see a behavior as "who I am" rather than "something I'm resisting" report better adherence over time. What the research does not support is his implicit claim that this closes the gap between any two people's trading outcomes; capital available, access to information, and plain luck in when a person started still produce very different outcomes even between two equally disciplined traders.

16.5 Patience and confidence: waiting versus knowing

Patience, in his account, is not passive waiting but the specific discipline of only acting on high-probability setups and treating "no trade today" as a legitimate, even routine, outcome. He says a professional does not have to trade every day, and some days he sends his own students a note that there is nothing worth taking. He frames impatience as the direct cause of rule-bending — traders become "trigger-happy" and start taking something that merely resembles a valid setup rather than one that actually is.

Confidence, by contrast, he treats as something to hold at the level of the system over a long run of trades, not at the level of any single trade. He says he always thinks in terms of the result over the next hundred trades, where the outcome is close to certain given the system's edge, while over any five or ten trades he says he genuinely has no idea what will happen. He calls confidence in one specific trade dangerous, because a loss on that trade then costs the trader their confidence and leaves them disappointed and angry — confidence, he argues, should be attached to the process, not the outcome of an individual bet. He backs this with a description of what an equity curve for a winning system actually looks like: it rises and falls like a stock price over a hundred trades rather than in a straight line, so short-term drawdowns inside a working system are expected, not a signal to change anything.

He states that a losing trade should register as one R lost — one unit of risk, never as a dollar amount, precisely because thinking in dollars makes a trader emotional in a way that thinking in R-multiples does not. He extends the accounting metaphor further, framing a winning trade as sales revenue and a losing trade as cost of goods sold — the way a shop simply expects some cost of doing business alongside its sales.

16.6 The documented tension: "listen to no one, including me"

This is the point at which the chapter has to hold two of his own statements next to each other without resolving them, because he never resolves them himself.

He states, more than once and across different videos, that a serious investor or trader should listen to no one's market predictions — including his own — because predictions are, in his words, purely for entertainment, and no one can predict the market. He goes further in a separate video, saying that if you shouldn't listen to outside experts, the logical extension is to listen to no one at all, and to follow only your own predetermined plan instead. In the same video he states that he does not even follow his own predictions himself.

The ledger documents dozens of instances of the underlying claim — "no one can predict the market" — repeated as a standalone principle across nearly every year of the channel's run, from 2018 through 2026. What the same set of videos also documents, recurring just as consistently, is him stating that principle and then, in the same breath or the same video, offering a specific view anyway. In one 2023 video he says the market's short-term reaction to panic selling can't be known, immediately followed by his own opinion that the risk-to-reward at that level is attractive. In another from the same year he repeats that no one can predict a breakout versus a shakeout, then says he is "secretly hoping" for a 15–20 percent sell-off so he can buy more shares. In a 2024 video he says no one can predict the market and that he never invests based on predictions — stated immediately after predicting a multi-year secular bull market driven by AI, in the same video. In 2025 the pattern recurs on a healthcare stock: he says no one can predict when it will revert to intrinsic value, then goes on to identify a specific chart pattern (a double-bottom bear trap) as a signal that the bottom may already be in. And in mid-2026, on the AI-bubble question, he says no one can predict what happens and calls anyone claiming certainty a "bullshitter" — then, in the very next statement, gives his own specific guess that the current rally is not the top but only "the first act" of a longer boom.

This book states the tension rather than resolving it, because he never resolves it himself in any card in the ledger. The honest reading is not that his rule is false — avoiding blind reliance on other people's forecasts is defensible advice on its own — but that he does not, in practice, apply the "listen to no one" caveat to himself with the same rigor he applies it to outside commentators. A reader following this book's method should treat his own market calls with the same skepticism he says should be applied to everyone else's, by his own stated logic.

Editor's note — why this pattern is worth naming rather than smoothing over

A behavioral-finance term for stating a disclaimer and then acting as though it doesn't apply to oneself is sometimes called "disclaimer effect" or, more broadly, a form of overconfidence bias — the well-documented tendency for people, including trained professionals, to rate their own judgment as more reliable than the same class of judgment coming from someone else. Fund managers, TV pundits, and retail forecasters show the identical pattern in published studies: a stated awareness that prediction is unreliable does not reduce the frequency of the predictions being made. Naming the mechanism does not settle whether any specific call of his was right or wrong — only that the caveat and the practice coexist without him treating that coexistence as a problem to fix.

16.7 His own disclosed mistakes, with numbers attached

The most valuable material in this chapter is not the general advice — it is what he says about his own trading history when a specific mistake comes with a quantified cost attached.

The largest of these is the wave-timing admission. He discloses that he used to try to time market waves: exiting a position after a wave up in anticipation of a wave down, and re-entering afterward. He says this approach worked roughly four times out of five in the short term, but that the fifth time cost him disproportionately more than the four wins combined — the market ran back up before he could re-enter, costing him multi-hundred-percent runs he would otherwise have held through. He states plainly that holding through instead, without trying to time the waves, would have made him far richer — he uses the specific word "billionaire" for what the habit may have cost him. This is not a hedged, general warning about market timing; it is a first-person account of a specific, named cost to a specific practice, and it is one of the more striking admissions anywhere in the archive.

A second, related disclosure concerns hedging. He states that for roughly twelve years earlier in his career, he used to buy put options or short the market to hedge his long-term portfolio whenever he anticipated a pullback — and that his win rate on that hedge was only about 30 percent. Twelve years is a long time to run a hedge with a losing win rate before the practice shows up as a disclosed regret rather than a stated rule; the book flags this because it is exactly the kind of long-running, quantified reversal that Appendix E of this project's evolution timeline exists to surface.

A third disclosed mistake concerns selling winners too early. He admits he used to jump in and out of Apple, Meta, and Microsoft shares repeatedly over the years — selling for a profit and buying back later — and now considers that pattern a mistake that cost him significant compounding. He separately notes that many investors, himself included in spirit if not by name in this particular card, panic-sold Meta near its bottom of roughly $80 in 2022, and that the stock was one of the most hated on the market at the time, trading at what he estimates was 8 to 12 times earnings excluding cash.

Editor's note — reading "cost me being a billionaire" as a claim, not a fact

All three of these figures — the billionaire framing, the 30 percent hedge win rate, the "significant compounding" lost on Apple/Meta/Microsoft — are his own self-reported estimates, made years after the fact, without a disclosed methodology for how the counterfactual (what he would have made had he held) was calculated. Chapter 1 flags self-reported returns as unverifiable in principle; the same caution applies here. The value of these admissions to a reader is not their precision — it's that they are honest, named, first-person reversals of practices he taught at the time, which is rarer and more useful than a polished record with no disclosed mistakes at all.

What he doesn't say about this

He does not explain how he arrived at the estimate that his wave-timing habit may have cost him "being a billionaire" — no disclosed portfolio size, no counterfactual calculation is shown in any card in the ledger. He does not reconcile the "listen to no one, including me" principle with his own repeated practice of giving predictions in the same videos where he states it; no card in the archive shows him addressing the contradiction directly. He does not disclose what, specifically, made him stop the twelve-year hedging practice when he did, only that the win rate on it was poor. He does not quantify how much money the in-and-out trading on Apple, Meta, and Microsoft actually cost him, using the word "significant" without a number. And while he catalogues failure-mode behaviors extensively, no card in this set shows him addressing how a trader is supposed to tell, in the moment, whether they are deviating from their system out of undisciplined fear or because a genuine change in market conditions calls for a genuine change in plan — the catalogue describes what deviation looks like after the fact, not how to distinguish it from legitimate adaptation in real time.

Chapter summary

  • He frames trading psychology as the discipline to follow a system with zero deviation, using the formula event + response = outcome, and argues the response, not the event, is what a trader actually controls.
  • He catalogues specific failure-mode behaviors after a losing streak — abandoning stops, revenge trading, overtrading, or the opposite failure of freezing entirely — and separately documents the mirror failure of raising risk after a winning streak.
  • He reuses a small set of recurring metaphors rather than new advice each time: never chase the girl (don't buy a rally), a great business falling is a bouncing ball or tennis ball rather than a falling knife, the bucking horse that throws riders off before it can run, and breathing out before breathing in.
  • He treats confidence as something that should attach to a system's edge over a hundred trades, never to any single trade, and states that an equity curve for a working system still rises and falls like a price chart.
  • He repeatedly states that no one, including him, can predict the market, and repeatedly gives specific market predictions in the same or nearby breath across years of videos — a documented, unresolved tension this book states rather than resolves.
  • He discloses that his old habit of timing market waves may have cost him "being a billionaire," that a twelve-year hedging practice ran at only a 30 percent win rate before he dropped it, and that trading in and out of Apple, Meta, and Microsoft cost him significant compounding — three quantified, first-person reversals of his own past practice.
  • He treats patience as accepting "no trade today" as a routine, legitimate outcome, and frames impatience as the direct cause of bending entry rules.

Try this

He suggests a concrete diagnostic for over-risking: if you wake up and rush to check your account balance, or find yourself checking it constantly through the day, that is itself the signal that you are trading with too much size or too much capital relative to what you can handle emotionally — the fix is to reduce size until five or ten losing trades in a row would not affect you emotionally.

Chapter 17

The Weekly Loop

In this chapter: the daily and weekly mechanics that turn everything in the previous sixteen chapters into an actual practice — how he screens, how he builds and prunes a watchlist, what he says his journal has to record, and why nearly every number he gives for "time spent" assumes tools that took years to build, several of which he now sells.

17.1 The headline claim: "30 minutes a day"

Across a decade of videos he returns to a specific number often enough that it functions as a recruiting pitch: less than 30 minutes a day. He tells one audience that he does not look at the market more than 30 minutes a day, tells another that trading needs less than one to two hours a day and that, once the skill is mastered as his is, 30 minutes a day is all it takes, and in a course-pitch video says his investing approach takes less than 30 minutes a day. A related but distinct claim appears in a video showing his own screeners at work: no more than 20 minutes a day to find candidates, because customized proprietary screeners do the searching automatically and produce what he calls the daily watchlist.

The number is real as a stated claim, but it comes bundled with conditions he states in the same breath and that are easy to drop when the figure gets repeated on its own. He says the 30-minute figure is what trading costs him now, after mastering the skill, and that it is only possible because screening software automates the search that would otherwise take far longer. He gives a graduated version of the same idea elsewhere: a long-term investor does not have to look at the market at all, a medium-term investor checks roughly every three months around earnings, a short-term trader checks daily, and he personally does not look at the market more than 30 minutes a day within that scheme. He tells a different audience that someone with a demanding job who can only spare 30 minutes a day should trade a swing system on daily candles rather than anything requiring intraday attention — the 30-minute budget is presented as a constraint that selects the style of trading, not a universal feature of trading itself.

His own on-camera routine, filmed rather than summarized, runs longer than the headline number on most days. He describes a typical day as roughly two hours split between an Asian morning session of about an hour and a US evening block of an hour to an hour and a half, shrinking toward half an hour only when nothing is worth acting on. A separate video breaks his day down further: two to three hours on currency trading, about 30 minutes on the stock account (which he says gets the least time of the three), and about an hour on the options account. When he actually times a session on camera and finds nothing to buy or trade, it runs about 40 minutes, with a note that acting on something would have added another 20 to 30 minutes on top. Students in one video self-report spending "30 minutes to two hours a day" — a figure the card flags as self-reported and unverified, not audited.

Editor's note — reading "30 minutes a day" as a marketing number

None of this makes the 30-minute figure false; he is consistent that it describes his own post-mastery, tool-assisted routine, and he says so explicitly. But the figure appears heavily in videos built around course or subscription pitches, where a low time cost is doing real persuasive work — it makes the whole system sound more accessible than the infrastructure behind it actually is. A reader building their own process from scratch, without years of screening-script development or a research team, should expect their own version of this loop to take substantially longer than 30 minutes until their own tools and pattern-recognition catch up. He does not say this directly in any card in the ledger; it follows from his own repeated qualifier that the short number applies "after mastery."

17.2 The screening step: what actually runs before he looks at a chart

The mechanism behind the short time figure is almost always the same: he does not scan the market by eye. He says he writes his own formulas — one he names "BMR short," for finding overbought stocks about to reverse down — and codes these as scanning scripts that a stock screener runs automatically every day within minutes. He describes the underlying workflow plainly: candidates come from screening software where his criteria are pre-programmed, so daily research is pressing a button rather than searching manually.

The platforms he names for this differ by purpose. For day trading he uses Thinkorswim's built-in scanner — going to Scan, then Stock Hacker, clearing the template and adding a custom study filter — and notes it requires a funded live account, not a demo. He is candid that a scanner only surfaces general setups and that every hit still has to be checked by hand against the full rule set he was taught, rather than trusted outright. For quick fundamental screens he uses Finviz, saying most of the data he wants sits on one page, and runs long-term quality screens there using thresholds like EPS growth above 10% over five years, sales growth above 10%, projected earnings growth above 10%, and return on equity above 15%. He also names TC2000 as a paid scanner he prefers over Interactive Brokers' built-in one, which he says "is not great", and describes a specific pre-market day-trading routine: scanners run about 25 minutes before the open, filtering for stocks gapping up more than 5% on volume with news behind the move, then cross- checked against a pre-market news page and ranked by volume.

For options, the pattern repeats: a scanner filtered to a 52-week implied- volatility rank above 30, further filtered by minimum share volume, produces a list he still reviews chart-by-chart for trend, support, or an oversold condition before acting. For valuation inputs on individual names, he says he pulls growth-rate projections from several sites at once — Finviz, Capital IQ, GuruFocus, Zacks, or Simply Wall Street depending on the video — and averages them, then deliberately uses a figure below the average as a conservative input, rather than trusting any single source.

Editor's note — what "screening" means across three different jobs

The word covers three distinct tasks in his material: a technical scanner that finds price patterns (Thinkorswim's Stock Hacker, TC2000), a fundamental screener that filters by financial ratios (Finviz), and an estimate aggregator that pulls analyst growth projections from multiple data providers to feed a valuation model (Capital IQ, GuruFocus, Zacks). These are not interchangeable, and a reader trying to replicate the routine needs a tool for each job, not one all-purpose scanner.

17.3 The daily routine, in his own order

One video, filmed rather than narrated after the fact, walks through an actual session start to finish and gives the clearest available picture of sequence. Before the US open he checks index futures — S&P, Dow, Nasdaq — to get a read on how the session is likely to open. The first action once the market opens is not looking for new trades: it is reviewing existing positions for anything to cut or any profit to take. He flags a practical trap here — displayed unrealized profit and loss on options can be "wonky" in the first hour of trading, and he shows the identical spread displaying a loss in one account and a roughly $1,000 profit in another account at the same moment, as evidence the early numbers can be a mispricing rather than a real reading.

Only after existing positions are handled does he run a fresh scan and save the results as a dated watchlist to work through; a hard filter at this stage is that the underlying must have liquid options, and he rejects one setup he calls "perfect price action" purely because it sits on a foreign ETF with illiquid options. When his own scan turns up nothing, the next step is checking student chat groups and a private group of professional traders at his company for posted ideas — described as a two-way exchange, not a one-directional feed of tips. After the trading accounts are handled, he moves to long-term investments, reviewing them on the same two criteria used at entry — undervalued price and proximity to a support level — before deciding whether to add. The final step of the routine is running through the portfolio watchlist itself for names that are both undervalued and near support, passing on anything over-extended until it retraces toward its moving averages. On the day this was filmed, nothing met the bar, and the session ended after about 40 minutes.

A separate video shows the swing-trading version of the same shape, with research done before the open, orders placed for the day, and execution left to the broker rather than watched live: he checks the market for 30 minutes to an hour before the open, places orders, and lets them fill automatically while he is elsewhere. A student's account of following the method describes the same order of operations at smaller scale: check the market roughly an hour after open to avoid FOMO entries, review existing positions for whether to roll, close, or hold, then scan for new setups — about 30 minutes total on a normal day, longer in a bearish market where more setups appear.

17.4 Building and maintaining the watchlist

The watchlist is not one list. He keeps several, split by purpose — a trading list, an investing list, a sector list — rather than combining everything into one, and in his charting platform links each watchlist to a chart window by color so clicking a ticker loads its chart immediately.

Construction differs by account. For day trading, he builds the list about 30 minutes before the US open from stocks gapping up more than 5% on high volume with a news catalyst — earnings or a similarly concrete event, not just price movement on its own. For swing trading a momentum strategy, he filters to a shorter list of roughly 10 to 12 names a month that pass his relative-strength criteria, then waits for a specific setup to trigger within that pre-filtered group rather than scanning the whole market fresh each day. For investing, the watchlist functions as a holding pen for good businesses at the wrong price: a stock that passes his quality screen but sits above his estimate of intrinsic value goes onto the watchlist rather than the portfolio, and only moves in once price falls toward the valuation.

Maintenance is where the watchlist earns its place as a process rather than a one-time list. He says he reviews his investing watchlist monthly and removes names that have run far above intrinsic value, adding them back only once price returns near that valuation. He describes the broader discipline the same way in a market sell-off: prepare a watchlist of good companies in advance and wait for a bullish signal before buying, rather than buying into the falling knife immediately. A once-a-year version of the same habit shows up in his annual market outlook videos, where he opens by reviewing the prior year's own forecast and scoring what he got right and wrong before making a new one — a rare instance of him auditing his own predictions on camera, which sits in tension with the prediction-avoidance material covered in Chapter 16.

At the top of the stack sits a monthly research cycle he describes running with a team, not alone: many hours at month's end going through the market for the highest relative-strength, undervalued names, covering both a secular-growth pick and a defensive pick each cycle. He describes a portfolio, more broadly, as something that has to be "monitored and reviewed daily, weekly and monthly", with the monthly layer deciding stock by stock which positions to add to, which to sell, and which need protecting ahead of a possible downtrend.

17.5 The journal: what it has to record and why

He is specific, more than once, about what a trading journal needs to contain and why an opinion about your own trading is worthless without it. He says the only way to know your own statistics is to keep a journal, and that trading has to be treated like a business with its own records. The minimum fields he names, repeated near-identically across two videos, are the entry price, the exit price, the trading signal or strategy followed, the risk taken per trade, and the resulting average win and average loss. A separate demonstration of his own journal shows additional fields in practice: stop-loss price, target price, and the actual exit price recorded per row, plus a color code — closed trades in green, open ones in black — updated immediately after each trade closes, not batched up later.

The unit he insists on is R, not dollars: a loss is recorded as one R (one unit of risk), and a win as some multiple of R, specifically so the record stays comparable across different position sizes and doesn't provoke the dollar-anchored emotional reaction covered in Chapter 16. He shows his own forex journal totaling wins and losses this way — a September at 10.5 net R across 17 trades, with wins recorded at 1.5R and losses at 1R — and a separate month at roughly 4R, 3.7R, and 7R in consecutive months, with one month at minus 2R, which he offers as evidence that losing months happen even inside a working system.

Sample size gets its own explicit rule: he says the journal needs at least 50 to 100 trades before it says anything reliable about a trader's actual statistics, and a related card sets the bar for judging a system's edge at 100 to 200–300 trades before drawing conclusions. Anyone who has never journaled, he says, should start immediately — and if they are currently losing money, should start with paper trading so the first 50 to 100 trades carry no real capital at risk. He applies the same discipline to his own students: live students send monthly trade journals, which he compiles into one sheet and reviews for win rate, wins, losses, and total R across the group.

The definition of a "good trade" he uses to grade every entry in the journal is deliberately decoupled from the outcome: a good trade is one where the system's rules were followed, independent of whether it made money, and a rule-breaking trade that happened to profit still counts as a bad trade. He extends this to a rule about regret: if you exit correctly by your rules and the trade then runs further in your favor without you, no regret is warranted; regret is only warranted when the rules themselves were broken.

Editor's note — R-multiple journaling as a known professional standard

Recording trades in R-multiples rather than dollar P&L is a documented practice in professional trading education more broadly (it traces most directly to Van Tharp's writing on position sizing and trade expectancy), not something unique to this channel. The reason it works mechanically is that it separates the quality of a decision from the size of the bet: a $50 loss on a $500 account and a $50 loss on a $5,000 account are the same 1R loss if risk was sized consistently, and grading trades in R keeps a trader's self-assessment from being distorted by position size drifting over time.

17.6 The monthly public review: a rare, checkable record

A handful of videos in the ledger are literal monthly trade reviews, and they are worth naming separately because they are one of the few places in the whole archive where a stated result can be checked against a stated methodology in the same video, rather than asserted after the fact.

In one, September 2017 closes at 18R over 10 trades — nine wins, one loss, a 90% win rate — for an expectancy of 1.83R per trade, and he converts this to a portfolio return by noting he risks 2% of capital per trade, making an 18R month roughly a 36% return. The very next month, October 2017, closes at minus 2.29R over 15 trades: four wins, seven losses, three breakevens, a 36% win rate against the prior month's 90%. He does not treat the reversal as a signal to change anything — he says he did nothing different in the losing month, ran the identical strategy and the same quality of high-probability trades, and explicitly rejects looking for an external cause like the Federal Reserve for a losing month that a small sample alone can explain. He uses a coin-flip illustration to make the statistical point concrete: ten or twenty flips can easily land five in a row without meaning the coin is biased, and it takes fifty to a hundred trials to see the true probability emerge. A month with an outsized 4.5R win and a separate month with a 3R loss are, on his account, good and bad luck that cancel out over a long enough run and are not evidence that the underlying system changed.

Editor's note — one paired example is not the same as a track record

This pair of consecutive months is genuinely useful because it is the rare case in the archive where a specific claim (90% win rate) sits next to the very next month's result (36% win rate) from the same disclosed strategy, letting a reader see variance in his own numbers rather than take a single cherry-picked month on faith. It is still only two months from one video series in one strategy; Chapter 1 flags all self-reported returns as unverifiable in principle, and that caution applies here too — there is no external audit of either month's fills, sizing, or account balance.

17.7 Tools and platforms, and where promotion enters the picture

The tools named across the ledger split into three groups: brokerages, data and screening subscriptions, and his own paid products.

For execution he names Thinkorswim from TD Ameritrade, used for both charting and moving-average overlays, and Interactive Brokers as an alternative broker, noting he has placed the identical spread on both and that either works for execution. He is candid that he prefers TradingView or Thinkorswim for charting even when trading through Interactive Brokers, because IB's own charting is weak, and later notes that Thinkorswim access narrowed for many people in Singapore unless they qualify as accredited investors, which limits which of his named tools a given reader can actually use. For data and screening he names Finviz repeatedly as a free, single-page source for fundamental screens, TradingView and ChartNexus for charts on non-US listings, and a stack of paid data providers — Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ, GuruFocus, Zacks, Simply Wall Street — that he says he cross-references for growth estimates rather than trusting any single source. He discloses a specific figure for the scale of this: roughly $50,000 a year in subscription fees for professional screening and research tools such as Bloomberg and Capital IQ.

The third category is his own paid infrastructure, and it needs a direct flag. A currency-strength meter his company built is given free to course students. A "slingshot screener" and other custom scanning scripts are described as proprietary and are given to students enrolled in his stock trading course. Most recently, a valuation and moat-scoring tool he calls "Stock Oracle" appears across a string of 2025– 2026 videos: he cites its moat scores and intrinsic-value output as the stated basis for buy, hold, and wait calls made live on camera, while the tool itself is described in the same videos as not yet public, limited to paying students, and approaching a general release. By the time of its public launch he reports roughly 5,000 existing users after a few months of student-only access, and continues previewing paid features — a screener, a heat map, additional listed markets — in a promotional context in videos through mid-2026.

Editor's note — the conflict of interest, stated plainly
