Several of the videos that cite Stock Oracle's output as evidence for a specific valuation call are, simultaneously, promotional material for that same paid tool — the analysis and the advertisement share a single video, with the tool's own numbers used as the proof that the tool is worth buying. That does not make the specific valuation calls wrong, and the underlying screening/valuation habits (cross-checking multiple data providers, using a conservative growth estimate, screening for quality before valuation) are documented independently across years of videos that predate the product. But a reader should weight any claim that leans on Stock Oracle's specific output — a moat score, an "Oracle value" figure — as coming from a source he has a direct financial stake in, not as an independent third-party tool the way Finviz or Bloomberg function in his other material.

What he doesn't say about this

He never discloses how long it took him personally to build the screening infrastructure that makes the 30-minute figure possible, nor what that infrastructure cost before it produced any return — the $50,000-a-year data subscription figure is given as a current, steady-state cost, not a startup cost. No card in the ledger shows him giving a beginner a realistic time estimate for the first year of building a personal watchlist and screening routine from scratch, without his scripts or his research team; the 30-minute claims are consistently framed as post-mastery. He does not reconcile the "review your own forecast annually" habit he demonstrates in the outlook videos with the "no one can predict the market, including me" principle documented in Chapter 16 — the annual review checks the forecast's accuracy but never uses a bad grade to stop making new forecasts. He does not disclose a formal cadence or checklist for removing a name from the trading watchlists the way he does for the investing watchlist's monthly prune; the trading-side watchlists in the ledger are described as being rebuilt fresh each session rather than maintained and pruned over time. And no card shows him addressing what a reader should do when the paid tools and subscriptions he uses — Bloomberg, Capital IQ, Stock Oracle — are out of reach financially; the free alternative he names most consistently, Finviz, appears in far fewer of the detailed workflow descriptions than the paid stack does.

Chapter summary

  • His stated time commitment — "30 minutes a day" — is real but conditional: it describes his own post-mastery routine, enabled by screening software he spent years building, and scales up sharply on his own filmed sessions and in his students' self-reports.
  • Screening is not one tool doing one job: a technical scanner (Thinkorswim Stock Hacker, TC2000) finds price patterns, a fundamental screener (Finviz) filters by financial ratios, and a data-aggregation step (Capital IQ, GuruFocus, Zacks) averages growth estimates for valuation — every scanner hit still gets checked by hand.
  • His filmed daily routine runs in a fixed order: review and manage existing positions first, then scan for new trades, then check community trade ideas if the scan is empty, then review long-term holdings, then review the portfolio watchlist last.
  • He keeps separate watchlists by purpose (trading, investing, sector) rather than one combined list, prunes the investing watchlist monthly for names that ran too far above intrinsic value, and rebuilds trading watchlists fresh each session against specific triggers like a gap-up with a news catalyst.
  • His journal standard requires entry price, exit price, signal followed, risk per trade, and win/loss recorded in R-multiples rather than dollars, updated immediately after each trade closes, with at least 50 to 100 trades before drawing conclusions from it.
  • A "good trade" in his journal is graded by rule-adherence, not by profit — a rule-breaking trade that wins still counts as a bad trade, and a rule-following trade that loses still counts as a good one.
  • His one publicly disclosed back-to-back monthly comparison — 90% win rate one month, 36% the next, on the identical strategy — is offered as his own evidence that short samples swing widely and shouldn't trigger a strategy change on their own.
  • Several tools he names for screening and valuation, most recently "Stock Oracle," are his own paid products cited as evidence for live buy/hold calls in the same videos that promote them — a conflict of interest worth weighing separately from the underlying screening habits themselves.

Try this

Build the four-layer loop he describes across his own routine videos, at whatever cadence fits your available time. Daily or per-session: review existing positions before looking for anything new, in that order. Per new candidate: run it through a fundamental quality screen before checking the chart, not the other way around. Monthly: go through your watchlist and remove anything that has run too far above your valuation, and revisit anything you cut once it comes back near that level. And continuously: log every trade in your journal immediately after it closes — entry, exit, signal, risk, and result in R-multiples — and withhold judgment on your own results until you have at least 50 to 100 trades logged.

Chapter 18

Beginners, Warnings, and Failure Modes

In this chapter: what Khoo tells someone starting from zero — how much capital that actually takes, how to pick a broker and hold down costs, how he says to spot the scams and impersonators that trade on his name — and, closing Part IV, a consolidated look at the mistakes he has admitted to on camera and what he says he would do differently.

18.1 Where he tells beginners to start

Across a decade of courses and webinars, Khoo is consistent that the entry point is small and the first months are meant to cost nothing. He tells beginners to start on a demo account with virtual money and move to real money only once they have been consistently profitable for a couple of months, comparing it to a flight simulator. To make paper trading feel consequential rather than a game, he has had students do fifty push-ups for every loss and run five miles if a month ends negative, on the theory that the exercise has to hurt or it will not train real discipline.

Once real money is in, the position-sizing instruction for a beginner is specific: risk one percent of capital per trade, not two or three, and only raise it with experience and confidence. He states this as a suggestion he applies to himself too, not a rule imposed only on students, and he is explicit that a student who risked closer to ten percent per trade — even one who tripled an account doing it — was doing something he does not recommend for a beginner.

He frames the skill gap directly: going into any market without training is, he says, like driving without a licence — legal in principle, but a matter of time before it hurts you financially. He is equally direct that skill takes time to build regardless of the instrument: forex trading, he says, is a profession like being a doctor or a lawyer, and even though it takes relatively less time to master than those professions, it still takes months of deliberate practice, not one or three days. He tells students who chase a new mentor, course, or book every time a strategy has a losing stretch that this loop — lose, blame the system, switch, lose again — can run for years without ever producing a competent trader.

He also gives beginners a filter for choosing instrument and timeframe rather than a single recommended one: there is no best strategy and no best instrument, he says, only ones that suit a person's temperament and available time — someone who can check the market only occasionally should invest, someone with under an hour a day can swing trade, and two to three hours a day is what day trading or scalping requires. He adds a personality-fit warning on top of the time-budget one: people who get very anxious watching a position move against them are, in his view, better suited to investing than trading, because trading requires a kind of emotional detachment that not everyone has or wants to build.

Editor's note — why "start small, build up" is a common structure across trading education

The specific sequence Khoo describes — demo account, then a small live account, then scaling with proven results — is not unique to him; it mirrors the standard progression taught across most retail trading education, and for a mechanical reason: a losing streak on a small account is a cheap lesson, the same losing streak on a large one is a large loss. What makes his version worth noting rather than generic is that he pairs it with a specific number (one percent risk per trade) and a specific gate (consistent profitability on paper, not just a good feeling) before capital gets added — the two things a beginner reading only motivational language about "starting small" would otherwise have to guess at themselves.

18.2 How much capital he actually says you need

Khoo gives a wide range of capital figures across the corpus, and the range tracks what the money is being asked to do rather than one fixed answer. For opening a stock trading account he has said as little as $3,000 is workable, with $5,000 mentioned in the same breath, while adding that he personally feels $10,000 is a better starting point if the goal is to generate income that actually supports living expenses. For forex specifically he quotes an even lower floor — a mini account with as little as $200 — while noting most people who take it seriously start with a few thousand. For options trading with a small account, he has told a live audience that $2,000 to $3,000 is "a relatively okay size" and that some individual strategies need only $50 to $100.

He is explicit that the $100,000-plus figures that show up in his own financial-freedom arithmetic (see Chapter 1) are a target, not a starting requirement: "you do not need $100,000 to start — you can start with $3,000," because the arithmetic is a matter of rate of return compounding upward from a smaller base, even though he concedes 5 percent on $3,000 is only about $150 a month at the start. In a 2022 breakdown he frames the capital question as depending on two separate things: a person's monthly expenses, and their skill level — the return they can actually generate on capital — rather than one number that applies to everyone. Working an illustrative Singapore-based example, he computes that a trader would need $4.8 million sitting in the bank at roughly 1 percent interest to generate $60,000 a year passively, against a much smaller sum needed if the money is compounding through active trading at his claimed 5 percent monthly return — the contrast he uses to make trading capital "look small" against the bank alternative.

Some thresholds he states as hard rather than a matter of preference. US pattern-day-trader rules, he says, normally require about $25,000 in the account before you can day trade stocks, though he notes non-US citizens can sidestep the rule by trading CFDs instead. Index options carry their own bigger floor in his account: he puts a proper index-options account at $50,000 to $100,000, and steers smaller accounts toward index-ETF options instead, which can be traded for a few hundred dollars per contract. On the investing side he repeats a capital-source rule regardless of account size: invest only money you will not need for the next few years — "a couple of years" in some videos, "three to five years" in others — so a downturn does not force a sale at the worst time. He tells students far from $100,000 to start with $2,000 to $5,000 and build over years rather than treat the full sum as a precondition for starting at all.

Editor's note — reading a spread of capital figures across nine years

These figures were recorded between 2017 and 2022, in webinars for different instruments (stocks, forex, options) and different account purposes (opening a live account versus generating replacement income). Reporting them as one number would erase real information: the honest read is that his stated minimum to begin has stayed low and consistent (a few hundred to a few thousand dollars, depending on instrument), while the figure that changes from video to video is what he considers enough to eventually live on — which depends on the return assumption he is making in that particular talk, a figure the book has already flagged in Chapter 1 as self-reported and unverifiable.

18.3 Choosing a broker and controlling costs

Khoo's broker guidance is built almost entirely around one idea: transaction costs compound against a trader the same way returns compound for them, so the broker relationship is itself a risk-management decision, not an administrative afterthought. For swing and short-term stock trading he says a broker charging very low commissions is a requirement, not a preference — he names his own broker's one-to-three-dollar-per-transaction fee as the level that keeps frequent in-and-out trading from being eaten alive by costs. He states the same principle for funds versus low-cost brokers directly: a mutual fund investor paying a 5 percent sales charge plus a 1 percent annual management fee has already lost 6 percent before the position even starts working, against ETFs that run at 0.3 to 0.5 percent and his own broker commissions of two or three dollars.

On forex specifically, he divides brokers into two structural types: market maker (or STP) brokers, who profit by marking up the market's bid-ask spread, and ECN brokers, who pass through the market spread and charge a flat commission instead. He states a preference for ECN brokers and gives the arithmetic behind it: a one-pip markup each way costs about $20 per lot round trip on his numbers, which on a $200 profit is nearly ten percent given away, against roughly $7 for a fixed ECN commission. Where a trader's country only offers market-maker brokers, his fallback rule is to pick one with the smallest possible markup — a maximum of one to one-and-a-half pips, no more. He also dismisses broker conspiracy theories about being deliberately stopped out, arguing that profitable and unprofitable traders alike use the same brokers, and that premature stop-outs and unfilled targets are a function of the bid-ask spread mechanics, not misconduct.

Beyond cost, he sets a small number of structural requirements. Options and futures trading require broker approval — declaring your experience level and applying through account management before the broker will let you place the trade. Short selling requires a margin account with collateral on deposit, and the position only works if the broker can actually source shares to lend, though he notes most brokers can for major names. For international and Hong Kong-listed positions he repeatedly flags that not every broker supports every market — he names Interactive Brokers specifically as one that lets him hold Hong Kong shares of companies like Alibaba, and warns that a broker unable to reach that market could force an investor into cash instead of the shares during an event like a spinoff. A long-time student, recounted in the corpus rather than stated by Khoo himself, adds a related practice: never keep all your money with one broker, because brokers themselves can fail — the student's stated practice, not a rule Khoo states himself.

On taxes and jurisdiction-specific costs, his statements are consistently scoped to his own situation as a Singapore-based, non-US-citizen investor, and the book preserves that scoping rather than generalizing it. He says capital gains are tax-free from where he operates, that Singapore REIT dividends and capital gains are tax-free for him, and that he avoids buying US-listed dividend stocks specifically because non-US citizens face a 30 percent withholding tax on US dividends, even though US capital gains are not taxed for him. In a 2026 series of disclosures, he explains he moved 99 percent of the holdings in his joint account with his wife into a newly formed corporate account, because a joint personal account is exposed to a 40 percent US estate tax if he dies, while a company or trust account structurally cannot "die" and so avoids that liability — a move he frames as administrative estate planning, not a performance change to the portfolio it created a visible discontinuity in.

Editor's note — US estate tax exposure for non-resident investors holding US assets

The mechanism Khoo describes — a US estate tax applying to non-US-citizen, non-US-resident investors who hold US-situs assets like US-listed stocks, above a very low exemption threshold relative to the one US citizens get — is a real and often-overlooked feature of US tax law that catches many international investors by surprise. It is the kind of cost this book's other chapters do not surface because it has nothing to do with strategy or timing: it is a structural cost of how an account is titled, unrelated to what is bought or sold inside it. It is also exactly the sort of question that requires a licensed cross-border tax advisor rather than a YouTube video — Khoo's own disclosure describes what he personally did, not a recommendation for viewers in different jurisdictions.

18.4 Spotting scams — in his own words, not as testimonial

Khoo has made multiple videos specifically warning about impersonators trading on his name and identity, and separately about categories of investment scam he says target beginners in general. This section reports his own stated material as his statements, kept distinct from any implied endorsement of his programs.

His core, repeated rule is unconditional: never send money to anyone claiming they will trade or invest it on your behalf — not to him, and not to anyone else — because he says the overwhelming majority of such offers are scams, and even the rare legitimate one still means losing control of the money to someone else's decisions. He states plainly that he personally manages only his own and his family's money through a family office, does not trade for the public, and that any account or message offering to do so under his name is not him. He gives two general tests a viewer can apply to any unsolicited offer, not only ones using his name: if someone claims their return is "risk-free," it is a scam, because he says every investment and every trade carries some risk — the only thing he treats as close to risk-free is a bank deposit, and even that is qualified since banks can fail, government insurance notwithstanding. And if an offer promises money with no skill and no knowledge required from the person receiving it, that too is a scam by his stated test, because he holds there is no free lunch anywhere in trading or investing — consistent profit takes real work, skill, and knowledge, full stop.

He names binary options specifically and repeatedly as a category to avoid: he describes them as structured with a negative expectancy, comparable to gambling — possible to profit from in the short run, but a losing proposition over time — and separately states that binary options brokers are often outright fraudulent operations rather than merely unfavorable products. He cites a 2017 Singapore newspaper report of $2.4 million lost to binary option scams by forty people in that country alone, speculating losses elsewhere are likely far larger, though he offers no sourcing beyond the one cited article. He is similarly direct about comment-spam scams on his own channel — accounts, often self-described binary options traders, promising outsized returns or offering to manage viewer money in the comments section — calling them scams outright and saying he marks them as spam.

On get-rich-quick framing generally, his stated test is simple: if someone tells you that you can get rich overnight, run away, because it is a scam by definition — there is no such thing. He extends the same skepticism to advertised return figures: people advertising 100 or 200 percent monthly returns do sometimes achieve it, he says, but not repeatably — it reflects extreme risk-taking that eventually wipes the account out, and even his own observation of traders hitting a hundred percent or even a thousand percent in a single month attributes it largely to luck rather than a repeatable edge. He states that 80 percent of retail investors and traders lose money, and separately cites a figure he attributes to brokers — that 80 percent of day traders lose 80 percent of their capital within 80 days — though he names no source for either statistic.

On what he considers a legitimate check against being scammed by a legitimate- looking broker, his instruction is: never send money unless the broker is regulated, or you have done independent due diligence yourself. He states his own filter for which products he will personally touch is regulation — he says he only trades and invests in products regulated by a stock exchange or an options exchange, and lists equities, forex, and listed options as the categories that pass that test for him.

Editor's note — how to actually verify broker regulation, since he does not walk through the steps

Khoo's rule ("only send money to a regulated broker, or do your own due diligence") is the right instinct, but none of the cards in this archive show him walking through how to check. The general, jurisdiction-independent version of that check is: identify which national regulator the broker claims to be licensed by (for example the Monetary Authority of Singapore, the US SEC/FINRA, the UK FCA, or Australia's ASIC), then search that regulator's own public register — not a link the broker provides — for the firm's name and license number. A broker that cannot be found on the regulator's own site under the name it uses to solicit you is not verified by that regulator, regardless of what its own website or marketing claims.

18.5 What he says he'd do differently: a capstone across the corpus

Several of the most consequential admissions in the archive are self-disclosed mistakes, made on camera, years after the fact, and they cluster around the same underlying error even though they surface in different chapters of this book. Brought together here, they form the honest "what he'd do differently" material the outline promised — not invented, only assembled from cards already anchored elsewhere in the corpus.

The largest, in his own telling, is the cost of trying to time market waves. He describes exiting after a wave up expecting a wave down, a method that worked roughly four times out of five in the short run — until the fifth time the market ran back up before he could re-enter, costing him the kind of multi-hundred-percent runs that, he estimates, would have made him "a billionaire" had he simply held through instead of trading the waves. He connects this to a broader, quantified admission about his early investing years: he says that if he had simply held long-standing positions in companies like Alphabet, Amazon, and Microsoft rather than jumping in and out trying to sell before drops and buy back lower, he estimates he would be five to ten times richer today. He states plainly that he no longer does this — after years of investing he stopped trying to sell out of the market and buy back lower during choppy periods, because the market as often as not simply does not come back down to where he sold it.

The second recurring thread is selling great companies too early. His most detailed version of this is his own Facebook/Meta history: he bought early after the IPO drop, sold at $50 believing he had made a smart 100 percent gain, and then spent years buying back in at progressively higher prices — $100, $150, $200, $300 — as the stock kept climbing without him, a mistake he now credits with costing an estimated tenfold in forgone gains against simply holding from the original entry. He draws the explicit lesson from this himself: holding for the long run as an investor beats getting in and out for short-term profits as a speculator, and he says he derives that conclusion directly from his own Facebook mistake, not from a rule he had going in. He has also more recently disclosed a mechanically different version of the same failure mode — selling covered calls against high-growth holdings including Nvidia and Meta, which then got assigned away and forced a sale before further gains he would otherwise have kept, which he now calls a mistake in hindsight.

A third thread is the retreat from active hedging itself. Chapters 7 and 11 show him building and explaining index-put and put-spread hedges against his long-term investing book in detail across 2019 and 2020. By 2025 he discloses that he does this "much less often" than he used to, citing the accumulated cost of paying for insurance that expires worthless whenever the market simply does not fall — the same trade-off he once described to students as being like paying a fire-insurance premium you should not resent just because your house did not burn down. He gives no specific date for when his own practice changed, and the book treats this as a documented shift in what he actually does, set against what earlier chapters show him teaching as a standing rule.

The fourth, most recent disclosure is UnitedHealth. Chapter 7 already covers his general no-stop-loss investing rule; UnitedHealth is the sharpest test of where he breaks it on his own terms. After fraud allegations surfaced against the company, he sold his entire position for a slight profit — not because his valuation model had changed, but because he was not comfortable holding a business associated with what he describes as a culture of alleged systemic fraud, even while stating he believed the company was "too big to fail" and would likely recover. He reinvested the proceeds into Novo Nordisk, HCA Healthcare, Pool Corporation, and ASML. Within roughly a year he is shown accumulating UnitedHealth shares again, describing it as undervalued on a lawsuit-driven pullback and declining to predict when it would recover — a sequence the book reports rather than resolves, since he does not reconcile the ethical-discomfort exit with the later valuation-driven re-entry into the same stock.

Editor's note — why these four threads belong together

Each of these four episodes looks, on its own, like an isolated anecdote from a different chapter of the channel's history. Read together, they share one shape: a stated rule (target discipline, "never sell a great company," a hedging routine, "no stop-loss on investments once bought for the right reasons") followed by a real, dated, dollar-quantified instance of Khoo departing from his own rule, followed by his own retrospective account of the cost or the reasoning. That pattern — rule, deviation, self-graded consequence — is the most useful thing a ten-year channel with this much archived footage can offer a reader, and it is also the material easiest to lose if a book about "his method" only reports the rules and skips the videos where he breaks them.

What he doesn't say

Khoo does not, in any card in this archive, walk a beginner through the mechanics of verifying broker regulation step by step — the general procedure in the 18.4 editor's note is supplied by this book, not by him. He does not address what happens to a beginner's small account, sized at his stated $2,000 to $5,000 starting range, during the account's first real losing streak beyond the general one-percent-risk instruction; there is no card walking through a worked example of what an eight-trade losing streak looks like in dollars on a small account. He does not reconcile his own 2025 UnitedHealth exit — driven by ethical discomfort rather than valuation — with his 2026 re-entry into the same stock on valuation grounds, leaving the reader to notice the tension rather than having it resolved for them. He also never addresses, in any card, how a beginner outside Singapore should read his tax-cost material, since nearly every stated figure (capital gains treatment, REIT tax status, US estate-tax exposure) is scoped to his own residency and citizenship status without a general framework for translating it to a different jurisdiction. And while he repeatedly says "regulated broker, therefore safe," he does not address what a reader should do when the regulator itself is not well known or is in a jurisdiction with weak enforcement — his test assumes the regulator can be trusted, a step he never examines.

Chapter summary

  • He tells beginners to start on a demo account and move to real money only after a couple of months of consistent paper profitability, sized at one percent risk per trade to start.
  • His stated minimum capital to open a live account ranges from $200 (a forex mini account) to $3,000–$10,000 (stocks), with the higher end reflecting his own preference for income-replacement rather than a hard minimum.
  • Broker choice is framed as a cost-control decision: he pushes low commissions, ECN pricing over market-maker markups in forex, and names specific brokers (his own, and Interactive Brokers for international access) as examples rather than endorsements.
  • His tax and cost statements are consistently scoped to his own status as a Singapore-resident, non-US-citizen investor — capital-gains-free at home, 30 percent US dividend withholding abroad — and do not generalize to other jurisdictions.
  • His scam-warning rule is unconditional: never send money to anyone offering to trade it for you, verify regulation independently, and treat "risk-free" or "no skill required" claims as automatic red flags.
  • Binary options are named repeatedly as a category to avoid outright, both for negative-expectancy structure and for outright broker fraud.
  • Four self-disclosed episodes — wave-timing regret, selling Meta far too early, retreating from active hedging, and the UnitedHealth exit-then- re-entry — form a documented "rule, deviation, consequence" pattern that runs across nearly a decade of the channel.
  • None of his return, capital, or self-corrected-mistake figures in this chapter are independently verifiable; they are reported, as throughout this book, as his own stated account.

Try this

Based on his own stated sequence for a true beginner: open a demo or paper trading account before funding a live one, and hold yourself to the same rule he sets — do not move to real money until you have produced a couple of consecutive months of consistent (not necessarily large) paper profit. When you do fund the live account, size your first trades at one percent of account capital at risk, not more, and write that number down before you place the trade rather than deciding it trade by trade. Separately, before sending money to any broker or program, run his stated regulation check yourself: find the specific regulator the firm claims to be licensed by, and confirm the firm's name and license number on that regulator's own public register rather than a link the firm supplies.
